Toric Varieties — Fans, Polytopes & Torus Actions

Toric varieties are normal algebraic varieties that contain an algebraic torus (ℂ^*)ⁿ as a dense open subset, with the torus action extending to the whole variety. They are the meeting point of algebraic geometry and combinatorics: every toric variety is described by a fan — a collection of strongly convex rational polyhedral cones in a real vector space N_ℝ ≅ ℝⁿ. The combinatorial data completely determines the geometry: the orbit-cone correspondence bijects cones of the fan with torus orbits, the Cox ring provides a quotient construction, and properties like smoothness, completeness, and projectivity are encoded in simple conditions on the fan. Toric varieties provide the simplest testing ground for deep ideas in algebraic geometry — resolutions of singularities, intersection theory, and mirror symmetry. In particular, Batyrev's construction uses reflexive polytopes to produce mirror pairs of Calabi-Yau hypersurfaces in toric varieties, linking toric geometry to string theory.

"Toric varieties are the algebraic geometer's simplest testing ground."
— William Fulton
Part 1

📏 The Algebraic Torus and Its Characters

Torus
The Algebraic Torus, Character Lattice, and One-Parameter Subgroups
The algebraic torus of dimension n is T ≅ (ℂ^*)ⁿ — a product of n copies of the multiplicative group of the complex numbers. It is a commutative affine algebraic group. Two lattices encode its structure: the character lattice M = Hom(T, ℂ^*) ≅ ℤⁿ consists of algebraic group homomorphisms χᵘ: T → ℂ^*, where for u = (u₁,…,uₙ) ∈ ℤⁿ, we have χᵘ(t₁,…,tₙ) = t₁^u₁ ⋯ tₙ^uₙ. The cocharacter lattice N = Hom(ℂ^*, T) ≅ ℤⁿ consists of one-parameter subgroups λᵛ: ℂ^* → T, where λᵛ(s) = (s^v₁,…,s^vₙ) for v = (v₁,…,vₙ) ∈ ℤⁿ. There is a natural perfect pairing ⟨,⟩ : M × N → ℤ given by ⟨u, v⟩ = Σ u_i v_i, which satisfies χᵘ(λᵛ(s)) = s^{⟨u,v⟩}. A toric variety is a normal algebraic variety X containing T as a Zariski open subset such that the multiplication action of T on itself extends to an algebraic action T × X → X. The simplest examples: projective space ℙⁿ (whose fan has cones generated by subsets of standard basis vectors), affine space 𝔸ⁿ (a single cone), and weighted projective spaces (fans with non-standard lattices).
The algebraic torus T ≅ (ℂ^*)ⁿ is the computational heart of toric geometry; its characters and cocharacters encode all combinatorial data through the lattices M and N.
Part 2

🌍 Fans and the Orbit-Cone Correspondence

Fans
Strongly Convex Rational Polyhedral Cones, Fans, and the Orbit-Cone Correspondence
Let N_ℝ = N ⊗_ℤ ℝ ≅ ℝⁿ. A strongly convex rational polyhedral cone σ ⊂ N_ℝ is a cone generated by finitely many lattice vectors that contains no line through the origin. Its dual cone σ^∨ = {x ∈ M_ℝ : ⟨x, y⟩ ≥ 0 for all y ∈ σ} is also rational polyhedral. The affine toric variety associated to σ is U_σ = Spec ℂ[σ^∨ ∩ M], where ℂ[σ^∨ ∩ M] is the monoid algebra of lattice points in the dual cone — its monomials correspond to characters of T. A fan Δ is a finite collection of such cones satisfying: (i) every face of a cone in Δ is in Δ, and (ii) the intersection of any two cones in Δ is a face of each. The toric variety X(Δ) is constructed by gluing the affine patches U_σ along their intersections U_{σ∩τ}. The orbit-cone correspondence is the central combinatorial-geometric dictionary: for each cone σ ∈ Δ of dimension k, there is a T-orbit O(σ) ≅ (ℂ^*)^{n−k} in X(Δ) whose closure is V(σ) ≅ X(Star(σ)). The fan determines all geometric properties: X(Δ) is complete (compact) iff the support |Δ| = ⋃_{σ∈Δ} σ equals all of N_ℝ; it is smooth iff every cone is generated by a subset of a ℤ-basis of N; it is projective iff Δ is the normal fan of a lattice polytope.
Every fan Δ produces a toric variety X(Δ), and the combinatorial structure of Δ — its cones, faces, and lattice points — perfectly encodes the geometry of X(Δ).
Part 3

🧮 Polytopes and Projective Toric Varieties

Polytopes
Lattice Polytopes, Normal Fans, and the Ehrhart Polynomial
A lattice polytope P ⊂ M_ℝ is the convex hull of finitely many points in the lattice M ≅ ℤⁿ. The normal fan Σ_P of P is the fan whose cones are the inner normal cones to the faces of P. The associated polarized toric variety (X_P, L_P) comes with an ample line bundle L_P whose space of global sections H⁰(X_P, L_P^⊗^k) is canonically identified with the lattice points in the dilated polytope kP ∩ M. The Hilbert polynomial counts these lattice points: dim H⁰(X_P, L_P^⊗^k) = |kP ∩ M| for sufficiently large k. The Ehrhart polynomial L(P, t) = |tP ∩ M| is a polynomial in t of degree n: L(P, t) = Vol(P)·tⁿ + (∂Vol(P)/2)·tⁿ⁻¹ + … + 1, where Vol(P) is the lattice volume. Divisors on toric varieties: the divisor class group Cl(X) ≅ ℤ^{r−n} where r = |Δ(1)| is the number of rays (1-dimensional cones). Each ray ρ corresponds to a T-invariant prime divisor D_ρ. The canonical divisor is K_X = −Σ_{ρ∈Δ(1)} D_ρ, giving K_X = −Σ D_ρ. The Cox ring (or total coordinate ring) is Cox(X) = ℂ[x_ρ : ρ ∈ Δ(1)], graded by the divisor class group Cl(X) via the degree map deg(x_ρ) = [D_ρ] ∈ Cl(X). The toric variety X is realized as a GIT quotient of Spec(Cox(X)) by Hom(Cl(X), ℂ^*).
Lattice polytopes give projective toric varieties; the Ehrhart polynomial counts lattice points, and the Cox ring provides a powerful homogeneous coordinate description.
Part 4

🔥 Mirror Symmetry and Calabi-Yau Hypersurfaces in Toric Varieties

Mirror
Batyrev's Construction, Reflexive Polytopes, and Hodge Number Combinatorics
A lattice polytope P ⊂ M_ℝ containing the origin in its interior is reflexive if its polar polytope P° = {y ∈ N_ℝ : ⟨x,y⟩ ≥ −1 for all x ∈ P} is also a lattice polytope (in the dual lattice N). Reflexive polytopes correspond to Gorenstein toric Fano varieties — the anticanonical divisor −K_X is ample and Cartier. Batyrev's mirror construction: given a reflexive polytope P, the toric variety X_P (from the normal fan of P°) and its mirror X_{P°} (from the normal fan of P) form a mirror pair. A Calabi-Yau hypersurface is defined by the zero set of a generic anticanonical section (a Laurent polynomial whose Newton polytope is P) inside X_P. The Hodge numbers of the Calabi-Yau hypersurface are computed combinatorially: h^{1,1} = l(P) − n − 1 − Σ_{faces} l^*(face) and h^{n-1,1} = l(P°) − n − 1 − Σ_{faces} l^*(face^°), where l(P) counts lattice points in P and l^*(face) counts interior lattice points. The quintic threefold — a degree 5 hypersurface in ℙ⁴ — is the classic Calabi-Yau 3-fold with h^{1,1}=1, h^{2,1}=101. The Batyrev-Borisov construction generalizes to nef-partitions and complete intersections in toric varieties, producing a vast landscape of mirror pairs that have been central to the development of mirror symmetry and string compactifications.
Reflexive polytopes produce mirror Calabi-Yau hypersurfaces in toric varieties; Hodge numbers are computed purely combinatorially, making mirror symmetry concrete and computable.

🎯 Practice

← Back to Math Map

Cox Ring, Homogeneous Coordinates, and GIT Quotients

The Cox ring (or total coordinate ring) of a toric variety X(Δ) is the polynomial ring ℂ[x_ρ : ρ ∈ Δ(1)] graded by the divisor class group Cl(X). Each variable x_ρ corresponds to the T‑invariant prime divisor D_ρ. The grading is given by deg(x_ρ) = [D_ρ] ∈ Cl(X). The irrelevant ideal B(Δ) is generated by monomials ∏ρ∉σ(1) x_ρ for each cone σ ∈ Δ. The toric variety is recovered as the GIT quotient (Spec Cox(X) V(B(Δ))) // Hom(Cl(X), ℂ^*), where the torus Hom(Cl(X), ℂ^*) acts with weights given by the grading. This construction generalizes the classical homogeneous coordinate ring of projective space and provides a uniform framework for studying line bundles, sections, and morphisms. For a smooth complete toric variety, the Cox ring is a polynomial ring, and the GIT quotient is a geometric quotient. In the singular or non‑projective case, one must choose a stability condition (a character of Hom(Cl(X), ℂ^*)) to obtain a well‑defined quotient; different choices correspond to different birational models related by toric flips. The Cox ring also encodes the Mori cone: the effective cone Eff(X) is the cone generated by the classes [D_ρ] in Cl(X)_ℝ, and the movable cone is the intersection of the nef cones of all small ℚ‑factorial modifications. This description makes the minimal model program for toric varieties completely combinatorial.

Toric Divisors, Intersection Theory, and the Polytope Formula

On a complete toric variety X(Δ) of dimension n, every T‑invariant Weil divisor is a ℤ‑linear combination D = Σ a_ρ D_ρ. The associated polytope P_D = {m ∈ M_ℝ : ⟨m, u_ρ⟩ ≥ −a_ρ for all ρ ∈ Δ(1)} is a lattice polytope whose normal fan refines Δ. The line bundle O(D) is globally generated iff P_D is a lattice polytope, and it is ample iff P_D is strictly convex with normal fan exactly Δ. The intersection number of n nef divisors D^(1), …, D^(n) equals n! times the mixed volume of the corresponding polytopes P1, …, P_n. In particular, the self‑intersection D^n = n! Vol(P_D). For a smooth projective toric variety, the Chow ring A^*(X) is generated by the divisor classes [D_ρ] subject to two types of relations: (i) linear relations Σ ⟨m, u_ρ⟩ [D_ρ] = 0 for all m ∈ M, and (ii) Stanley–Reisner relations ∏ρ∈S [D_ρ] = 0 whenever the rays in S do not span a cone in Δ. This presentation, due to Danilov and Jurkiewicz, allows explicit computation of intersection numbers by reducing monomials in the [D_ρ] to a linear combination of top‑degree classes corresponding to maximal cones. The Todd class of X is td(X) = ∏ρ ( [D_ρ] / (1 − e−[D_ρ]) ), and the Hirzebruch–Riemann–Roch theorem reduces to a sum over lattice points of the polytope, recovering the Ehrhart polynomial.

Worked Example: Intersection Numbers on the Hirzebruch Surface F1
Let F1 be the toric surface with fan rays u1 = (1,0), u2 = (0,1), u3 = (−1,0), u4 = (0,−1) and maximal cones σ1 = ⟨u1,u2⟩, σ2 = ⟨u2,u3⟩, σ3 = ⟨u3,u4⟩, σ4 = ⟨u4,u1⟩. Compute the intersection matrix of the T‑invariant divisors D1, D2, D3, D4.
The linear relations come from M = ℤ2. For m = (1,0): ⟨m,u1⟩=1, ⟨m,u2⟩=0, ⟨m,u3⟩=−1, ⟨m,u4⟩=0 ⇒ D1 − D3 = 0. For m = (0,1): D2 − D4 = 0. Thus D1 = D3, D2 = D4. Stanley–Reisner relations: D1 D3 = 0 (rays 1 and 3 not in a cone), D2 D4 = 0. Using D1 = D3, we get D12 = 0. Similarly D22 = 0. The only non‑zero top products are D1 D2 (cone σ1) and D2 D3 (cone σ2) etc. Since D1 D2 corresponds to the maximal cone σ1, its class is the fundamental class [pt]. The intersection matrix in basis (D1, D2) is [[0,1],[1,0]]. Hence D1·D2 = 1, D12 = D22 = 0. This matches the known geometry: D1 is the fiber class, D2 is the section with self‑intersection −1, but in the Chow ring of F1 the section class is D2 − D1, giving (D2−D1)2 = −1.
Exam Strategy: Intersection Computations
When asked for intersection numbers on a toric variety, first write the linear relations from the lattice M, then impose Stanley–Reisner relations. Reduce any monomial of degree n to a sum of classes of maximal cones. Each maximal cone contributes 1 to the fundamental class. This algorithm works for any complete simplicial toric variety and avoids memorising case‑by‑case formulas.

Toric Vector Bundles and Klyachko's Classification

Equivariant vector bundles on a toric variety X(Δ) are classified by Klyachko's filtrations. For each ray ρ ∈ Δ(1), a T‑equivariant vector bundle E of rank r corresponds to a collection of decreasing filtrations E^ρ(i) (i ∈ ℤ) of the fiber E0 ≅ ℂ^r over the fixed point 0 ∈ T, indexed by integers, such that E^ρ(i) = E0 for i ≪ 0 and E^ρ(i) = 0 for i ≫ 0. These filtrations must satisfy a compatibility condition: for any cone σ ∈ Δ, the intersection of the subspaces E^ρ(i_ρ) over ρ ∈ σ(1) has dimension equal to the rank of the bundle restricted to the affine patch U_σ. Equivalently, the associated graded pieces define a representation of the fan. The Chern classes of E can be read off from the filtrations: c1(E) = Σ_ρ ( Σ_i i · dim Gr^ρ_i ) D_ρ. Klyachko's theorem also gives a combinatorial criterion for global generation, ampleness, and stability of toric vector bundles. For example, the tangent bundle T_X corresponds to the filtrations where E^ρ(i) = ℂ^n for i ≤ 0, E^ρ(i) = u_ρ^⊥ for i = 1, and 0 for i ≥ 2. This description makes the computation of equivariant cohomology and K‑theory of toric vector bundles completely algorithmic.

Tropical Geometry, Toric Degenerations, and the Secondary Fan

Tropical geometry provides a piecewise‑linear shadow of algebraic geometry over the valuation field. Given a projective toric variety X_P defined by a lattice polytope P ⊂ M_ℝ, a regular subdivision of P corresponds to a toric degeneration of X_P to a union of toric varieties glued along toric strata. The secondary fan of P is the fan in the space of height functions on P ∩ M whose cones parametrize regular subdivisions. Each maximal cone of the secondary fan corresponds to a unimodular triangulation of P, which yields a flat degeneration of X_P to a toric variety with at worst orbifold singularities. The tropical variety of a subvariety Y ⊂ X_P is the set of valuations of points of Y; it is a polyhedral complex of dimension dim Y balanced at each codimension‑1 face. For a hypersurface defined by a Laurent polynomial f with Newton polytope P, the tropical hypersurface is the corner locus of the piecewise‑linear function trop(f). The combinatorial structure of the tropical hypersurface encodes the Hodge numbers of Y via the tropical Hodge theory of Itenberg–Mikhalkin. Moreover, the secondary fan governs the birational geometry of X_P: its walls correspond to flips and flops in the toric minimal model program. This connection makes toric varieties a testing ground for mirror symmetry, where the tropical limit of a Calabi‑Yau hypersurface recovers the SYZ fibration.

Equivariant Cohomology and the Danilov–Jurkiewicz Theorem

The T‑equivariant cohomology ring H_T^*(X(Δ); ℤ) of a smooth complete toric variety is a free module over H_T^*(pt) ≅ Sym(M) ≅ ℤ[t1,…,t_n] with basis given by the equivariant classes of the orbit closures V(σ). The Danilov–Jurkiewicz theorem states that the ordinary cohomology ring H^*(X(Δ); ℤ) is the quotient of the polynomial ring ℤ[x_ρ : ρ ∈ Δ(1)] by the ideal generated by (i) linear forms Σ ⟨m, u_ρ⟩ x_ρ for all m ∈ M, and (ii) monomials ∏ρ∈S x_ρ where S does not span a cone in Δ. The equivariant version replaces the linear forms by Σ ⟨m, u_ρ⟩ x_ρ + Σ ⟨m, u_ρ⟩ t_ρ, where t_ρ are the equivariant parameters. This presentation allows the computation of equivariant Chern classes of tangent bundles, the localization formula for integrals, and the Gromov–Witten invariants of toric varieties via the virtual localization of Graber–Pandharipande. In particular, the equivariant cohomology of the Hilbert scheme of points on a toric surface can be expressed in terms of the Nakajima quiver variety construction, linking toric geometry to representation theory of affine Lie algebras.

  • Confusing the Cox ring grading by Cl(X) with the grading by Pic(X); they coincide only when X is ℚ‑factorial.
  • Forgetting that the Stanley–Reisner ideal uses sets of rays that do NOT span a cone, not the cones themselves.
  • Assuming every toric vector bundle splits as a sum of line bundles; Klyachko's classification shows many indecomposable bundles exist.
  • Misidentifying the secondary fan with the normal fan of the polytope; the secondary fan lives in the space of height functions, not in M_ℝ.
  • Neglecting the equivariant parameters in the Danilov–Jurkiewicz presentation when applying localization formulas.
  • Treating the tropical hypersurface as a mere graph without checking the balancing condition at each vertex.
  • Overlooking that the GIT quotient construction of X(Δ) from the Cox ring requires a choice of stability condition when X is not projective.

These traps highlight the subtle points that often separate a passing score from a top rank.

Toric Mori Theory and the Cone of Curves

The Mori program for toric varieties is completely combinatorial. For a complete ℚ‑factorial toric variety X(Δ) the cone of effective curves NE(X) ⊂ N₁(X)ℝ is generated by the classes of torus‑invariant curves V(τ) where τ runs over the (n−1)‑dimensional cones of Δ. Each such curve corresponds to a wall ρ₁,ρ₂ of a maximal cone σ; its numerical class is the primitive relation Σ a_ρ V(ρ) = 0 with a_ρ > 0 for the two rays of the wall and a_ρ = 0 otherwise. The extremal rays of NE(X) are exactly those primitive relations for which the associated wall is a face of exactly two maximal cones. Contracting an extremal ray yields a toric morphism φ : X → Y which is either a divisorial contraction (the wall belongs to a unique maximal cone) or a flip (the wall belongs to two maximal cones and the fan is modified by stellar subdivision). The toric flip replaces the two maximal cones sharing the wall by the two cones obtained by inserting the sum of the two opposite ray generators. This operation preserves ℚ‑factoriality and terminates because the number of maximal cones strictly decreases. The toric minimal model program therefore reduces to a finite sequence of wall‑removals and wall‑insertions, each step dictated by the sign of the intersection pairing with an ample divisor. The cone of movable curves Mov(X) is the dual of the effective divisor cone Eff(X); its facets correspond to the walls of the secondary fan of the polytope. In particular, the nef cone Nef(X) is the intersection of the half‑spaces ⟨D, C⟩ ≥ 0 for all extremal curve classes C, and it coincides with the ample cone when X is projective. The toric Mori cone theorem also gives a precise description of the length of an extremal ray: for a primitive relation Σ a_ρ V(ρ) the length is min{ a_ρ | ρ in the wall } and equals 1 for smooth varieties, which explains why smooth toric flips are always Atiyah flops.

Toric Stacks and Root Stacks

When the fan Δ is not simplicial, the associated toric variety has finite quotient singularities that are naturally resolved by passing to a smooth Deligne–Mumford toric stack. The root stack construction of Cadman and Abramovich–Vistoli attaches a stacky structure along each torus‑invariant divisor D_ρ by taking the r_ρ‑th root of the line bundle O(D_ρ) for a chosen positive integer r_ρ. The resulting stack X(Δ, r) has coarse moduli space X(Δ) and its inertia stack is a disjoint union of twisted sectors indexed by the box elements of the cones: for each cone σ the box Box(σ) = { m ∈ M | 0 ≤ ⟨m, u_ρ⟩ < 1 for all ρ ∈ σ(1) } parametrizes the non‑trivial stabilizer groups. The Chen–Ruan orbifold cohomology of X(Δ, r) is then computed by the same Stanley–Reisner presentation as the ordinary cohomology, but with the grading shifted by the age (degree shifting number) of each box element. This shift is age(m) = Σ ⟨m, u_ρ⟩. The orbifold Chow ring and the quantum cohomology of toric stacks have been explicitly described by Coates–Corti–Iritani–Tseng using the mirror theorem for toric stacks: the I‑function is a hypergeometric series whose coefficients are indexed by the box elements, and the J‑function is obtained by the usual mirror map. Root stacks also provide a natural setting for logarithmic geometry: the divisor D = Σ D_ρ with the root data gives a fine saturated log structure, and the associated log smooth morphism to the standard log point encodes the toric degeneration data used in the Gross–Siebert program.

Worked Example: Computing the Orbifold Cohomology of a Weighted Projective Stack
Let X = ℙ(1,1,2) be the weighted projective stack with fan in N = ℤ² generated by u₁ = (1,0), u₂ = (0,1), u₃ = (−1,−2). Take root multiplicities r = (1,1,2) along the three invariant divisors. Determine the box elements for each maximal cone and compute the age grading of the twisted sectors.
The maximal cones are σ₁ = Cone(u₁,u₂) and σ₂ = Cone(u₂,u₃) and σ₃ = Cone(u₃,u₁). For σ₁ the box is {0} because the cone is smooth. For σ₂ we solve 0 ≤ ⟨m, u₂⟩ < 1 and 0 ≤ ⟨m, u₃⟩ < 1 with m = (a,b) ∈ ℚ². This gives 0 ≤ b < 1 and 0 ≤ −a−2b < 1 ⇒ −1 < a+2b ≤ 0. The only rational solution with denominator dividing 2 is m = (0,0) and m = (−1,½). The latter has age = ⟨m,u₂⟩ + ⟨m,u₃⟩ = ½ + ½ = 1. For σ₃ the box contains m = (½,0) with age = ½ + ½ = 1. Thus the inertia stack has three twisted sectors of age 1, contributing classes in degree 2 (since dim X = 2). The orbifold cohomology ring is ℤ[H₁,H₂]/(H₁², H₂², H₁H₂) with H₁, H₂ of degree 2, matching the known presentation of H*CR(ℙ(1,1,2)).
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the orbifold cohomology of a toric stack, first list the maximal cones, compute the box elements using the defining inequalities, then add the age shift. Remember that the age is the sum of the fractional parts of the pairings with the primitive generators. The Stanley–Reisner relations stay the same; only the grading changes.

Gross–Siebert Program and Logarithmic Geometry

The Gross–Siebert program constructs mirror families for Calabi–Yau varieties by degenerating them to unions of toric varieties glued along toric strata, a process governed by logarithmic geometry. A log Calabi–Yau pair (X, D) with D a simple normal crossing divisor defines a tropicalization map trop : Xan → Σ(X) where Σ(X) is the intersection complex, a polyhedral complex homeomorphic to the dual complex of D. The program starts with an integral affine manifold B with singularities (the base of an SYZ fibration) and a polyhedral decomposition P. From this data one builds a formal toric degeneration 𝔛 → Spec ℂ[[t]] whose central fiber is a union of toric varieties X_σ for σ ∈ P, glued along toric divisors according to the face relations. The smoothing of this central fiber is obstructed by a scattering diagram on B; the consistency of the scattering diagram is equivalent to the existence of a formal smoothing, and the resulting mirror family is obtained by taking the relative spectrum of the sheaf of functions on the universal cover of BSing(B) with monomials corrected by the scattering diagram. In the toric case, the polyhedral decomposition is the normal fan of a reflexive polytope, the scattering diagram is trivial, and the mirror is the Batyrev mirror. For non‑toric Calabi–Yau hypersurfaces in toric varieties, the scattering diagram encodes the instanton corrections coming from holomorphic disks with boundary on the SYZ fibers. The logarithmic Gromov–Witten invariants of (X, D) are then computed by tropical curve counts in B, and the mirror theorem of Gross–Siebert–Ruddat identifies these with the periods of the mirror family. This framework has been used to prove mirror symmetry for log Calabi–Yau surfaces and for many higher‑dimensional examples, and it provides a geometric interpretation of the tropical vertex and the wall‑crossing formulas of Kontsevich–Soibelman.

Toric Varieties over Non‑Archimedean Fields and Berkovich Spaces

Let K be a complete non‑archimedean field with valuation val : K* → ℝ and residue field k. A toric variety X(Δ) over K admits a natural analytification X(Δ)an in the sense of Berkovich. The Berkovich space contains a canonical skeleton Sk(X) which is a polyhedral complex canonically identified with the tropical variety of X(Δ). Concretely, Sk(X) is the union of the cones σ ⊗ ℝ inside N_ℝ, glued along faces, and the retraction map τ : X(Δ)an → Sk(X) is given by the valuation of the torus coordinates. The fibers of τ over the relative interior of a cone σ are isomorphic to the analytification of the torus T_σ = Hom(σ^⊥ ∩ M, K*). This description makes the cohomology of X(Δ)an computable via the weight spectral sequence of Nicaise–Xu: the E₁ page is the direct sum of the cohomology of the toric strata, and the differentials are induced by the face maps of the fan. In particular, the étale cohomology of X(Δ) with ℓ‑adic coefficients (ℓ ≠ char k) is isomorphic to the singular cohomology of the skeleton with a Galois action factoring through the inertia group. For a proper toric variety, the skeleton is a compact polyhedral complex, and its homology groups are the graded pieces of the weight filtration on H^*(XK̄, ℚ_ℓ). This tropical approach also yields a non‑archimedean analogue of the Danilov–Jurkiewicz theorem: the cohomology ring of the skeleton with coefficients in the sheaf of piecewise polynomial functions is the Stanley–Reisner ring of the fan. Moreover, the non‑archimedean SYZ conjecture predicts that the Berkovich analytification of a Calabi–Yau hypersurface in a toric variety fibers over its skeleton with torus fibers, and the mirror is obtained by dualizing the integral affine structure on the skeleton.

Toric Hilbert Schemes and Quiver Varieties

The Hilbert scheme of n points on a smooth toric surface X(Δ) carries a natural torus action, and its fixed points are in bijection with collections of monomial ideals in the coordinate rings of the affine patches, i.e. with n‑tuples of Young diagrams fitting inside the cones of Δ. This combinatorial description was used by Ellingsrud–Strømme to compute the Betti numbers of Hilb^n(ℂ²) and by Nakajima to identify Hilb^n(ℂ²) with a quiver variety for the Jordan quiver. For a general smooth toric surface, the fixed points correspond to tuples of partitions (λ_ρ) indexed by the rays ρ ∈ Δ(1) such that Σ |λ_ρ| = n and the partitions satisfy compatibility conditions along the edges of the fan. The tangent space at a fixed point decomposes into weight spaces whose characters are given by the hook lengths of the boxes in the diagrams. The equivariant Poincaré polynomial is then a sum over fixed points of products of (1 − tweight)-1. This formula matches the generating function of the equivariant Donaldson–Thomas invariants of the local Calabi–Yau threefold Tot(K_X). Moreover, the toric Hilbert scheme Hilb^n(X) is isomorphic to a fine moduli space of framed representations of the McKay quiver associated to the fan, and the torus action corresponds to the natural scaling of the framing. The wall‑crossing formula for the DT invariants as the stability condition varies is governed by the Kontsevich–Soibelman wall‑crossing identity, and the chambers are precisely the cones of the secondary fan of the polytope defining X. This connection makes toric Hilbert schemes a testing ground for the integration map of Joyce–Song and for the categorification of DT invariants via cohomological Hall algebras.

  • Confusing the Mori cone NE(X) with the cone of movable curves Mov(X); they are dual to Eff(X) and Nef(X) respectively, not to each other.
  • Assuming every toric stack is a root stack of a toric variety; some stacks require gerby structures not captured by root data.
  • Neglecting the age shift when computing orbifold cohomology; the grading is not the same as the ordinary cohomological degree.
  • Treating the scattering diagram in the Gross–Siebert program as a set of walls in the fan; it lives on the integral affine manifold B, not on the fan itself.
  • Overlooking that the Berkovich skeleton of a toric variety is a deformation retract only after a suitable toric alteration; the retraction map is not a homeomorphism in general.
  • Identifying the fixed points of Hilb^n(X) with arbitrary n‑tuples of partitions; the compatibility conditions along cone edges are essential.
  • Forgetting that the secondary fan governs both toric flips and wall‑crossing for DT invariants; the chambers are the same but the stability conditions differ.

These advanced topics illustrate how toric geometry serves as a computational laboratory for birational geometry, mirror symmetry, non‑archimedean analysis, and enumerative invariants.

Toric Varieties and Cluster Algebras: Mutation, Seeds, and the Laurent Phenomenon

Cluster algebras, introduced by Fomin and Zelevinsky in 2002, provide a combinatorial framework that intersects toric geometry through the notion of toric degenerations. A cluster algebra of geometric type is defined by an initial seed consisting of a quiver (or an exchange matrix) and a set of cluster variables. The mutation rule generates new seeds, and the Laurent phenomenon guarantees that every cluster variable is a Laurent polynomial in the initial cluster variables with integer coefficients. When the quiver arises from a triangulation of a marked surface, the associated cluster algebra coincides with the coordinate ring of a corresponding moduli space, which often admits a toric degeneration. For instance, the Grassmannian Gr(2,n) has a cluster algebra structure whose initial seed corresponds to the Plücker coordinates; the toric degeneration given by the Gelfand–Tsetlin polytope yields a flat family whose special fiber is a toric variety whose fan is the normal fan of the polytope. The cluster variables then become characters of the torus acting on this toric variety, and mutation corresponds to a piecewise-linear transformation on the tropicalization of the variety. This connection was exploited by Gross, Hacking, Keel, and Kontsevich (2018) to construct canonical bases for cluster algebras via theta functions on the associated log Calabi–Yau varieties. In the toric setting, the exchange matrix can be read off from the intersection pairing of toric divisors on a suitable blow‑up of the toric variety, and the mutation sequence mirrors a sequence of toric flips. The Laurent phenomenon translates into the statement that the Cox ring of the toric variety is generated by the cluster variables, and the grading by the divisor class group matches the grading by the tropicalization of the cluster variables. This interplay provides a powerful computational tool: the fan of the toric degeneration can be read directly from the quiver, and the cluster complex is isomorphic to the simplicial complex of compatible collections of rays in the fan.

Toric Calabi–Yau Threefolds, Brane Tilings, and Dimer Models

A toric Calabi–Yau threefold is a non‑compact toric variety whose canonical divisor is trivial; equivalently, its fan in Nℝ ≅ ℝ³ consists of cones over a lattice polygon in a plane at height one. Such threefolds are classified by convex lattice polygons up to GL(2,ℤ) equivalence, and their toric diagrams are precisely the Newton polygons of the defining equations. In string theory, D‑branes probing these singularities give rise to supersymmetric gauge theories whose matter content and superpotential are encoded by a bipartite graph on a torus called a dimer model or brane tiling. The dimer model was systematized by Hanany, He, and collaborators around 2005: each face of the dimer corresponds to a gauge group factor, each edge to a bifundamental chiral field, and each node to a superpotential term. The perfect matchings of the dimer are in bijection with the toric divisors of the Calabi–Yau threefold, and the Kasteleyn matrix of the dimer computes the partition function, which equals the generating function of Donaldson–Thomas invariants of the threefold. The toric fan can be reconstructed from the dimer by taking the Newton polygon of the characteristic polynomial of the Kasteleyn matrix; the resulting polygon is the toric diagram. Seiberg duality of the gauge theory corresponds to a mutation of the dimer, which in toric geometry is a toric flip (a change of triangulation of the toric diagram). The dimer model also provides a combinatorial description of the derived category of coherent sheaves on the toric Calabi–Yau: the category is equivalent to the category of representations of the Jacobian algebra of the quiver with superpotential, and the stability conditions for Bridgeland stability are governed by the central charge given by the periods of the holomorphic three‑form. This dictionary has been used to compute generating functions of BPS invariants, to test the topological vertex, and to construct explicit mirror geometries via the SYZ fibration whose base is the amoeba of the dimer's spectral curve.

Worked Example: From Dimer to Toric Diagram
Consider the dimer model for the conifold, which is a square with two black and two white nodes and edges forming a 4‑cycle. Write down the Kasteleyn matrix, compute its determinant, and identify the Newton polygon. Show that the polygon is the standard square with vertices (0,0), (1,0), (0,1), (1,1) in the plane at height one, and explain how the four perfect matchings correspond to the four toric divisors of the conifold.
Assign coordinates to the nodes: black nodes at positions (0,0) and (1,1), white nodes at (1,0) and (0,1) on the unit square torus. Choose a Kasteleyn orientation (e.g., all horizontal edges oriented right, vertical edges oriented up, with a sign change on one edge to satisfy the Kasteleyn condition). The Kasteleyn matrix K is a 2×2 matrix with entries K₁₁ = z + w, K₁₂ = 1 + zw, K₂₁ = 1 + zw, K₂₂ = z + w (up to gauge). Its determinant is (z + w)² − (1 + zw)² = z² + w² − 1 − z²w². The Newton polygon of this Laurent polynomial in variables z,w is the convex hull of exponent vectors (2,0), (0,2), (0,0), (2,2). After a GL(2,ℤ) change of basis (u = z², v = w²) this becomes the square with vertices (0,0), (1,0), (0,1), (1,1). The four perfect matchings of the dimer are the four ways to select two disjoint edges covering all nodes; each matching gives a monomial in the determinant, and the exponent vector of that monomial is a vertex of the Newton polygon. These four vertices correspond to the four toric divisors of the conifold, which are the irreducible components of the toric boundary. The fan of the conifold is the cone over this square, confirming the toric diagram.
Exam Strategy: Dimer–Toric Dictionary
When a question asks to pass from a dimer model to the toric diagram, first write the Kasteleyn matrix with formal variables for each edge. Compute the determinant as a Laurent polynomial; its Newton polygon (after a suitable GL(2,ℤ) transformation) is the toric diagram. Remember that perfect matchings map to vertices of the Newton polygon, and Seiberg duality corresponds to a flip of the triangulation of that polygon. Practice with the conifold, ℂ³/ℤ₃, and the suspended pinch point (SPP) to internalize the steps.

Toric Varieties in Arithmetic Geometry: Heights, Rational Points, and Manin's Conjecture

Toric varieties serve as a primary testing ground for Manin's conjecture on the asymptotic distribution of rational points of bounded height on Fano varieties. For a smooth projective toric variety X over a number field K, the anticanonical divisor −K_X is ample, and the height function H−K_X can be expressed explicitly in terms of the toric data. Batyrev and Tschinkel (1995) proved Manin's conjecture for toric varieties: the number N(X, B) of K‑rational points of height ≤ B satisfies N(X, B) ∼ c_X B (log B)ρ−1 as B → ∞, where ρ = rank Pic(X) and c_X is the Peyre constant, a product of local densities. The proof uses the description of rational points via the Cox ring: a point corresponds to a primitive integer vector in the total coordinate space modulo the torus action, and the height condition translates into a set of linear inequalities defining a region in ℝΔ(1). Counting lattice points in this region yields the asymptotic via geometry of numbers. The constant c_X factors as α(X) β(X) τ(X), where α(X) is the volume of the effective cone, β(X) = 1 for toric varieties (the Brauer–Manin obstruction vanishes), and τ(X) is the Tamagawa measure of the adelic space. For singular toric varieties, the conjecture was extended by Batyrev and Tschinkel (1998) using the minimal model program to resolve singularities while controlling the height. The toric case also illustrates the role of thin sets: accumulating subvarieties correspond to toric strata whose anticanonical degree is smaller, and their contribution is of lower order. Recent work by Bourqui and Tschinkel (2020) refined the error term using the theory of universal torsors and the geometry of the Cox ring. In addition, toric varieties provide explicit examples where the Batyrev–Manin conjecture for Campana points (points with bounded multiplicity) can be tested, and where the Manin–Peyre conjecture for varieties with a group action can be verified by comparing the height zeta function with the Ehrhart series of the associated polytope.

Derived Categories of Toric Varieties and Homological Mirror Symmetry

The bounded derived category of coherent sheaves D^b(Coh X) on a smooth projective toric variety X admits a full exceptional collection constructed from line bundles associated to the rays of the fan. Bondal (1990) and later Kawamata (2006) showed that the collection {O_X(D) | D ∈ Pic(X) with certain ordering} is exceptional and generates D^b(Coh X). The endomorphism algebra of this collection is the path algebra of a quiver with relations, which is precisely the Jacobian algebra of the quiver obtained from the toric data via the McKay correspondence for abelian subgroups of SL(3,ℂ). Homological mirror symmetry (HMS) for toric varieties, proved by Abouzaid (2009) for toric Calabi–Yau manifolds and by Fukaya–Oh–Ohta–Ono (2010) for compact toric Fano manifolds, states an equivalence between D^b(Coh X) and the Fukaya category Fuk(X^∨) of the mirror Landau–Ginzburg model (X^∨, W). The mirror X^∨ is a toric variety with a superpotential W given by the sum of monomials corresponding to the toric divisors. The equivalence sends the exceptional collection to a set of Lagrangian thimbles (vanishing cycles) in the fiber of W, and the morphisms between them are computed by Floer cohomology, which matches the Ext groups via the toric mirror map. The toric mirror map identifies the Kähler parameters of X with the complex parameters of W, and the quantum cohomology ring QH^*(X) is isomorphic to the Jacobian ring of W. This isomorphism is a manifestation of the closed‑string mirror symmetry, while HMS provides the open‑string version. The toric setting makes the construction completely combinatorial: the fan of X determines the Newton polytope of W, and the secondary fan of that polytope governs the wall‑crossing of stability conditions on both sides. Recent advances by Ganatra–Pardon–Shende (2020) use microlocal sheaf theory to give a purely topological proof of HMS for toric varieties, identifying the wrapped Fukaya category with the category of constructible sheaves on the skeleton of the mirror.

Toric Degenerations of Flag Varieties and the Gelfand–Tsetlin Polytope

Flag varieties G/B for a semisimple algebraic group G admit flat toric degenerations whose special fibers are toric varieties defined by the Gelfand–Tsetlin polytope. The construction, due to Kogan and Miller (2000) for type A and extended by Caldero (2002) and Alexeev–Brion (2004) to other types, uses a valuation on the coordinate ring ℂ[G/B] coming from a highest‑weight vector. The associated graded algebra is the semigroup algebra of the lattice points in the Gelfand–Tsetlin polytope GT(λ) for a dominant weight λ. This polytope is defined by interlacing inequalities between the eigenvalues of successive principal minors of a Hermitian matrix; its lattice points correspond to Gelfand–Tsetlin patterns, which index a basis of the irreducible representation V_λ. The toric variety X(GT(λ)) is a projective toric variety whose fan is the normal fan of GT(λ). The degeneration is equivariant with respect to a maximal torus T ⊂ G, and the T‑fixed points of the flag variety correspond to the vertices of GT(λ). The moment map image of the flag variety under the T‑action is exactly GT(λ), so the degeneration can be viewed as a degeneration of the symplectic structure to a toric symplectic manifold. This construction has applications to the representation theory of quantum groups: the crystal basis of V_λ is realized as the set of lattice points in GT(λ), and the toric degeneration provides a geometric interpretation of the crystal operators as piecewise‑linear maps on the polytope. Moreover, the toric variety X(GT(λ)) is a spherical variety for G, and its Cox ring is the homogeneous coordinate ring of the flag variety, which is a cluster algebra of finite type. The secondary fan of GT(λ) coincides with the fan of the toric variety of the string polytope, and wall‑crossing in this secondary fan corresponds to mutations in the cluster algebra structure on ℂ[G/B]. This circle of ideas connects the geometry of flag varieties, the combinatorics of polytopes, and the algebra of cluster algebras, providing a unified framework for studying degenerations, mirror symmetry, and representation theory.

  • Confusing the Laurent phenomenon in cluster algebras with the fact that the Cox ring is a polynomial ring; the Cox ring of a toric variety is polynomial, but the cluster algebra structure on a subring may have non‑trivial exchange relations.
  • Assuming every toric Calabi–Yau threefold admits a dimer model; dimer models exist only for those whose toric diagram is a convex lattice polygon with a consistent bipartite graph, which is always true for toric Calabi–Yau threefolds but the construction of the dimer requires a choice of triangulation.
  • Neglecting the role of the Brauer–Manin obstruction in Manin's conjecture for non‑toric varieties; for toric varieties it vanishes, but this is a special feature not to be generalized.
  • Mixing up the exceptional collection of line bundles on a toric variety with a full strong exceptional collection; the Bondal collection is strong only after a suitable ordering, and the endomorphism algebra is not necessarily hereditary.
  • Thinking that the Gelfand–Tsetlin polytope is the moment polytope for the full group G; it is the moment polytope for a maximal torus T, and the G‑action does not preserve the toric degeneration.
  • Overlooking that the secondary fan of the Gelfand–Tsetlin polytope governs both toric flips and cluster mutations; the chambers correspond to different cluster structures on the coordinate ring.
  • Forgetting that the mirror Landau–Ginzburg superpotential for a compact toric Fano variety is a sum of monomials corresponding to the toric divisors, not an arbitrary Laurent polynomial.
The advanced topics above illustrate how toric geometry continues to serve as a computational laboratory linking combinatorics, arithmetic, representation theory, and string theory.

Quantum Cohomology of Toric Varieties and the Mirror Theorem

The quantum cohomology ring QH*(X) of a smooth projective toric variety X is a deformation of the ordinary cohomology ring where the cup product is corrected by genus‑zero Gromov–Witten invariants. For toric varieties the presentation is completely combinatorial: if X is defined by a fan Σ in a lattice N, the small quantum cohomology is generated by the divisor classes Dρ corresponding to rays ρ∈Σ(1) with relations

  • Linear relations: Σ⟨m, uρ⟩ Dρ = 0 for all m∈M = Hom(N,ℤ).
  • Quantum Stanley–Reisner relations: ∏ρ∈P Dρ = qβ(P) ∏ρ∉P Dρ for each primitive collection P, where β(P) is the effective curve class dual to P.

Givental’s mirror theorem (1998) identifies the small J‑function of X with the hypergeometric series

JX(q, z) = z eτ/z Σd∈Eff(X) qd ∏ρ ∏k=1⟨Dρ, d⟩ (Dρ + k z) / ∏ρ ∏k=1⟨Dρ, d⟩ (k z)

where τ = Σ tρ Dρ. The coefficients of this series are precisely the genus‑zero one‑point Gromov–Witten invariants. The proof uses the torus action to localize the virtual fundamental class on the moduli space of stable maps M̄0,1(X, d) to fixed loci indexed by decorated graphs. Each fixed locus is a product of projective spaces, and the contribution reduces to a rational function in the equivariant parameters. Summing over all graphs yields the hypergeometric series. The mirror theorem has been extended to the big quantum cohomology by Coates–Corti–Iritani–Tseng (2009) using the quantum Lefschetz principle and to orbifold toric stacks by Chen–Ruan (2004). Applications include the computation of quantum periods for Fano toric varieties, the verification of the Gamma conjecture for toric complete intersections, and the construction of Frobenius manifolds on the Kähler moduli space.

Tropicalization of Subvarieties in Toric Varieties

Given a subvariety Y ⊂ Tn of the algebraic torus, its tropicalization Trop(Y) is a polyhedral complex in Nℝ = N ⊗ ℝ that captures the asymptotic behavior of Y under the valuation map val: (K×)n → ℝn for a non‑Archimedean field K. When Y is closed in a toric variety XΣ, the tropicalization lives in the extended tropical fan Σtrop obtained by adding the cones of Σ at infinity. The fundamental theorem of tropical geometry (Kapranov 2000; Speyer–Sturmfels 2004) states that Trop(Y) is the support of a balanced weighted polyhedral complex of dimension dim Y. For a hypersurface defined by a Laurent polynomial f = Σ cu xu, Trop(V(f)) is the corner locus of the piecewise‑linear function u ↦ val(cu) + ⟨u, ·⟩, i.e. the (n−1)‑skeleton of the regular subdivision of the Newton polytope Newt(f) induced by the valuations of the coefficients. This subdivision is dual to the tropical hypersurface. For higher‑codimension subvarieties, one uses the tropical basis theorem: a set of polynomials {fi} is a tropical basis if Trop(V(f1,…,fr)) = ∩ Trop(V(fi)). The existence of finite tropical bases for toric varieties follows from the fact that the coordinate ring is a multigraded polynomial ring modulo a toric ideal. Algorithms by Bogart–Jensen–Speyer–Sturmfels–Thomas (2007) compute tropicalizations via Gröbner fans over valued fields. Tropicalization respects many geometric operations: Trop(Y₁ ∩ Y₂) ⊂ Trop(Y₁) ∩ Trop(Y₂) with equality when the intersection is transverse in the sense of stable intersections; Trop(φ(Y)) = φtrop(Trop(Y)) for a toric morphism φ. These properties make tropicalization a powerful tool for computing Gromov–Witten invariants via the correspondence theorem of Nishinou–Siebert (2006) and Mikhalkin (2005), where counts of tropical curves in Σtrop equal genus‑zero invariants of XΣ.

Worked Example: Quantum Product on ℙ²
Compute the quantum product of the hyperplane class H with itself in the small quantum cohomology of ℙ², and verify the mirror theorem prediction for the J‑function coefficient of q.
The fan of ℙ² has rays u₁=(1,0), u₂=(0,1), u₃=(-1,-1). Divisor classes D₁,D₂,D₃ satisfy D₁=D₂=D₃=H and the linear relation D₁+D₂+D₃=0 gives 3H=0 in ordinary cohomology. The primitive collection is {ρ₁,ρ₂,ρ₃} with β = class of a line. The quantum Stanley–Reisner relation is D₁D₂D₃ = q. Since D₁=D₂=D₃=H, we get H³ = q. In ordinary cohomology H³=0, so the quantum product is H⋆H = H², and H⋆H⋆H = q. The small J‑function is J(q,z)=z eH log q /z Σd≥0 q^d ∏k=13d (H+kz)/ (kz)3. Expanding to order q: coefficient of q is (H+z)(H+2z)(H+3z)/ (z3 3! ) = (H³+6H²z+11Hz²+6z³)/6z³. Using H³=q, the term linear in q and independent of z is 1, matching the genus‑zero one‑point invariant ⟨τ₀(1)⟩0,1=1. Thus the mirror theorem holds for ℙ².
Exam Strategy
When asked to compute a quantum product on a toric variety, first write down the fan, identify primitive collections, and write the quantum Stanley–Reisner relations. Use the linear relations to reduce all divisor classes to a basis of Pic(X). The mirror theorem is often tested by asking for the first few terms of the J‑function; remember that the hypergeometric series simplifies because ⟨D_ρ, d⟩ are just the intersection numbers of the curve class with the toric divisors.

Okounkov Bodies and Toric Degenerations

An Okounkov body ΔY(L) ⊂ ℝn associated to a big line bundle L on a projective variety Y of dimension n is a convex body that encodes the asymptotic growth of sections of multiples of L. For a toric variety XΣ with a torus‑invariant ample divisor D, the Okounkov body with respect to a full flag of torus‑invariant subvarieties coincides exactly with the moment polytope PD = {m∈Mℝ | ⟨m, uρ⟩ ≥ -aρ ∀ρ}, where D = Σ aρ Dρ. This identification, proved by Kaveh–Khovanskii (2012) and independently by Lazarsfeld–Mustaţă (2009), shows that toric varieties are the prototypical examples where the Okounkov body is a lattice polytope. More generally, for any projective variety Y with a valuation v: ℂ(Y)× → ℤn of maximal rank, the graded algebra grv R(Y, L) = ⊕k≥0 ⊕α∈ℤn (Fα H⁰(Y, kL) / F>α H⁰(Y, kL)) is a finitely generated ℂ‑algebra whose Proj is a toric degeneration of Y. The fan of this toric variety is the normal fan of ΔY(L). Anderson (2013) showed that for a flag variety G/B with a highest‑weight line bundle Lλ, the Okounkov body with respect to the Gelfand–Tsetlin flag is the Gelfand–Tsetlin polytope GT(λ). The associated toric degeneration is precisely the one constructed by Kogan–Miller and Caldero. The theory extends to log‑concavity of Duistermaat–Heckman measures, to the computation of volumes of line bundles via vol(L) = n! vol(Δ(L)), and to the construction of Newton–Okounkov bodies for arbitrary valuations, providing a bridge between birational geometry and convex geometry.

Moduli of Stable Maps to Toric Varieties and Localization

The moduli stack M̄g,n(X, β) of genus‑g, n‑pointed stable maps to a toric variety X = XΣ with curve class β ∈ H₂(X, ℤ) carries a perfect obstruction theory and a virtual fundamental class. The torus T = (ℂ×)n acts on X and hence on the moduli space. The virtual localization formula of Graber–Pandharipande (1999) expresses any equivariant integral over the virtual class as a sum over T‑fixed loci. For toric targets, the fixed loci are indexed by decorated graphs Γ where vertices correspond to contracted components mapping to torus‑fixed points of X, edges correspond to components mapping to torus‑invariant curves with degree de > 0, and flags correspond to marked points. The contribution of a graph Γ is a product over vertices v of integrals over M̄g(v), n(v) of certain ψ‑classes and Hodge classes, multiplied by edge contributions of the form 1/(de (ψ1 + ψ2)) and vertex contributions involving the equivariant Euler class of the normal bundle. The sum over all graphs yields a combinatorial formula for genus‑zero and higher‑genus Gromov–Witten invariants. For ℙn this reproduces the Kontsevich recursion for rational curves. For general toric varieties, the formula has been implemented in the software package Givental and used to compute quantum cohomology rings, verify mirror symmetry predictions, and study the crepant resolution conjecture for toric orbifolds. The localization approach also underlies the proof of the mirror theorem by Givental and the higher‑genus mirror symmetry conjectures of Bouchard–Klemm–Mariño–Pasquetti (2008) for toric Calabi–Yau threefolds.

Batyrev–Borisov Mirror Construction for Complete Intersections in Toric Varieties

Batyrev’s original mirror construction (1994) associates to a reflexive polytope Δ ⊂ Mℝ a Calabi–Yau hypersurface Z ⊂ XΔ and a mirror family Z∨ ⊂ XΔ∨ where Δ∨ is the polar polytope. Borisov extended this to Calabi–Yau complete intersections of codimension r ≥ 2 using nef‑partitions of a reflexive polytope Δ. A nef‑partition is a decomposition of the set of vertices of Δ into r subsets V₁,…,Vr such that the Minkowski sum of the corresponding lattice polytopes Δi = Conv(Vi) equals Δ and each Δi is a lattice polytope containing the origin in its interior. The anticanonical divisor of XΔ splits as a sum of nef divisors D = D₁+…+Dr, and a generic section of ⊕ O(Di) defines a Calabi–Yau complete intersection Z of dimension n−r. The mirror family is constructed from the dual nef‑partition of the polar polytope Δ∨. The Hodge numbers satisfy the mirror symmetry relations hp,q(Z) = hn−r−p,q(Z∨). The combinatorial formulas for Hodge numbers involve counting lattice points in faces of the Δi and their duals. For example, for a Calabi–Yau threefold complete intersection of two hypersurfaces in a toric fourfold, the Hodge numbers are given by

h1,1 = l(Δ) - 5 - Σ l*(codim 1 faces) + Σ l*(codim 2 faces) - Σ l*(codim 3 faces)

where l(Δ) is the number of lattice points in Δ and l* counts interior lattice points. The construction has been used to produce thousands of mirror pairs, to test the topological mirror symmetry conjecture, and to provide geometric backgrounds for heterotic string compactifications. Recent work by Braun–Kreuzer (2011) classifies all nef‑partitions of reflexive polytopes up to dimension five, yielding a complete list of toric Calabi–Yau complete intersections in those dimensions.

  • Confusing the quantum Stanley–Reisner relations with the classical Stanley–Reisner ideal; the quantum relations involve curve classes and Novikov variables.
  • Assuming tropicalization of a subvariety always equals the intersection of tropicalizations of its defining equations; a tropical basis is required for equality.
  • Thinking every Okounkov body is a lattice polytope; it is a convex body that is a polytope only when the valuation comes from a toric flag or a Khovanskii basis.
  • Neglecting the contribution of Hodge integrals at vertices in the virtual localization formula; they are essential for higher‑genus invariants.
  • Believing any decomposition of a reflexive polytope gives a nef‑partition; the Minkowski sum condition and interior‑origin condition are both necessary.
  • Mixing up the polar polytope Δ∨ with the dual nef‑partition; the mirror complete intersection lives in the toric variety of Δ∨ but uses the dual nef‑partition of Δ∨.

These advanced topics illustrate how toric geometry continues to provide explicit computational frameworks for quantum cohomology, tropical geometry, Okounkov bodies, localization, and mirror symmetry of complete intersections.

Gamma Class, Integral Structures, and the Gamma Conjecture for Toric Varieties

The gamma class Γ̂X of a smooth projective toric variety X is defined as the product over all toric prime divisors Di of the gamma function evaluated at 1 + Di. In the Chow ring A*X this becomes a polynomial in the divisor classes whose coefficients are rational numbers involving values of the Riemann zeta function at positive integers. For a toric Fano variety the anticanonical class −KX is ample, and the gamma class provides a canonical integral structure on the quantum cohomology QH*X after the change of variables q = et. The Gamma Conjecture (Galkin–Golyshev–Iritani, 2009) asserts that the asymptotic expansion of the fundamental solution of the quantum differential equation at the large radius limit is governed by Γ̂X and that the Stokes matrix of the quantum connection coincides with the Euler pairing on the bounded derived category Db(Coh X). For toric varieties the conjecture has been proved by Iritani (2009) using the toric mirror theorem and the Mellin–Barnes integral representation of the I-function. The proof identifies the gamma class with the asymptotic class of the I-function and shows that the central charge Z(E) = ∫X Γ̂X · ch(E) · e−c1(X)/2 matches the period integrals of the mirror Landau–Ginzburg model.

Key computational features:

  • The gamma class is multiplicative under products: Γ̂X×Y = Γ̂X ⊗ Γ̂Y.
  • For projective space ℙn, Γ̂ℙn = Γ(1+H)n+1 where H is the hyperplane class.
  • The integral structure is independent of the choice of toric divisors; it is determined by the fan.

Examiners often test the ability to compute the first few terms of Γ̂X for a given fan and to relate them to the coefficients of the I-function. A common twist is to ask for the gamma class of a toric blow‑up and to verify the Gamma Conjecture prediction for the Stokes matrix in rank two.

Crepant Resolution Conjecture and Toric Orbifolds

Let 𝒳 be a smooth toric Deligne–Mumford stack with coarse moduli space X that admits a crepant resolution π : Y → X. The Crepant Resolution Conjecture (Ruan, 2006; Bryan–Graber, 2008) predicts that the orbifold quantum cohomology QH*orb(𝒳) and the quantum cohomology QH*Y become isomorphic after analytic continuation in the quantum parameters and a linear change of variables (the “crepant transformation”). For toric orbifolds the conjecture can be formulated entirely in terms of the I-functions: the I-function of 𝒳 (defined via the extended stacky fan) and the I-function of Y (defined via the refined fan of the resolution) are related by a substitution qi ↦ (−1)di qi and a shift of the Kähler parameters by the ages of the twisted sectors. The conjecture has been proved for all toric Calabi–Yau threefold orbifolds by Coates–Iritani–Tseng (2015) using the toric mirror theorem and the wall‑crossing formula for the I-function. In the non‑Calabi–Yau case the conjecture holds for weighted projective spaces and their crepant resolutions (e.g., ℙ(1,1,2) resolved to the total space of Oℙ1(−2)). The proof proceeds by matching the J-functions after applying the mirror map and showing that the difference is a solution of the quantum differential equation that vanishes at the large radius limit.

Important distinctions:

  • The orbifold I-function includes contributions from twisted sectors indexed by the box elements of the stacky fan.
  • The crepant transformation involves a sign (−1)age for each twisted sector.
  • The conjecture fails if the resolution is not crepant (i.e., if the discrepancy divisor is non‑zero).

A typical exam question asks to write the I-function for the orbifold ℙ(1,1,3) and its crepant resolution, then to perform the analytic continuation and verify the equality of the first three quantum products.

Worked Example: Crepant Resolution of ℙ(1,1,2)
Compute the orbifold I‑function of the weighted projective line 𝒳 = ℙ(1,1,2) and the I‑function of its crepant resolution Y = Tot(Oℙ1(−2)). Show that after the substitution q ↦ −q the two I‑functions coincide up to the mirror map.
The stacky fan of ℙ(1,1,2) has rays (1,0), (0,1), (−1,−2) with ages 0, 0, 1/2. The orbifold I‑function is I𝒳(q,z) = ep log q / z Σd≥0 qd ∏k=1d (p + kz)−2 ∏k=12d (2p + kz)−1 · (1 + (p/z) · [1/2]) where [1/2] denotes the twisted sector. The resolution Y has fan with rays (1,0), (0,1), (−1,−1), (−1,−2). Its I‑function is IY(Q,z) = ep1 log Q1/z + p2 log Q2/z Σd1,d2≥0 Q1d1 Q2d2 ∏ (p1+kz)−1 ∏ (p2+kz)−1 ∏ (p1+p2+kz)−1 ∏ (2p1+2p2+kz)−1. Setting Q1 = q, Q2 = 1 and identifying p = p1+p2 yields IY = ep log q / z Σ qd ∏ (p+kz)−2 ∏ (2p+kz)−1. The twisted sector term in I𝒳 contributes a factor (1 + p/(2z)) which matches the expansion of the extra factor from the resolution after the substitution q ↦ −q. The mirror map for both is log q = log Q + O(Q), so the equality holds.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the crepant resolution conjecture, first write the stacky fan and the refined fan side by side. Identify the box elements and their ages. The substitution q ↦ (−1)age q is the key step; do not forget the sign. Then match the I‑functions term by term up to the required order. The mirror map is usually trivial at the first order, so you can often skip its explicit computation unless the question explicitly asks for it.

McKay Correspondence for Toric Quotient Singularities

Let G ⊂ SL(n,ℂ) be a finite abelian subgroup acting diagonally on ℂn. The quotient singularity ℂn/G is toric, and its minimal resolution Y → ℂn/G is also toric. The McKay correspondence establishes an equivalence between the derived category Db(CohG(ℂn)) of G-equivariant coherent sheaves and Db(Coh Y). In the toric setting this equivalence is realized by the Fourier–Mukai transform with kernel the structure sheaf of the G-cluster scheme HilbG(ℂn), which coincides with the toric resolution Y. The fan of Y is obtained by subdividing the cone σ = ℝ≥0n using the junior simplex Δ = {u ∈ σ ∩ (1/|G|)ℤn | Σ ui = 1}. Each lattice point in Δ corresponds to an irreducible representation of G, and the toric divisors of Y correspond to the facets of Δ. The intersection matrix of the exceptional divisors is the negative of the Cartan matrix of the associated affine Dynkin diagram when n = 2 (Klein singularities). For n = 3 the correspondence relates the representation theory of G to the geometry of the crepant resolution used in the topological vertex. The derived equivalence implies that the Hochschild homology of Y is isomorphic to the G-invariant part of the Hochschild homology of ℂn, which yields the orbifold Hodge numbers.

Key points for examination:

  • The junior simplex is the convex hull of the images of the standard basis vectors in the quotient lattice.
  • The number of exceptional divisors equals the number of non‑trivial irreducible representations of G.
  • The McKay quiver of G is the dual graph of the toric fan of Y.

A common trap is to confuse the McKay correspondence for abelian subgroups (toric) with the non‑abelian case where the resolution is not toric and the derived equivalence is more subtle (Bridgeland–King–Reid). Examiners may ask to draw the fan of the resolution for G = ℤ/3 × ℤ/3 ⊂ SL(3,ℂ) and to identify the corresponding quiver.

Donaldson–Thomas Invariants of Toric Calabi–Yau Threefolds

For a toric Calabi–Yau threefold X (e.g., the total space of Oℙ1(−1)⊕Oℙ1(−1) or ℂ3), the Donaldson–Thomas (DT) invariants DTβ count ideal sheaves IZ with ch3(IZ) = β weighted by the Behrend function. The partition function ZDT(q,Q) = Σβ DTβ qn Qβ factorizes into a product of MacMahon functions M(q) = ∏k=1∞ (1−qk)−k associated to each toric fixed point, multiplied by edge and face contributions determined by the toric diagram. The topological vertex (Aganagic–Klemm–Mariño–Vafa, 2003) gives a combinatorial algorithm: assign a plane partition to each vertex, a pair of partitions to each edge, and sum over all configurations with weight q|π|. The DT/PT correspondence (Pandharipande–Thomas, 2009) relates DT invariants to stable pair invariants PTβ; for toric CY3 the equivalence is proved by the wall‑crossing formula of Kontsevich–Soibelman (2008) applied to the quiver with potential derived from the dimer model. The wall‑crossing formula expresses the change of DT invariants across walls of marginal stability in terms of the product of quantum dilogarithms. In the toric case the quiver is the periodic quiver of the dimer, and the wall‑crossing reduces to the pentagon identity for the quantum dilogarithm.

Computational highlights:

  • The DT partition function of ℂ3 is M(q).
  • For the resolved conifold, ZDT = M(q)2 ∏k=1∞ (1−Q qk)−k.
  • The vertex formalism computes all genus‑zero DT invariants; higher genus requires the refined vertex.

Examiners often ask to write the topological vertex expression for a given toric diagram (e.g., the suspended pinch point) and to extract the DT invariants for the first few curve classes. A twist is to require the use of the wall‑crossing formula to compute DT invariants in a non‑generic chamber.

Quantum K‑Theory of Toric Varieties and q‑Difference Equations

Quantum K‑theory deforms the K‑theory ring K(X) by genus‑zero Gromov–Witten invariants with K‑theoretic insertions. For a toric variety X the small quantum K‑theory ring QK(X) is generated by the classes of toric line bundles O(Di) with relations given by the quantum K‑theoretic I-function IK(q, Q) (Givental–Lee, 2003). The I-function satisfies a system of q‑difference equations Δi IK = 0 where Δi are the quantum difference operators obtained by quantizing the classical K‑theory relations. The mirror theorem for quantum K‑theory (Lee, 2004; Givental, 2015) identifies the J-function (the generating function of K‑theoretic Gromov–Witten invariants) with the I-function after a q‑hypergeometric mirror map. The q‑difference equations are the K‑theoretic analog of the quantum differential equations; their solutions form a finite‑dimensional vector space over the field of elliptic functions. The monodromy of the q‑difference system is related to the Stokes matrix of the quantum differential equation via the non‑commutative Hodge structure. For toric varieties the quantum K‑theory ring has been computed explicitly: the relations are ∏i (1−qDi)⟨Di,β⟩ = qβ for each curve class β. The quantum K‑theory of flag varieties (which are not toric but admit toric degenerations) can be obtained by specialization.

Key facts for exams:

  • The quantum K‑theory ring is a deformation of the classical K‑theory ring over the Novikov ring ℤ[[q, Q]].
  • The q‑difference operators are of the form Δ = ∏ (1−qD) − qβ.
  • The mirror map is a q‑hypergeometric series; its first term is the classical mirror map.

A typical question asks to write the quantum K‑theory relations for ℙ2 and to verify that the q‑difference equation annihilates the I-function up to order Q2. Another twist is to compare the quantum K‑theory of a toric variety with the quantum cohomology via the limit q → 1 (the “classical limit”).

  • Confusing the gamma class with the Todd class; the gamma class involves Γ(1+D) while the Todd class involves D/(1−e−D).
  • Assuming the crepant resolution conjecture holds for any resolution; it requires the resolution to be crepant (discrepancy zero).
  • Misidentifying the junior simplex in the McKay correspondence; it is the slice Σ ui = 1 in the cone, not the whole cone.
  • Forgetting the sign (−1)age in the crepant transformation of the I‑function.
  • Thinking the topological vertex computes all genus DT invariants; it computes only genus zero unless refined.
  • Mixing the quantum K‑theory q‑parameter with the Novikov variable Q; they play different roles.
  • Neglecting the wall‑crossing formula when computing DT invariants in a non‑generic chamber; the invariants change across walls.
These advanced topics illustrate the deep interplay between toric geometry, mirror symmetry, derived categories, and enumerative invariants.

Landau–Ginzburg Models and Hori–Vafa Mirror Symmetry for Fano Toric Varieties

While Batyrev's construction provides a mirror Calabi–Yau hypersurface for a reflexive polytope, the Hori–Vafa proposal (2000) gives a mirror Landau–Ginzburg (LG) model for any Fano toric variety. Let X be a smooth Fano toric variety of dimension n defined by a complete fan Σ in Nℝ with primitive ray generators v1, …, vm. The mirror is the pair ((ℂ×)n, W) where the superpotential W : (ℂ×)n → ℂ is the Laurent polynomial W(z) = Σi=1m zvi. The variables z1, …, zn are coordinates on the dual torus (ℂ×)n = Hom(M, ℂ×). The critical points of W correspond to solutions of the quantum cohomology relations of X. The Jacobian ring Jac(W) = ℂ[z1±1, …, zn±1] / (∂W/∂z1, …, ∂W/∂zn) is isomorphic to the small quantum cohomology ring QH•(X) after a mirror map identifying the Kähler parameters Qi with coefficients in W. Givental's I-function IX(Q, z) = Σd∈Eff(X) Qd ∏i=1m 1 / ∏k=1⟨Di,d⟩ (Di + kz) matches the oscillatory integral ∫γ eW/z Ω / Ω0 over a suitable cycle γ, where Ω is the holomorphic volume form on (ℂ×)n. The mirror map is extracted from the coefficient of z0 in the I-function. For ℙn, W = z1 + … + zn + Q / (z1⋯zn) and the quantum cohomology relation is Hn+1 = Q. The Hori–Vafa mirror extends to toric stacks and to complete intersections via the Batyrev–Borisov construction, where the superpotential becomes a sum of monomials corresponding to the nef-partition. A key computational tool is the J-function, which generates genus-zero Gromov–Witten invariants with one descendant insertion; the mirror theorem (Givental 1998, Lian–Liu–Yau 1997) identifies JX with IX after the mirror map. Examiners often ask to write the superpotential for a given toric Fano variety (e.g., ℙ1 × ℙ1 or the blow-up of ℙ2 at a point) and to verify the quantum cohomology relation from the critical point equations.

Delzant's Theorem, Moment Maps, and Symplectic Toric Geometry

Delzant's theorem (1988) classifies compact symplectic toric manifolds — compact connected symplectic manifolds (M, ω) of dimension 2n equipped with an effective Hamiltonian action of a torus Tn = (ℝ/2πℤ)n — by their moment polytopes. A moment map μ : M → 𝔱* ≅ ℝn satisfies d⟨μ, ξ⟩ = ιξM ω for all ξ ∈ 𝔱, where ξM is the vector field generated by ξ. The image Δ = μ(M) is a convex polytope (Atiyah–Guillemin–Sternberg convexity theorem). Delzant's condition: Δ is a Delzant polytope — a rational convex polytope where at each vertex exactly n edges meet, and their primitive inward normal vectors form a ℤ-basis of the weight lattice ℤn. The theorem states: there is a bijection between isomorphism classes of compact symplectic toric manifolds and Delzant polytopes. The construction from a Delzant polytope Δ = {x ∈ ℝn | ⟨x, ui⟩ ≥ λi, i = 1, …, m} with primitive inward normals ui ∈ ℤn and constants λi ∈ ℝ is symplectic reduction: let K = ker(ℤm → ℤn, ei ↦ ui) ≅ ℤm−n. The torus Tm−n = K ⊗ ℝ/ℤ acts on ℂm with moment map μℂm(z) = (|z1|2/2, …, |zm|2/2). The reduced space μℂm−1(λ) / Tm−n for a regular value λ ∈ 𝔱*K is the symplectic toric manifold with moment polytope Δ. The Kähler form ω is the reduction of the standard form on ℂm. The correspondence with algebraic toric varieties: the fan Σ is the normal fan of Δ; the ray generators are the ui. The polytope Δ is the moment polytope of the ample line bundle L = 𝒪X(Σ λi Di). Duistermaat–Heckman measure: the pushforward of the Liouville measure ωn/n! under μ is the Lebesgue measure on Δ. This gives a symplectic proof of the Hirzebruch–Riemann–Roch theorem for toric varieties via localization. The symplectic perspective is essential for Floer theory, Lagrangian torus fibers, and the SYZ conjecture. Examiners test the construction of the reduced space from a given polytope (e.g., the trapezoid for the Hirzebruch surface F1) and the verification of the Delzant condition at vertices.

Worked Example: Superpotential and Quantum Cohomology of the Blow-up of ℙ² at a Point
Let X be the blow-up of ℙ² at a torus-fixed point. Its fan has rays v₁ = (1,0), v₂ = (0,1), v₃ = (−1,−1), v₄ = (−1,0). Write the Hori–Vafa superpotential W and derive the quantum cohomology relations from the critical point equations ∂W/∂z₁ = ∂W/∂z₂ = 0.
The primitive ray generators are v₁=(1,0), v₂=(0,1), v₃=(−1,−1), v₄=(−1,0). The superpotential is W(z₁,z₂) = z₁ + z₂ + z₁⁻¹z₂⁻¹ + Q₁ z₁⁻¹, where Q₁ is the Kähler parameter associated to the exceptional divisor (the ray v₄). The critical point equations are: ∂W/∂z₁ = 1 − z₁⁻²z₂⁻¹ − Q₁ z₁⁻² = 0 ⇒ z₁² = z₂⁻¹ + Q₁ ∂W/∂z₂ = 1 − z₁⁻¹z₂⁻² = 0 ⇒ z₂² = z₁⁻¹ Substitute z₁ = z₂⁻² into the first equation: z₂⁻⁴ = z₂⁻¹ + Q₁. Multiply by z₂⁴: 1 = z₂³ + Q₁ z₂⁴. The quantum cohomology ring QH•(X) is generated by the hyperplane class H (pullback from ℙ²) and the exceptional class E, with relations H² = E² + Q₀ (where Q₀ is the parameter for the line class) and H·E = 0, E² = −Q₁. In terms of the variables z₁, z₂ corresponding to the dual torus coordinates, the relations are exactly the critical point equations. Setting Q₀ = 1 (by scaling), the relation 1 = z₂³ + Q₁ z₂⁴ matches the quantum relation for the blow-up. The Jacobian ring ℂ[z₁±,z₂±]/(∂W) is 4-dimensional, matching the Betti numbers of X.
Exam Strategy: Mirror Symmetry Computations
When asked for the Hori–Vafa superpotential, always list the primitive ray generators first. The number of terms equals the number of rays. Each Kähler parameter Qᵢ attaches to the monomial for the corresponding ray; if the variety is Fano, all Qᵢ appear linearly. For the critical point equations, clear denominators to get polynomial relations. The quantum cohomology ring is the Jacobian ring; its dimension equals the sum of Betti numbers. Check that the number of critical points (counted with multiplicity) matches the Euler characteristic. For Delzant polytopes, verify the ℤ-basis condition at each vertex by computing the determinant of the matrix of inward normals — it must be ±1. The symplectic reduction construction is the inverse of the fan-to-polytope map: the polytope inequalities ⟨x, uᵢ⟩ ≥ λᵢ give the moment map image directly.

Toric Varieties in Algebraic Statistics: Maximum Likelihood and Toric Models

Algebraic statistics studies statistical models through the lens of commutative algebra and toric geometry. A toric model is a parametric statistical model where the probability distributions are given by monomial maps. Let A = (aij) be an d × n integer matrix of rank d with columns aj ∈ ℤd. The model is the map fA : (ℝ>0)d → Δn−1 (the open probability simplex) defined by fA(θ)j = θaj / Σk θak, where θa = ∏i=1d θiai. The Zariski closure of the image of fA in ℙn−1 is the projective toric variety XA associated to the matrix A. Its affine cone is Spec ℂ[ℕA], the semigroup algebra of the cone generated by the columns of A. The model is log-linear: log pj = ⟨aj, log θ⟩ − log(Σ θak). Maximum likelihood estimation (MLE) for a data vector u ∈ ℕn (counts) seeks p ∈ XA ∩ Δn−1 maximizing the likelihood L(p) = ∏ pjuj. The MLE exists if and only if u lies in the relative interior of the polytope PA = conv{a1, …, an} (Birch's theorem, 1963). When it exists, the MLE is unique and satisfies the moment equations A p = A u / |u|, where |u| = Σ uj. The algebraic degree of the MLE (the ML degree) is the number of complex critical points of the log-likelihood function on the toric variety; for a generic data vector it equals the volume of the polytope PA times d! (Catanese–Hosten–Khetan–Sturmfels, 2006). The toric fiber product of models corresponds to the join of polytopes. Graphical models for discrete random variables (e.g., hierarchical log-linear models) are toric models when the graph is decomposable; the matrix A is the incidence matrix of cliques. The EM algorithm for missing data in toric models has a geometric interpretation as alternating projections onto the toric variety and the linear space of observed margins. Examiners test the computation of the ML degree for small models (e.g., the 2×2 independence model has ML degree 1, the 2×2×2 no-three-way interaction model has ML degree 10) and the verification of the moment equations for a given data vector.

Tropical Compactifications of Subvarieties and Moduli of Curves

Tropical compactifications provide a systematic way to compactify a subvariety Y ⊂ (ℂ×)n inside a toric variety XΣ such that the boundary is a normal crossings divisor and the closure Ȳ is Schön (smooth over the generic point of every toric stratum). The tropical variety trop(Y) ⊂ ℝn is the support of a polyhedral complex of dimension dim Y, defined as the set of weight vectors w such that the initial ideal inw(I(Y)) contains no monomials. A fan Σ is a tropical fan for Y if its support equals trop(Y) and it refines the Gröbner fan of I(Y). Tevelev (2007) proved that if Σ is a tropical fan for Y, then the closure Ȳ in XΣ is a tropical compactification: Ȳ intersects each toric stratum transversely, and the intersection with the dense torus is Y. The moduli space of curves Mg,n admits tropical compactifications via the moduli space of tropical curves Mg,ntrop. The fan of the toric variety is the Bergman fan of the matroid associated to the graph of the tropical curve. Hacking–Keel–Tevelev (2009) constructed the Hassett spaces M̄g,A as log canonical models of M̄g with weighted boundary divisors; these are tropical compactifications of Mg when the weights are generic. The tropical Torelli map sends a tropical curve to its Jacobian, a principally polarized tropical abelian variety. The tropical Hodge numbers of a tropical variety are the dimensions of the cohomology of the associated tropical fan; for a Schön subvariety, they equal the Hodge numbers of the compactification. The tropicalization of the moduli space of abelian varieties Ag is the moduli space of principally polarized tropical abelian varieties, which is the quotient of the cone of positive definite quadratic forms by GL(g, ℤ). Examiners ask to compute the tropical variety of a given subvariety (e.g., a plane curve, a Grassmannian) and to verify that a given fan is a tropical fan. A common question: show that the closure of a curve in ℙ2 defined by a generic polynomial is a tropical compactification if and only if the Newton polygon subdivision is induced by the tropical curve.

The Minimal Model Program for Toric Varieties and Log Calabi–Yau Pairs

The Minimal Model Program (MMP) for toric varieties is entirely combinatorial and provides a testing ground for the general theory. A toric variety X = X(Σ) is a Mori dream space: its Cox ring is finitely generated, the nef cone is rational polyhedral, and there are finitely many small ℚ-factorial modifications. The cone of curves NE(X) is generated by the classes of torus-invariant curves Cτ corresponding to codimension-2 cones τ ∈ Σ. The Mori cone is the dual of the nef cone. A flip corresponds to a wall in the secondary fan (or GIT fan) of the Cox ring: crossing the wall replaces a set of cones in Σ by a different set with the same support, changing the variety by a birational map that is an isomorphism in codimension 1. The log MMP for a pair (X, D) where D = Σ ai Di is a ℚ-divisor supported on the toric boundary: the log canonical divisor is KX + D = Σ (ai − 1) Di. The pair is log Calabi–Yau if KX + D ≡ 0, i.e., ai = 1 for all i. In this case the log MMP terminates with a minimal model (where KX + D is nef) or a Mori fiber space (a fibration where KX + D is negative on fibers). For toric log Calabi–Yau pairs, the minimal models correspond to the maximal cones in the log canonical fan, which is a refinement of the secondary fan. The volume of KX + D is computed by the polytope formula: vol(KX + D) = n! vol(PKX+D) where P is the polytope {m ∈ Mℝ | ⟨m, vi⟩ ≥ ai − 1}. The abundance conjecture holds for toric varieties: if KX + D is nef, it is semi-ample. The termination of flips follows from the finiteness of the secondary fan. The log minimal model program for toric pairs is equivalent to the variation of GIT quotient for the Cox ring with respect to the linearization given by D. The crepant resolution of a log terminal singularity corresponds to a maximal projective crepant partial (MPCP) desingularization, which exists and is unique up to flops. Examiners test the computation of the cone of curves for a given fan, the identification of extremal rays, and the description of the corresponding contraction (divisorial, flipping, or fiber type). A typical question: given the fan of a singular toric threefold, run the MMP to reach a minimal model or Mori fiber space, drawing the fan at each step.

  • Confusing the Hori–Vafa superpotential with the Batyrev mirror — Hori–Vafa gives an LG model for Fano toric varieties; Batyrev gives a CY hypersurface for reflexive polytopes. They are different constructions.
  • Forgetting the Delzant condition at vertices — a rational polytope is Delzant iff at each vertex the primitive inward normals form a ℤ-basis; the determinant must be ±1, not just non-zero.
  • Misstating the MLE existence condition — the MLE exists iff the data vector lies in the relative interior of the model polytope, not just the polytope. Boundary points give non-existent or non-unique MLEs.
  • Confusing the tropical variety with the tropical fan — trop(Y) is a set; a tropical fan is a fan structure refining the Gröbner fan. Not every fan supported on trop(Y) is a tropical fan.
  • Assuming every toric variety is a Mori dream space — only ℚ-factorial toric varieties with finitely generated Cox ring are MDS; non-ℚ-factorial ones may have infinitely generated Cox rings.
  • Mixing up the log canonical divisor formula — KX + D = Σ (ai − 1) Di for D = Σ ai Di; the coefficient is ai − 1, not ai.
  • Thinking the secondary fan equals the Mori fan — the secondary fan parametrizes regular triangulations; the Mori fan (GIT fan) parametrizes GIT chambers. They coincide for smooth toric varieties but differ in general.

Stanley–Reisner Rings, Hochster's Formula, and Alexander Duality for Toric Varieties

The Cox ring of a toric variety X(Σ) is a polynomial ring S = k[x_ρ : ρ ∈ Σ(1)] graded by the class group Cl(X). The irrelevant ideal B(Σ) is generated by monomials ∏ρ∉σ(1) x_ρ for each cone σ ∈ Σ.
