The quotient S/B(Σ) is the Stanley–Reisner ring k[Δ] of the simplicial complex Δ whose faces are the sets of rays contained in a cone of Σ. Hochster's formula computes the graded pieces of the local cohomology modules H^i_B(S) in terms of reduced simplicial cohomology of links in Δ: for a degree α ∈ Cl(X), H^i_B(S)_α ≅ ⊕F∈Δ tilde{H}i-|F|-1(link_Δ(F); k) where the sum runs over faces F with α supported on F. This description yields the Betti numbers of toric varieties via the isomorphism H^*(X; k) ≅ H^*_B(S)[-dim X] for smooth projective X. Alexander duality for Stanley–Reisner rings states that the canonical module ωk[Δ] is isomorphic to the Stanley–Reisner ring of the Alexander dual complex Δ^∨, shifted by the degree of the anticanonical divisor. In toric geometry this duality reflects Serre duality on X and gives a combinatorial proof of the Hard Lefschetz theorem for projective toric varieties. For example, the fan of ℙ2 has Δ a 2‑simplex; its Stanley–Reisner ring is k[x,y,z]/(xyz) and Hochster's formula reproduces the cohomology ring k[h]/(h3). The same method applied to the fan of a Hirzebruch surface F_n yields the well‑known Betti numbers b0 = b4 = 1, b2 = 2, and the intersection form determined by the self‑intersection of the zero section.

Logarithmic Gromov–Witten Theory on Toric Varieties: Relative Invariants and Degeneration

A toric variety X carries a natural divisorial log structure M_X = O_X ∩ j_* OX^∘^* where X^∘ is the dense torus. Log stable maps to (X, D) with D the toric boundary are maps from log curves whose contact orders with D are prescribed by a tuple of cone‐wise linear functions on the fan. The virtual fundamental class of the moduli stack of log stable maps is constructed via the Olsson–Abramovich–Chen obstruction theory, and its degree defines logarithmic Gromov–Witten invariants Ng,β(μ) where μ records the contact orders. The degeneration formula of Li–Ruan and Ionel–Parker expresses absolute GW invariants of a toric variety as a sum over splittings of the curve class β into relative invariants of the pieces X0 and X_∞ glued along a toric divisor. In the toric setting the gluing data are encoded by tropical curves in the fan Σ: each tropical curve corresponds to a degeneration of the target, and the multiplicity of the tropical curve equals the product of relative invariants. Gross–Siebert's program identifies the generating function of log GW invariants with the scattering diagram of the mirror, providing a tropical proof of the remodeling conjecture for local toric Calabi–Yau threefolds. For instance, the log GW invariants of the resolved conifold Oℙ1(-1)⊕Oℙ1(-1) are computed by counting tropical curves in the fan of ℙ1×ℙ1 with appropriate unbounded edges; the result matches the topological vertex computation of Aganagic–Klemm–Mariño–Vafa.

Worked Example: Log Canonical Threshold of a Monomial Ideal
Compute the log canonical threshold lct(𝔞) of the monomial ideal 𝔞 = (x2 y, x y3) in k[x,y] using the Newton polyhedron method.
The Newton polyhedron P(𝔞) is the convex hull in ℝ2≥0 of the exponent vectors (2,1) and (1,3) plus the positive orthant. Its compact faces are the segment joining (2,1) and (1,3). Howald's formula states lct(𝔞) = minu∈ℝ2>0 (⟨u,1⟩+1)/⟨u, a⟩ where a runs over the vertices of the compact faces. For a vertex v = (v1,v2) the minimum is achieved when u is proportional to the inward normal of the face containing v. The segment has inward normal (1,1) (since the line through (2,1) and (1,3) has slope -2, normal (2,1) but we need the normal pointing into the orthant; the correct normal is (1,1) after scaling). Evaluating at u = (1,1): ⟨u,1⟩+1 = 1+1+1 = 3. For v = (2,1): ⟨u,v⟩ = 3, ratio = 1. For v = (1,3): ⟨u,v⟩ = 4, ratio = 3/4 = 0.75. The minimum is 3/4. Hence lct(𝔞) = 3/4.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the log canonical threshold of a toric ideal, first draw the Newton polyhedron of the generators. Identify the compact faces and compute the inward primitive normal vectors. Apply Howald's formula lct = minfaces (⟨n,1⟩+1)/⟨n, v⟩ where n is the primitive inward normal and v any vertex of that face. This avoids solving a linear programming problem from scratch and is the standard shortcut examiners expect.

Scattering Diagrams, Theta Functions, and Mirror Symmetry for Log Calabi–Yau Toric Varieties

For a log Calabi–Yau pair (Y, D) where Y is a toric variety and D a reduced toric divisor with K_Y + D ∼ 0, Gross–Hacking–Keel construct a scattering diagram 𝔇 in the tropicalization of the dual intersection complex of D. The diagram consists of walls (codimension‑1 cones) each carrying a formal power series f_𝔇 = 1 + c z^m where m is a primitive normal vector and c counts rational tropical curves with prescribed tangency conditions. Theta functions ϑ_p for p ∈ Σ^∘(ℤ) are defined as sums over broken lines in the scattering diagram; they form a basis of the ring of regular functions on the mirror X^∨. The multiplication of theta functions is governed by the scattering diagram and reproduces the cluster algebra structure on the coordinate ring of X^∨. In the case Y = ℙ2 and D a triangle of lines, the scattering diagram is the standard one for the Markov cluster algebra; the theta functions coincide with the cluster variables and the mirror X^∨ is the affine cubic surface {x y z = x + y + z + 1} in (ℂ^*)3. For a general toric log Calabi–Yau surface, the mirror is obtained by gluing tori along the walls of the scattering diagram, and the resulting variety carries a natural symplectic form whose periods are given by the theta functions. This construction proves the mirror symmetry conjecture for log Calabi–Yau surfaces and provides an explicit algorithm to compute the mirror map and the Yukawa coupling.

Log Canonical Thresholds, Multiplier Ideals, and the Bernstein–Sato Polynomial on Toric Varieties

The log canonical threshold lct(𝔞) of an ideal 𝔞 ⊂ O_X on a smooth toric variety X can be read off from the Newton polyhedron of 𝔞 with respect to the toric coordinates. Howald's theorem states that for a monomial ideal 𝔞 in the Cox ring, lct(𝔞) = minu∈ℝ^n>0 (⟨u,1⟩+1)/maxα∈𝔞 ⟨u,α⟩. Multiplier ideals J(𝔞^c) for rational c are also monomial and correspond to the set of lattice points m ∈ M such that ⟨u,m⟩ ≥ c·maxα∈𝔞 ⟨u,α⟩ - ⟨u,1⟩ for all u in the dual cone. The jumping numbers of the multiplier ideal filtration are precisely the rational numbers c where the set of such m changes; they are computed by the facets of the Newton polyhedron. The Bernstein–Sato polynomial b_𝔞(s) of a toric ideal is a product of linear factors (s + λ) where λ runs over the jumping numbers of the multiplier ideals of 𝔞. For a hyperplane arrangement defined by a toric divisor D = Σ D_i, the roots of b_D(s) are the negative rational numbers -lct(𝔞^J) for all subsets J of the components. In the case of the A‑hypergeometric system associated to a lattice configuration A, the b‑function of the toric ideal I_A coincides with the product of (s + β_i) where β_i are the roots of the indicial polynomial of the GKZ system. These results give a complete combinatorial description of singularities of toric varieties and their D‑module theoretic invariants.

Motivic Integration, Igusa Zeta Functions, and the Denef–Loeser Formula for Toric Varieties

Let X be an affine toric variety defined by a strongly convex rational polyhedral cone σ ⊂ N_ℝ. The arc space L(X) carries a natural motivic measure μ taking values in the Grothendieck ring of varieties K0(Var_k). The motivic Igusa zeta function Z_X(T) = ∫L(X) Tord_t f dμ, where f is a regular function on X, can be expressed purely in terms of the fan Σ of a resolution of singularities. Denef and Loeser proved that for a toric variety the motivic zeta function equals Στ∈Σ [E_τ^∘] (ℒ-1)dim τ ∏i∈τ (ℒ TN_i)/(1-ℒ TN_i) where E_τ are the exceptional divisors, N_i the multiplicities of the pullback of f, and ℒ the class of the affine line. Specializing ℒ → q-s gives the p‑adic Igusa zeta function Z_X(s) whose poles are at s = -ν_i/N_i with ν_i the log discrepancies. The monodromy conjecture for toric varieties follows from the fact that the poles of Z_X(s) correspond to eigenvalues of the monodromy action on the nearby cycles of f. For example, for the affine toric surface x y = z2 (the A1 singularity) the fan has two rays and one 2‑cone; the Denef–Loeser formula yields Z(T) = (ℒ-1)2 ℒ T2 / ((1-ℒ T)(1-ℒ T2)) + (ℒ-1) ℒ T / (1-ℒ T). The poles at T = ℒ-1 and T = ℒ-1/2 give the expected monodromy eigenvalues exp(2πi·1) and exp(2πi·1/2). This combinatorial approach makes the computation of Igusa zeta functions for any toric variety a routine exercise in fan manipulation.

  • Confusing the Stanley–Reisner ideal of the fan with the irrelevant ideal of the Cox ring — the former is generated by square‑free monomials corresponding to non‑faces of the simplicial complex, the latter by all monomials whose support is not contained in a cone.
  • Applying Hochster's formula without shifting the degree by the anticanonical class — the isomorphism H^*(X) ≅ H^*_B(S)[-dim X] requires a degree shift that is often omitted in hasty computations.
  • Misidentifying the contact order data in log GW theory — the tuple μ must be a collection of cone‑wise linear functions on the fan, not merely a list of integers.
  • Assuming the scattering diagram is finite — for non‑compact log Calabi–Yau surfaces the diagram typically has infinitely many walls; truncation at a given degree is necessary for explicit theta function calculations.
  • Using the wrong normal vector in Howald's lct formula — the inward primitive normal to a compact face of the Newton polyhedron must be used, not the outward normal.
  • Forgetting that multiplier ideals on toric varieties are monomial — their jumping numbers are determined solely by the facets of the Newton polyhedron, not by arbitrary ideals.
  • Overlooking the specialization ℒ → q-s when passing from motivic to p‑adic Igusa zeta functions — the poles in the motivic variable ℒ do not directly give the p‑adic poles without this substitution.

These advanced topics illustrate how combinatorial data of fans and polytopes control deep invariants across algebraic geometry, symplectic topology, and arithmetic geometry.

Toric Fano Varieties: Classification, Smoothness, and the Kleinschmidt Theorem

A toric variety X_Σ is Fano if and only if its fan Σ is the normal fan of a lattice polytope P ⊂ M_ℝ that is reflexive in the sense of Batyrev: P contains the origin in its strict interior and its polar dual P^* = {u ∈ N_ℝ | ⟨u, m⟩ ≥ -1 ∀ m ∈ P} is also a lattice polytope. Equivalently, the anticanonical divisor -K_X = Σ D_ρ is ample, which translates to the condition that the support function φ-K_X(u_ρ) = 1 for every ray generator u_ρ. The classification of smooth toric Fano varieties is a finite problem in each dimension. Kleinschmidt (1988) proved that for a smooth toric Fano variety of dimension n, the Picard number ρ(X) satisfies ρ(X) ≤ n + 1, with equality only for products of projective spaces. Batyrev (1999) classified all smooth toric Fano threefolds: there are exactly 18 deformation types, corresponding to 18 reflexive polytopes in dimension 3. In dimension 4, Øbro (2007) showed there are 124 smooth toric Fano fourfolds. The classification algorithm uses the fact that the primitive ray generators of a smooth Fano fan form the vertices of a reflexive polytope in N, and smoothness means every maximal cone is generated by a ℤ-basis of N. The Kleinschmidt bound is sharp: ℙ1 × ℙn-1 has ρ = n+1. For singular toric Fano varieties, the number of ℚ-factorial terminal Fano varieties is still finite in each dimension (Borisov–Borisov), but the count grows rapidly. The anticanonical polytope P-K_X = {m ∈ M_ℝ | ⟨m, u_ρ⟩ ≥ -1 ∀ ρ} is precisely the moment polytope for the anticanonical embedding, and its lattice points correspond to sections of -K_X. The Hilbert series of the anticanonical ring is encoded in the Ehrhart series of P-K_X. Mirror symmetry for Fano toric varieties relates the quantum cohomology of X_Σ to the Landau–Ginzburg model with superpotential W = Σ zu_ρ on the dual torus, where the critical points of W correspond to the eigenvalues of quantum multiplication by -K_X.

The GKZ Hypergeometric System and Toric Geometry

The Gel'fand–Kapranov–Zelevinsky (GKZ) hypergeometric system, also called the A-hypergeometric system, is a system of linear partial differential equations associated to an integer matrix A ∈ ℤd × n of rank d and a parameter vector β ∈ ℂ^d. The columns a1, …, a_n of A define a toric variety X_A as the closure of the image of the map (ℂ^*)^d → ℂ^n given by t ↦ (ta1, …, ta_n). The toric ideal I_A ⊂ ℂ[∂1, …, ∂_n] is generated by the binomials ∂^u - ∂^v for u, v ∈ ℕ^n with A u = A v. The GKZ system consists of the toric operators ∂^u - ∂^v for u, v with A u = A v, together with the Euler operators Σ_j aij z_j ∂_j - β_i for i = 1, …, d. The holonomic rank of this D-module equals the normalized volume of the convex hull of the columns of A when β is generic. For a projective toric variety X_Σ defined by a fan Σ, the matrix A can be taken as the ray generators u_ρ with an extra row of 1's, so that the GKZ system computes the periods of the mirror Landau–Ginzburg model. The solutions of the GKZ system are the A-hypergeometric functions Φ_γ(z) = Σu ∈ ℕ^n, A u = γ z^u / u! for γ ∈ ℂ^d. The monodromy of the GKZ system around the discriminant locus (the principal A-determinant) is described by the secondary fan of A. The connection to mirror symmetry is that the GKZ system for the anticanonical matrix A = [u_ρ | 1] is the Picard–Fuchs system for the mirror family of Calabi–Yau hypersurfaces in X_Σ. The indicial polynomial of the GKZ system at the large complex structure limit gives the roots that appear in the Gamma class and the Gamma conjecture. The b-function of the toric ideal I_A coincides with the product of (s + β_i) where β_i are the roots of the indicial polynomial, as noted in the Denef–Loeser formula. The GKZ system also controls the variation of Hodge structure on the mirror family and the quantum cohomology D-module of X_Σ via the mirror theorem of Givental and Lian–Liu–Yau.

Worked Example: GKZ System for ℙ2
Let X = ℙ2 with fan rays u1 = (1,0), u2 = (0,1), u3 = (-1,-1). Write the matrix A for the anticanonical GKZ system, the toric ideal I_A, and the Euler operators. Compute the holonomic rank for generic β.
The anticanonical matrix A has columns (u_ρ, 1): A = [[1, 0, -1], [0, 1, -1], [1, 1, 1]] ∈ ℤ3×3. The toric ideal I_A ⊂ ℂ[∂1, ∂2, ∂3] is generated by binomials ∂^u - ∂^v with A u = A v. The kernel of A over ℤ is spanned by (1,1,1), so I_A = ⟨∂1 ∂2 ∂3 - 1⟩? Wait: A(1,1,1)^T = (0,0,3)^T ≠ 0. Actually the relation is u1 + u2 + u3 = 0 in N, but with the extra row of 1's, the relation is (1,1,1) gives sum of first two rows 0, third row 3. The correct relation is (1,1,1) for the first two rows, but we need A u = 0. The matrix A has rank 3, so kernel is 0. The toric ideal is trivial? No: the toric ideal for the projective toric variety is in the Cox ring variables. For the GKZ system, A is d × n with d = dim X + 1 = 3, n = 3. The columns are a1 = (1,0,1), a2 = (0,1,1), a3 = (-1,-1,1). The lattice of relations L = {l ∈ ℤ3 | A l = 0}. Solve: l1 - l3 = 0, l2 - l3 = 0, l1 + l2 + l3 = 0 ⇒ l1 = l2 = l3 = 0. So L = 0, I_A = 0. The Euler operators: E1 = z1 ∂1 - z3 ∂3 - β1, E2 = z2 ∂2 - z3 ∂3 - β2, E3 = z1 ∂1 + z2 ∂2 + z3 ∂3 - β3. The holonomic rank is the normalized volume of conv(a1, a2, a3) in the affine hyperplane where the last coordinate is 1. The points are (1,0), (0,1), (-1,-1) in ℤ2. The triangle has area 3/2, normalized volume = 3. So rank = 3 for generic β.
Exam Strategy: GKZ and Mirror Symmetry
When asked about the GKZ system for a toric variety, first identify the matrix A: it is the ray generators with an extra row of 1's for the projective case. The toric ideal I_A is generated by binomials corresponding to the lattice of relations among columns of A. The holonomic rank equals the normalized volume of the polytope conv(columns of A) in the affine chart where the last coordinate is 1. For mirror symmetry, the GKZ system for the anticanonical A is the Picard–Fuchs system of the mirror Calabi–Yau. The indicial roots at the large complex structure limit are the exponents in the Gamma class. Memorize the ℙ2 example: A = 3×3, rank 3, volume 3.

Stringy Invariants of Toric Varieties: The Stringy E-Function and Orbifold Cohomology

For a toric variety X_Σ with at worst Gorenstein canonical singularities, Batyrev defined the stringy E-function E_st(X; u, v) as a generalization of the Hodge–Deligne polynomial that remains invariant under crepant resolutions. If Σ is a fan in N_ℝ and X_Σ is ℚ-Gorenstein with canonical singularities, the stringy E-function is E_st(X; u, v) = Σσ ∈ Σ (uv - 1)dim σ [E_σ^∘; u, v] where E_σ^∘ is the torus orbit corresponding to σ, and [E_σ^∘; u, v] is its Hodge–Deligne polynomial. For a toric variety, each orbit is a torus (ℂ^*)n - dim σ, whose Hodge–Deligne polynomial is (uv - 1)n - dim σ. Thus E_st(X; u, v) = Σσ ∈ Σ (uv - 1)^n. This is independent of the fan structure and depends only on the number of cones of each dimension. For a crepant resolution π: Y → X, the stringy E-function of X equals the ordinary Hodge–Deligne polynomial of Y: E_st(X; u, v) = E(Y; u, v). This is the motivic version of the crepant resolution conjecture. The stringy Hodge numbers hp,q_st(X) are the coefficients of u^p v^q in E_st(X; u, v). For a Gorenstein toric Fano variety, the stringy Hodge numbers satisfy hp,q_st = hn-p,n-q_st and the stringy Euler characteristic χ_st(X) = Σ (-1)p+q hp,q_st equals the number of maximal cones in any crepant resolution. The orbifold cohomology of a toric Deligne–Mumford stack X_Σ (with stacky fan Σ, where each ray ρ has a positive integer b_ρ) is H^*_orb(X_Σ) = ⊕v ∈ Box(Σ) H*-2 age(v)(Xσ(v)) where Box(Σ) = {v ∈ N | v = Σρ ∈ σ c_ρ u_ρ, 0 ≤ c_ρ < 1} is the set of box elements, and age(v) = Σ c_ρ. For a toric orbifold with trivial generic stabilizer, the orbifold Hodge numbers match the stringy Hodge numbers of the coarse moduli space. The Chen–Ruan orbifold cup product on H^*_orb is computed by the stacky fan: the obstruction bundle over the 3-multisectors is determined by the age grading. The crepant resolution conjecture of Ruan states that the orbifold quantum cohomology of X_Σ is isomorphic to the quantum cohomology of a crepant resolution Y after analytic continuation in the Kähler parameters and a suitable symplectic transformation. For toric orbifolds, this was proved by Coates–Corti–Iritani–Tseng using the mirror theorem and the GKZ system.

Toric Varieties and the Secondary Polytope: Triangulations and the GKZ Decomposition

The secondary polytope Σ(A) of a point configuration A = {a1, …, a_n} ⊂ ℤ^d is a convex polytope whose vertices correspond to regular triangulations of A, and whose faces correspond to regular subdivisions. It lives in the space ℝ^n / (row space of A + ℝ·1). The normal fan of the secondary polytope is the secondary fan, whose cones are the sets of weight vectors ω ∈ ℝ^n that induce the same regular subdivision. The GKZ hypergeometric system has a natural decomposition of its solution space indexed by the cones of the secondary fan: for each regular triangulation T, there is a basis of solutions given by the A-hypergeometric series Φ_γ^T(z) supported on the cones of T. The secondary polytope also controls the toric degenerations of the toric variety X_A: each vertex of Σ(A) corresponds to a flat toric degeneration of X_A to a union of toric varieties glued along toric strata, and the edges correspond to flips between triangulations. The volume of the secondary polytope is related to the discriminant of the toric variety. For a reflexive polytope P defining a Calabi–Yau hypersurface, the secondary polytope of the lattice points of P encodes the different phases of the gauged linear sigma model (GLSM) and the different large volume limits in the Kähler moduli space. The GKZ decomposition of the secondary fan gives the phase structure of the GLSM: each maximal cone corresponds to a geometric phase (a toric variety or a Landau–Ginzburg model), and the walls correspond to flops or transitions. The secondary polytope of the anticanonical matrix A = [u_ρ | 1] for a toric Fano variety X_Σ has vertices corresponding to the different crepant resolutions of the singular Fano variety defined by the same anticanonical polytope. The number of vertices equals the number of smooth toric Fano varieties with the same anticanonical polytope. The secondary fan is the fan of the GIT quotient of the Cox ring: the chambers are the GIT chambers for the action of the torus on the affine space Spec Cox(X). The secondary polytope also appears in the theory of tropical geometry: the tropicalization of the Grassmannian Gr(d, n) is the secondary fan of the uniform matroid, and the secondary polytope is the Newton polytope of the principal A-determinant.

Equivariant K-Theory of Toric Varieties and the Localization Theorem

The equivariant K-theory K_T0(X_Σ) of a toric variety X_Σ with respect to the torus action T = (ℂ^*)^n is a module over the representation ring R(T) = ℤ[M] = ℤ[t1^±, …, t_n^±]. The fixed points of the T-action are the torus-invariant points x_σ corresponding to the maximal cones σ ∈ Σ(n). The localization theorem in equivariant K-theory states that the restriction map K_T0(X_Σ) → ⊕σ ∈ Σ(n) K_T0(x_σ) = ⊕σ R(T) becomes an isomorphism after inverting the multiplicative set generated by (1 - t^m) for m ∈ M {0}. The class of a T-equivariant line bundle L_χ corresponding to a character χ ∈ M restricts at x_σ to the character χ evaluated on the fixed point, which is t^χ. More generally, the class of a T-equivariant vector bundle E restricts to a virtual representation at each fixed point. The equivariant K-theory ring K_T0(X_Σ) is generated by the classes of the torus-invariant divisors D_ρ, with relations given by the Stanley–Reisner ideal of the fan and the linear equivalences Σ ⟨m, u_ρ⟩ D_ρ = 0 for m ∈ M. The Todd class in equivariant K-theory is td_T(X_Σ) = Π_ρ (1 - [O(-D_ρ)]) / (1 - t-u_ρ [O(-D_ρ)]). The equivariant Riemann–Roch theorem computes the equivariant index of a vector bundle E as χ_T(X_Σ, E) = Σσ (E|x_σ · td_T(Tx_σ X_Σ)) / (Πρ ∈ σ (1 - t-u_ρ)). This formula is the K-theoretic analogue of the Atiyah–Bott localization formula in cohomology. The quantum K-theory of X_Σ, developed by Givental and Lee, is a deformation of the equivariant K-theory ring by the Novikov variables q^d, with the product defined by genus-zero K-theoretic Gromov–Witten invariants. The quantum K-theory ring satisfies a q-difference equation (the quantum K-theory D-module) which is the K-theoretic lift of the quantum differential equation. The mirror theorem in quantum K-theory identifies the quantum K-theory of X_Σ with the K-theoretic I-function, which is a solution of the q-difference GKZ system. The equivariant K-theory of toric varieties also controls the K-theoretic stable envelopes of Maulik–Okounkov, which give a basis of K_T0(X_Σ) indexed by the fixed points, with triangularity properties with respect to the attracting sets.

  • Confusing the stringy E-function with the ordinary Hodge–Deligne polynomial — the stringy version sums over all cones with weight (uv-1)dim σ, while the ordinary version requires a smooth resolution.
  • Misidentifying the matrix A for the GKZ system — for projective toric varieties, A must include the extra row of 1's; omitting it gives the affine cone GKZ system, not the projective one.
  • Assuming the secondary polytope vertices correspond to all triangulations — they correspond only to regular (coherent) triangulations; non-regular triangulations do not appear as vertices.
  • Forgetting that the localization theorem in K-theory requires inverting (1 - t^m) — the restriction map is not an isomorphism over R(T) itself, only after localization.
  • Mixing up the age grading in orbifold cohomology with the degree shifting in the stringy E-function — age(v) = Σ c_ρ for box elements, while the stringy E-function uses the cone dimension directly.
  • Overlooking that the Kleinschmidt bound ρ ≤ n+1 applies only to smooth toric Fano varieties — singular ℚ-factorial Fano varieties can have larger Picard number.
  • Confusing the secondary fan with the fan of the toric variety — the secondary fan lives in ℝ^n / (row space + ℝ·1) and parametrizes triangulations, while the toric fan lives in N_ℝ and parametrizes orbits.

Toric Varieties and Non‑Commutative Crepant Resolutions: Tilting Bundles and Derived Equivalences

A crepant resolution of a Gorenstein singularity is a resolution whose canonical divisor is the pullback of the canonical divisor on the singular space. For toric singularities, such resolutions exist and are themselves toric. When a crepant resolution does not exist or is not unique, one can often replace it by a non‑commutative crepant resolution (NCCR) in the sense of Van den Bergh: an endomorphism algebra Λ = EndR(M) of a reflexive R‑module M that has finite global dimension and satisfies a homological Calabi–Yau condition. For an affine toric variety X = Spec R defined by a cone σ ⊂ Nℝ, the module M can be taken as the direct sum of rank‑one reflexive modules corresponding to the lattice points in the fundamental parallelepiped of the dual cone σ∨. The resulting algebra Λ is Morita equivalent to the path algebra of a quiver with relations, the McKay quiver of the singularity. When X admits a crepant resolution Y → X, the derived category Db(Coh Y) is equivalent to Db(mod Λ) via a tilting bundle on Y. On a smooth toric variety Y, a tilting bundle is a vector bundle T such that Exti(T, T) = 0 for i > 0 and T generates Db(Coh Y). King’s construction shows that for a toric variety Y = XΣ with fan Σ, the sum of line bundles T = ⊕m ∈ P ∩ M OY(Dm) over the lattice points of a suitable polytope P gives a tilting bundle. The endomorphism algebra End(T) is then presented by a quiver whose vertices correspond to the summands and whose arrows correspond to sections of the line bundles O(Dm − Dm′). The relations come from the multiplication in the Cox ring. This quiver is precisely the toric quiver associated to the fan Σ. The derived equivalence Db(Coh Y) ≃ Db(mod End(T)) realises homological mirror symmetry at the categorical level: the Fukaya category of the mirror Landau–Ginzburg model is equivalent to the category of matrix factorisations of the superpotential, which in turn is equivalent to Db(mod Λ) for the NCCR Λ. For toric Calabi–Yau threefolds, the NCCR is unique up to Morita equivalence and its quiver is the dimer model on a torus, with the superpotential given by the sum of cycles. The consistency conditions for the dimer (cancellation of anomalies) translate into the Calabi–Yau condition on the quiver with potential. This circle of ideas connects the minimal model program (flops correspond to mutations of the tilting bundle), non‑commutative geometry (NCCRs as non‑commutative resolutions), and string theory (D‑branes on the singularity are modules over the NCCR).

Toric Varieties and the Theory of Valuations: The Valuative Tree, Tropicalization, and the Space of Valuations

The set of all valuations on the function field ℂ(XΣ) that are trivial on ℂ and centered on a toric variety XΣ carries a natural topology and a piecewise‑linear structure. A valuation v : ℂ(XΣ)× → ℝ ∪ {∞} is determined by its restriction to the character lattice M, giving a linear map v|M : M → ℝ, and by its values on the torus‑invariant prime divisors Dρ. The space of valuations ValX is homeomorphic to the inverse limit of the fans of all toric modifications of X. For a smooth toric surface, this space is the valuative tree of Favre and Jonsson, a dendrite whose ends correspond to divisorial valuations (given by exceptional divisors on blow‑ups) and whose interior points correspond to irrational valuations. The tropicalization map trop : XΣan → Nℝ from the Berkovich analytification sends a valuation to its restriction to M. Its image is the support of the fan Σ, and the fibres are tori of varying dimension. The tropical variety of a subvariety Z ⊂ XΣ is the closure of trop(Z ∩ T) in Nℝ; it is a rational polyhedral complex of dimension dim Z. The tropical compactification of a subvariety Z ⊂ T is the closure of Z in a toric variety XΣ whose fan Σ is a refinement of the tropical variety trop(Z). The theory of Newton–Okounkov bodies associates to a valuation v of maximal rank a convex body Δv(L) ⊂ ℝn for each big line bundle L. On a toric variety, the valuation given by the order of vanishing along a flag of torus‑invariant subvarieties yields the moment polytope of L. More generally, any valuation v with rational rank n gives a Newton–Okounkov body that is a rational polytope, and the map v ↦ Δv(L) is continuous on the space of valuations. The volume of a valuation vol(v) = limk→∞ (n! / kn) dim ℂ{ f ∈ ℂ(X) : v(f) ≥ k } is a homogeneous function on ValX that computes the log canonical threshold of the valuation. The minimizer of the normalized volume function vol(v) / AX(v) (where AX is the log discrepancy) is a central object in K‑stability; for toric varieties it is always a divisorial valuation corresponding to a ray in the fan. The valuative perspective unifies the study of singularities (log canonical thresholds), degenerations (tropical compactifications), and the geometry of the space of all toric models of a given variety.

Worked Example: Valuation on ℙ² and its Newton–Okounkov Body
Let X = ℙ² with homogeneous coordinates [x:y:z]. Consider the flag Y₁ = {z=0} ≅ ℙ¹, Y₂ = {z=0, y=0} = [1:0:0]. The associated valuation v on ℂ(x,y) = ℂ(ℙ²) is v(f) = (ordY₁(f), ordY₂(f|Y₁)) ∈ ℤ² with lexicographic order. Compute the Newton–Okounkov body Δv(O(1)).
The line bundle O(1) has sections spanned by x, y, z. Their valuations: v(x/z) = (0,0), v(y/z) = (0,1), v(1) = (0,0) but 1 is not a section of O(1) on the affine chart z≠0. On the chart z≠0, sections are a + bx + cy with a,b,c ∈ ℂ. The valuation v(a + bx + cy) = (0,0) if a≠0; if a=0, b≠0 then v = (0,0); if a=b=0, c≠0 then v = (0,1). The value semigroup is generated by (0,0) and (0,1). The Newton–Okounkov body is the convex hull of (0,0) and (0,1) in ℝ², i.e., the segment [0,1] on the second axis. This matches the moment polytope of O(1) on ℙ² projected along the flag direction.
Exam Strategy: Valuations and Tropicalization
When a question asks for the tropicalization of a subvariety in a toric variety, first write the subvariety by equations in the torus coordinates. The tropical variety is the corner locus of the tropical polynomial (the piecewise‑linear function obtained by replacing + with min and × with +). For a hypersurface {f=0}, trop(V(f)) is the (n−1)-skeleton of the Newton polytope subdivision induced by the coefficients. Remember that the tropicalization map is continuous and proper on the Berkovich space; the fibre over a point in the relative interior of a cone σ is a torus of dimension dim σ. For valuation questions, distinguish between divisorial valuations (finite rank, correspond to exceptional divisors) and irrational valuations (infinite rank, correspond to ends of the valuative tree). The log discrepancy AX(v) for a toric valuation v(u) = ⟨w, u⟩ with w ∈ σ∨ is AX(v) = ⟨w, ∑ uρ⟩ + 1.

Toric Varieties and Mirror Symmetry for Open Strings: Lagrangian Floer Theory, Disk Potentials, and the SYZ Conjecture

The Strominger–Yau–Zaslow (SYZ) conjecture proposes that mirror symmetry is a geometric duality between special Lagrangian torus fibrations. For a toric Calabi–Yau manifold X, the moment map μ : X → ℝn provides a Lagrangian torus fibration over the moment polytope P, with singular fibres over the boundary. The mirror Y is constructed by dualising the torus fibres, yielding a Landau–Ginzburg model (Y, W) where Y is a torus bundle over the interior of P and W is a superpotential. Open string mirror symmetry relates the Fukaya category of X (with objects Lagrangian branes) to the category of matrix factorisations of W on Y. The disk potential (or open Gromov–Witten potential) for a Lagrangian torus fibre Lu over u ∈ Int(P) is a generating function of counts of holomorphic disks with boundary on Lu. For toric Calabi–Yau manifolds, Cho and Oh showed that the only holomorphic disks are the basic disks intersecting a single toric divisor Dρ once. The disk potential is Wu(y) = ∑ρ exp(−⟨u, uρ⟩) yuρ, where y ∈ (ℂ×)n are coordinates on the mirror torus. The critical points of Wu correspond to Lagrangian fibres that bound no non‑constant holomorphic disks, i.e., to objects of the Fukaya category that are unobstructed. The Floer cohomology HF(Lu, Lu) is isomorphic to the Jacobian ring ℂ[y±] / (∂W/∂yi) when u is a critical point. The SYZ transform sends a Lagrangian brane (L, ∇) to a matrix factorisation of W on the mirror. For a toric Fano variety X, the mirror is a Landau–Ginzburg model ((ℂ×)n, W) with W = ∑ρ zuρ. The Hori–Vafa mirror is recovered by taking the critical locus of W. The Gross–Siebert program constructs the mirror by degenerating X to a union of toric varieties and using tropical geometry to glue the dual torus bundles. The scattering diagram on the base of the fibration encodes the wall‑crossing of the superpotential; its consistency is equivalent to the associativity of the Fukaya category. The open‑closed map sends the disk potential to the closed Gromov–Witten potential via the divisor equation. For toric Calabi–Yau threefolds, the disk potential computes the generating function of open BPS invariants, which are integers counting holomorphic disks with boundary on a Lagrangian brane. These invariants satisfy the Kontsevich–Soibelman wall‑crossing formula as the Lagrangian brane moves across walls in the base.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of D‑Modules: Holonomic GKZ Systems, Irregularity, and the Riemann–Hilbert Correspondence

The Gel'fand–Kapranov–Zelevinsky (GKZ) hypergeometric system is a system of linear partial differential equations on the space of parameters β ∈ ℂd and variables x ∈ ℂn associated to an integer matrix A ∈ ℤd×n. For a projective toric variety XΣ defined by a fan Σ with ray generators uρ ∈ N, the matrix A is the (d+1)×n matrix whose columns are (uρ, 1). The GKZ system MA(β) is a holonomic D‑module on the torus (ℂ×)n (or on the affine space ℂn for the affine cone). Its solutions are the A‑hypergeometric functions Φγ(x) = ∑l ∈ ℒ xγ+l / Γ(γ+l+1) where ℒ = kerℤ A and γ satisfies Aγ = β. The Riemann–Hilbert correspondence identifies regular holonomic D‑modules with perverse sheaves. For the GKZ system, the corresponding perverse sheaf is the vanishing cycle complex of the family of hypersurfaces defined by the Laurent polynomial ∑ aρ zuρ. The irregularity of the GKZ system along the coordinate hyperplanes measures the failure of regular singularities; it is computed by the volumes of the faces of the Newton polytope. The Hodge filtration on the GKZ D‑module corresponds under the Riemann–Hilbert correspondence to the weight filtration on the vanishing cycle complex. The Gauss–Manin connection on the cohomology of the fibres of the Laurent polynomial gives a regular holonomic D‑module isomorphic to the GKZ system for generic β. The monodromy of the GKZ system is the monodromy of the vanishing cycles, which is quasi‑unipotent. The b‑function (Bernstein–Sato polynomial) of the discriminant of the GKZ system controls the poles of the meromorphic continuation of the hypergeometric integrals. For toric varieties, the D‑module of differential operators on the Cox ring gives a presentation of the quantum D‑module of XΣ. The quantum differential equation is a connection on the cohomology bundle over the Kähler moduli space; its flat sections are the J‑function. The mirror theorem identifies this quantum D‑module with the GKZ system on the mirror side. The irregular Hodge theory of Sabbah and Yu extends the Hodge theory to irregular singular D‑modules; for the GKZ system, the irregular Hodge numbers are the Hodge numbers of the toric variety. The Fourier–Laplace transform of the GKZ system gives the D‑module of the mirror Landau–Ginzburg model, realising the mirror symmetry at the level of D‑modules.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of Cluster Varieties: A‑Varieties, X‑Varieties, and the Fock–Goncharov Ensemble

Cluster algebras, introduced by Fomin and Zelevinsky, have a geometric realisation as rings of regular functions on cluster varieties. A cluster variety comes in two flavours: the A‑variety (cluster variety of type A) and the X‑variety (cluster variety of type X). For a toric variety XΣ, the Cox ring Cox(X) is a cluster algebra if and only if X is a cluster variety of finite type or a cluster variety of infinite type with a toric model. The Fock–Goncharov construction associates to a quiver Q with a skew‑symmetric exchange matrix B a pair of cluster varieties AQ and XQ with a map p : AQ → XQ. The A‑variety is a union of tori (ℂ×)n glued by cluster mutations; its ring of regular functions is the upper cluster algebra. The X‑variety is a union of tori glued by tropicalised mutations; it carries a canonical Poisson structure. For a toric variety XΣ, the fan Σ can be encoded by a quiver whose vertices are the rays of Σ and whose arrows are given by the intersection pairing of the corresponding divisors. The cluster structure on the Cox ring is given by the Gale dual of the fan: the exchange matrix B is the matrix of the pairing between the divisor classes and the Mori cone generators. The cluster variables correspond to the toric divisors and their mutations correspond to flops of the toric variety. The Fock–Goncharov duality exchanges the A‑variety of Q with the X‑variety of the dual quiver Q∨. In the toric setting, this duality is the Batyrev–Borisov mirror symmetry: the A‑variety of the fan Σ is the mirror of the X‑variety of the dual fan Σ∨. The theta functions of Gross–Hacking–Keel–Kontsevich provide a canonical basis of the cluster algebra, indexed by the tropical points of the X‑variety. For a toric variety, these theta functions are the characters of the torus, and the scattering diagram is the fan itself. The cluster Donaldson–Thomas invariants of the quiver Q are the BPS invariants of the associated 3d Calabi–Yau category; for toric Calabi–Yau threefolds they coincide with the open Gromov–Witten invariants. The quantum cluster algebra deforms the cluster algebra by a quantum parameter q; its representation theory is controlled by the quantum torus algebra. The cluster modular group (the group of mutations modulo the automorphisms of the quiver) acts on the cluster variety and on the space of stability conditions. For toric varieties, this group is the toric mapping class group generated by flops and automorphisms of the fan.

  • Confusing the NCCR endomorphism algebra with the Cox ring — the NCCR is EndR(M) for a specific reflexive module M, while the Cox ring is the total coordinate ring; they coincide only for smooth varieties where M = R.
  • Assuming every valuation on a toric variety is monomial — only valuations that are trivial on the torus and centered on the toric boundary are monomial; general valuations can have non‑trivial restriction to the character lattice.
  • Mixing up the disk potential for a Lagrangian torus fibre with the closed Gromov–Witten potential — the disk potential is a function on the mirror torus (open string), while the closed potential is a function on the Kähler moduli space (closed string); they are related by the open‑closed map.
  • Thinking the GKZ system is always regular holonomic — it is regular holonomic only for generic β; for resonant β it has irregular singularities along the coordinate hyperplanes.
  • Identifying the A‑variety with the toric variety itself — the A‑variety is a cluster variety that may be a proper open subset of the toric variety (the complement of the frozen divisors); the full toric variety is obtained by adding the frozen variables.
  • Overlooking that the scattering diagram for a toric variety is just the fan — the walls are the codimension‑one cones, and the wall‑crossing automorphisms are trivial because the fan is already consistent; non‑trivial scattering appears only for non‑toric degenerations.
  • Confusing the Fock–Goncharov X‑variety with the mirror torus — the X‑variety is a cluster variety that compactifies the mirror torus; its tropical points parametrise the theta functions, which for toric varieties are just the characters.

The Chow Ring and Cohomology of Toric Varieties: Structure and Generators

The Chow ring, denoted A*(X), is a fundamental invariant in algebraic geometry that captures the intersection theory of algebraic cycles on a variety X. For a smooth projective toric variety XΣ, its Chow ring is particularly well-behaved and can be explicitly described in terms of its fan Σ. Furthermore, for smooth projective varieties over the complex numbers, the Chow ring is isomorphic to the singular cohomology ring H*(X, ℂ) as a graded ring, making it a powerful tool for studying topological invariants.

The Chow ring A*(XΣ) is generated by the classes of the prime toric divisors. A prime toric divisor Dρ corresponds to each one-dimensional cone (ray) ρ in the fan Σ. These divisors are irreducible, T-invariant subvarieties of codimension one. The ring is graded by codimension, so Ak(XΣ) consists of classes of cycles of codimension k. The product in the Chow ring corresponds to the intersection product of cycles.

The structure of the Chow ring is given by a quotient of a polynomial ring. Let N be the lattice of one-parameter subgroups and M be the dual lattice of characters. For each ray ρ ∈ Σ(1), let uρ be its primitive generator in N. The Chow ring A*(XΣ) is isomorphic to the quotient of the polynomial ring ℂ[Dρ | ρ ∈ Σ(1)] by an ideal generated by two types of relations:

  1. Linear Relations: For each character m ∈ M, the sum Σρ∈Σ(1) <m, uρ> Dρ is linearly equivalent to zero. These relations arise from the fact that any T-invariant principal divisor on XΣ can be expressed in this form. Geometrically, these relations encode the linear equivalences among the toric divisors.
  2. Stanley–Reisner Relations: For any collection of rays {ρ1, ..., ρk} that do not form a cone in the fan Σ, the product Dρ1Dρ2...Dρk is zero in A*(XΣ). These relations capture the combinatorial structure of the fan, ensuring that divisors corresponding to rays that do not share a common cone have an empty intersection. This ideal is precisely the Stanley–Reisner ideal of the fan, which defines the combinatorial structure of the associated simplicial complex.

Thus, A*(XΣ) ≅ ℂ[Dρ | ρ ∈ Σ(1)] / (Ilinear + IStanley-Reisner). The degree of the toric variety, for instance, can be computed as the self-intersection number of the anticanonical divisor, which is an element in A1(XΣ). The coefficients of the Hilbert polynomial of a projective toric variety are also encoded in the Chow ring.

Toric Singularities: Classification and Resolution by Fan Subdivision

While smooth toric varieties are often preferred for theoretical simplicity, many toric varieties arising in applications are singular. Understanding these singularities and their resolution is a crucial aspect of toric geometry. Toric singularities are particularly tractable because they are determined by the local structure of the fan around the singular point, and they can always be resolved by a process of fan subdivision.

A toric variety XΣ is smooth if and only if every cone σ ∈ Σ is generated by a subset of a basis for the lattice N. If this condition is not met for some cone, then the corresponding affine toric variety Uσ (and thus XΣ) is singular at the fixed point of the torus action within Uσ.

Toric singularities can be classified based on properties related to the canonical divisor and the Minimal Model Program:

  • Rational Singularities: A singularity is rational if its higher direct images of the structure sheaf under a resolution are trivial. For toric varieties, XΣ has rational singularities if and only if for every cone σ ∈ Σ, the origin is the only lattice point in the relative interior of σ. This implies that the resolution of singularities has rational fibers.
  • Canonical Singularities: A singularity is canonical if it is rational and the canonical divisor KX is Q-Cartier and KX + Δ is a Q-Cartier divisor for some effective divisor Δ. In toric terms, this corresponds to a specific condition on the lattice points in the cones, related to the anticanonical divisor.
  • Terminal Singularities: A stronger condition than canonical. These are crucial for the Minimal Model Program. For toric varieties, XΣ has terminal singularities if for every cone σ ∈ Σ, the origin is the only lattice point in the interior of σ.
  • Gorenstein Singularities: A singularity is Gorenstein if the canonical divisor KX is Cartier (i.e., locally principal). For toric varieties, this means there exists a character m ∈ M such that <m, uρ> = 1 for all primitive generators uρ of rays ρ. This implies that the anticanonical divisor is a sum of toric divisors.

Resolution of Singularities by Fan Subdivision: All toric singularities are rational and can be resolved by a sequence of blow-ups. In the language of fans, a blow-up corresponds to a subdivision of the fan. A subdivision Σ' of Σ is a fan such that every cone in Σ' is contained in a cone in Σ, and every cone in Σ is a union of cones in Σ'. A subdivision Σ' corresponds to a proper birational map XΣ' → XΣ. If Σ' is a simplicial (all cones are simplicial) and regular (all primitive generators of rays of a cone form part of a basis) subdivision, then XΣ' is a smooth toric variety.

The general algorithm for resolving toric singularities involves:

  1. Identifying all singular cones σ ∈ Σ, i.e., those whose primitive generators do not form part of a basis for N.
  2. For each singular cone σ, adding new rays to the fan. These new rays correspond to lattice points in the interior of the convex hull of the primitive generators of σ.
  3. Subdividing σ into smaller, smooth cones using these new rays. A common technique is a stellar subdivision, where a new ray is added through a lattice point in the interior of a cone, and the cone is then subdivided into smaller cones using this new ray and the original rays.
  4. Repeating this process until all cones in the refined fan Σ' are smooth. This procedure always terminates and yields a smooth resolution XΣ'.

For example, the affine variety ℂ²/Z₂ (an A₁ singularity), corresponding to the cone generated by (1,0) and (0,1) in N, but with the lattice N' = Z(2,0) + Z(0,2) or similar, is singular. To resolve it, one adds a new ray through the lattice point (1,1) and subdivides the original cone into two smaller cones, each generated by a basis of N. This corresponds to blowing up the singular point at the origin.

Technique: Identifying and Resolving Toric Singularities
Consider the toric variety XΣ associated with the fan Σ in N = Z² consisting of two cones: σ₁ = cone((1,0), (0,1)) and σ₂ = cone((0,1), (-1,-1)). Assume the primitive generators are u₁=(1,0), u₂=(0,1), u₃=(-1,-1). Determine if XΣ is smooth. If not, describe a resolution of its singularities by subdividing the fan.

Step 1: Analyze the cones for smoothness.

A toric variety is smooth if and only if every cone in its fan is generated by a subset of a basis for the lattice N. Here N = Z².

  • For σ₁ = cone(u₁, u₂): The primitive generators are u₁=(1,0) and u₂=(0,1). These form a basis for Z². Thus, σ₁ is a smooth cone. The corresponding affine chart Uσ₁ is isomorphic to ℂ².
  • For σ₂ = cone(u₂, u₃): The primitive generators are u₂=(0,1) and u₃=(-1,-1). The determinant of the matrix formed by these vectors is det([[0, -1], [1, -1]]) = 0×(-1) - (-1)×1 = 1. Since the determinant is ±1, u₂ and u₃ also form a basis for Z². Thus, σ₂ is a smooth cone. The corresponding affine chart Uσ₂ is isomorphic to ℂ².

Since all cones in Σ are smooth, the toric variety XΣ itself is smooth. Therefore, no resolution is needed.

Self-correction/Extension: Let's consider a slightly different fan to illustrate a singular case. Suppose we have a fan with a single cone σ = cone((2,0), (0,2)) in N = Z². The primitive generators are u₁=(2,0) and u₂=(0,2). These do not form a basis for Z² (det([[2,0],[0,2]]) = 4 ≠ ±1). This cone is singular, and the corresponding affine variety is ℂ²/Z₂ (the A₁ singularity). To resolve this, we would add a new ray. A common choice is u₃=(1,1). We then subdivide σ into σ'₁ = cone((2,0), (1,1)) and σ'₂ = cone((1,1), (0,2)). Now, check these new cones:

  • For σ'₁ = cone((2,0), (1,1)): The primitive generators are (2,0) and (1,1). det([[2,1],[0,1]]) = 2. Still not a basis. This is still singular.
  • For σ'₂ = cone((1,1), (0,2)): The primitive generators are (1,1) and (0,2). det([[1,0],[1,2]]) = 2. Still not a basis. This is still singular.

This shows that simply adding a ray through (1,1) is not enough to make the *original* generators part of a basis. The resolution process requires finding *new* primitive generators that form a basis for *each new cone*. For the cone σ = cone((2,0), (0,2)), the correct resolution involves adding rays corresponding to (1,0) and (0,1). The fan becomes Σ' = cone((2,0), (1,0), (0,1), (0,2)). The cones would be cone((2,0),(1,0)), cone((1,0),(0,1)), cone((0,1),(0,2)). This is not a fan. The correct subdivision for Xσ = ℂ²/Z₂ is to subdivide the cone generated by (2,0) and (0,2) by adding the rays (1,0) and (0,1). The new fan would have rays (1,0), (0,1), and the cones would be cone((1,0), (0,1)). This corresponds to the standard resolution of ℂ²/Z₂ to ℂ² by blowing up the origin.

Let's use a simpler singular example: Xσ where σ is the cone generated by u₁=(1,0) and u₂=(1,2) in Z². The determinant of [[1,1],[0,2]] is 2, so it's singular. To resolve, we add a ray u₃=(0,1). Now we have cones σ'₁ = cone(u₁, u₃) = cone((1,0), (0,1)) and σ'₂ = cone(u₃, u₂) = cone((0,1), (1,2)).

  • σ'₁: (1,0), (0,1) form a basis. Smooth.
  • σ'₂: (0,1), (1,2). det([[0,1],[1,2]]) = -1. Form a basis. Smooth.

Thus, adding the ray (0,1) resolves the singularity.

Exam Day Strategy: Visualizing Toric Geometry
When dealing with toric varieties, especially in lower dimensions (2 or 3), always try to sketch the fan. The fan is the combinatorial backbone of the variety, and many properties (smoothness, singularities, projective embeddings, resolutions) can be directly read from its structure. For instance, to check for smoothness, visually inspect if the primitive generators of each cone form a basis for the lattice. For resolutions, imagine adding new rays to "thin out" the cones until they become smooth. This visual intuition is invaluable for quickly grasping complex concepts and identifying potential pitfalls.

Toric Fibrations and the Geometry of Families

A toric fibration represents a natural generalization of a single toric variety to a family of toric varieties parameterized by a base space. This concept is crucial for constructing more complex algebraic varieties, especially in contexts like Calabi-Yau geometry and string theory, where families of varieties play a significant role. A toric fibration is a surjective morphism f: X → Y where X and Y are algebraic varieties, and the general fiber f⁻¹(y) for y in some dense open subset of Y is a toric variety. Often, the base Y itself is a toric variety, leading to "toric over toric" fibrations.

The construction of a toric fibration often involves a relationship between the lattice N of the total space X and a sublattice N' corresponding to the fibers. If N' is a sublattice of N, then the quotient lattice N/N' can be identified with the lattice of the base. The fan of the total space XΣ is then related to the fan of the base YΔ and the fans of the fibers. Specifically, for a fibration X → Y, the fan Σ of X is often a "cone over" the fan Δ of Y, with the additional data of the fiber fans.

A particularly important class of toric fibrations are elliptic fibrations, where the general fiber is an elliptic curve (a smooth projective curve of genus one). In the context of toric geometry, these are often compactifications of 1-dimensional algebraic tori. Elliptic fibrations are extensively used in the construction of Calabi-Yau manifolds, such as K3 surfaces (which can be elliptic fibrations over P¹) and Calabi-Yau threefolds.

The behavior of the fibers in a toric fibration is not uniform across the base. Over certain loci in the base Y, the fibers can degenerate. These degenerations are characterized by specific types of singularities in the fiber, often classified by Kodaira types for elliptic fibrations. The structure of these degenerations is directly encoded in the local combinatorial data of the fan of the total space X over the corresponding points in the base Y. For instance, the type of singular elliptic fiber (e.g., nodal, cuspidal, or more complex types like In, II, III, IV, In*, IV*, III*, II*) can be determined by the arrangement of rays and cones in the fan of X near the singular locus in Y.

Applications of toric fibrations are diverse:

  • Construction of Calabi-Yau Manifolds: Many Calabi-Yau manifolds, which are central to string theory, are realized as toric fibrations. The ability to construct and analyze these complex manifolds using combinatorial data from fans is a major strength of toric geometry.
  • Mirror Symmetry: The concept of mirror symmetry, which relates two different Calabi-Yau manifolds, extends naturally to fibrations. The mirror of a Calabi-Yau fibration is often another Calabi-Yau fibration, with the roles of base and fiber potentially interchanged or transformed.
  • Compactifications of Moduli Spaces: Toric fibrations frequently appear as boundary components in compactifications of moduli spaces of other geometric objects, such as curves or abelian varieties. This provides a concrete geometric interpretation for the "boundary" of these moduli spaces.
  • Logarithmic Geometry: The degenerations observed in toric fibrations are naturally described within the framework of logarithmic geometry. Here, the base variety Y is equipped with a log structure that encodes the information about the singular locus, providing a refined understanding of the family.

The study of toric fibrations allows for a systematic approach to understanding families of varieties, leveraging the combinatorial power of fans to analyze their geometric and topological properties, including their singularities and deformations.

Deformations of Toric Varieties and Their Moduli Spaces

Deformation theory investigates how geometric objects can vary in families. For toric varieties, this leads to insights into their rigidity, the structure of their moduli spaces, and profound connections to mirror symmetry. While the concept of a "moduli space of toric varieties" might seem straightforward, the rigidity properties of smooth toric varieties introduce nuances.

Rigidity of Smooth Toric Varieties: Smooth projective toric varieties exhibit a strong form of rigidity. They admit no non-trivial deformations as toric varieties. This means that any deformation of a smooth projective toric variety that preserves its toric structure (i.e., the torus action) must itself be isomorphic to the original variety. This rigidity stems from the fact that the automorphism group of a smooth projective toric variety is finite, and its infinitesimal deformations are trivial. Consequently, the "moduli space of smooth projective toric varieties" is essentially a discrete set of points, each representing an isomorphism class of a fan.

However, this rigidity does not extend to singular toric varieties. Singularities can often be smoothed out in families, meaning a singular toric variety can deform into a smooth one or into other singular varieties. For instance, a cone in the fan that is not generated by a basis of the lattice can be deformed into a family of cones that are smooth in the generic fiber.

Moduli Spaces of Toric Varieties and Related Objects:

  • Moduli of Toric Pairs (X, D): More interesting moduli spaces arise when considering pairs (X, D) where X is a toric variety and D is a specific divisor (e.g., the anticanonical divisor or a boundary divisor). These pairs often have non-trivial deformations.
  • Moduli of Toric Fano Varieties: Fano varieties are varieties with ample anticanonical divisor. While the moduli spaces of general Fano varieties are complex, those for toric Fano varieties are better understood due to their combinatorial nature. These moduli spaces parameterize different fan structures that yield Fano varieties.
  • Toric Degenerations as Deformations: A toric degeneration, where a variety X degenerates into a simpler, often singular, variety X₀ (which is typically a union of toric varieties), can be viewed as a deformation over a curve. The central fiber is X₀, and the generic fiber is X. This perspective is crucial in understanding the geometry of compactifications of moduli spaces.
  • Universal Deformations: For certain classes of singular toric varieties (e.g., those with canonical singularities), there exists a universal deformation space that captures all possible deformations. This space provides a local parameterization of the family of varieties around the singular point.

Connection to Mirror Symmetry: Deformations play a central role in mirror symmetry. For a Calabi-Yau toric variety, deformations of its complex structure correspond to deformations of the complexified Kähler structure on its mirror. The complex structure moduli space of a Calabi-Yau toric hypersurface is often described by the GKZ hypergeometric system, which governs the periods of the mirror family. Conversely, the Kähler moduli space of a toric variety is intimately related to its fan and the cone of ample divisors. The Hori-Vafa mirror symmetry construction for Fano toric varieties provides a concrete link, where the Landau-Ginzburg potential describes the complex structure deformations on the mirror side, and its critical points relate to the Gromov-Witten invariants of the original Fano variety.

Furthermore, toric varieties often appear as boundary components in compactifications of moduli spaces of other geometric objects, such as the moduli space of stable curves. Understanding the deformations of these boundary components, which are often themselves toric varieties, is essential for a complete picture of the compactified moduli space.

Common Traps

  • Confusing Chow Ring with Cox Ring: While both are related to the combinatorial structure of the fan, the Chow ring (A*(X)) describes intersection theory and is generated by divisor classes modulo rational equivalence, whereas the Cox ring (R(X)) is the total coordinate ring, a polynomial ring whose quotient by an ideal defines the variety itself. They are distinct algebraic objects with different purposes.
  • Assuming all toric varieties are smooth: Many fundamental results apply to smooth toric varieties, but a significant portion of the theory deals with singular ones. Ignoring singularities can lead to incorrect conclusions, especially when discussing resolutions or specific classifications.
  • Overlooking the two types of relations in the Chow Ring: The Chow ring for a smooth toric variety is defined by both linear relations (from characters) and Stanley-Reisner relations (from non-cones). Omitting either set of relations will result in an incorrect ring structure.
  • Misinterpreting "toric fibration": A toric fibration is not simply a map where the total space and base are toric. The key is that the *fibers* are toric varieties, and the map is equivariant with respect to a torus action.
  • Believing all smooth projective toric varieties deform: Smooth projective toric varieties are rigid as toric varieties. Their fan structure is fixed under deformation. Deformations are typically considered for singular toric varieties or for toric varieties as part of a larger class of varieties (e.g., a toric Calabi-Yau as a general Calabi-Yau).
  • Incorrectly applying resolution by fan subdivision: Simply adding a ray through any lattice point might not result in a smooth subdivision. The new cones must also satisfy the smoothness condition (their primitive generators must form a basis for the lattice).
  • Mixing up complex structure and Kähler moduli spaces in mirror symmetry: For Calabi-Yau toric varieties, deformations of the complex structure on one side correspond to deformations of the Kähler structure on the mirror side. It's crucial to keep these distinct moduli spaces separate.

Toric Varieties and the Abelian/Non-Abelian Correspondence

The abelian/non-abelian correspondence, developed by Martin, Ciocan-Fontanine, Kim, and Sabbah, provides a powerful method to compute Gromov–Witten invariants and quantum cohomology of GIT quotients by non-abelian groups in terms of abelian quotients. For a toric variety X = V//G where G is a reductive group (e.g., GL(r)), the correspondence relates the quantum cohomology of X to that of an associated abelian quotient X_ab = V//T, where T ⊂ G is a maximal torus. The key mechanism is the quantum Martin formula, which expresses the J-function of X as a sum over the Weyl group W of G of the J-function of X_ab twisted by the equivariant Euler class of the bundle associated to the adjoint representation. Concretely, if X = Gr(r, n) is the Grassmannian, then X_ab = (Pn-1)^r // (C^*)r-1 is a toric variety, and the quantum cohomology of Gr(r, n) is recovered from the toric quantum cohomology of X_ab by applying the Weyl group symmetrization and a correction factor involving the roots of G. This correspondence has been extended to the level of D-modules and the quantum differential equation, proving that the Givental connection for X is the W-invariant part of the connection for X_ab. The abelian/non-abelian correspondence is a cornerstone of modern enumerative geometry, allowing the computation of genus-zero Gromov–Witten invariants of flag varieties, Grassmannians, and other homogeneous spaces using the well-developed machinery of toric mirror symmetry.

Toric Varieties and Ehrhart Theory: Lattice Point Enumeration and Polytopes

Ehrhart theory provides a deep combinatorial bridge between the geometry of a lattice polytope P ⊂ M_R and the algebraic geometry of the associated projective toric variety X_P. For a lattice polytope P, the Ehrhart polynomial L_P(k) = |kP ∩ M| counts the number of lattice points in the k-th dilate of P. Ehrhart's theorem states that L_P(k) is a polynomial in k of degree dim P, with leading coefficient vol(P) (the normalized volume), second coefficient (1/2) vol(∂P), and constant term 1. The Ehrhart series ∑k≥0 L_P(k) t^k = h^*(t) / (1-t)d+1 has a numerator h^*(t) = ∑i=0^d h_i^* t^i with non-negative integer coefficients h_i^* (the h*-vector). For a smooth projective toric variety X_P, the h*-vector coincides with the h-vector of the boundary complex of the fan, and the Ehrhart polynomial evaluates the Hilbert polynomial of the anticanonical embedding: χ(X_P, O(kD)) = L_P(k) for k ≥ 0, where D is the ample divisor corresponding to P. The Ehrhart–Macdonald reciprocity L_P(-k) = (-1)^d LP°(k) relates lattice points in the interior P° to the dual polynomial. This reciprocity is a combinatorial shadow of Serre duality on X_P. The Stanley's non-negativity theorem for the h*-vector of a lattice polytope is equivalent to the nefness of the anticanonical divisor on the associated toric variety. Furthermore, the Ehrhart ring R(P) = ⊕k≥0 (kP ∩ M) t^k is precisely the Cox ring of X_P graded by the divisor class group, and its Hilbert series is the Ehrhart series. The study of Ehrhart positivity (when h_i^* > 0) connects to the existence of full strong exceptional collections on X_P and to the geometry of the secondary fan of P.

Worked Example: Ehrhart Polynomial of the Standard Simplex
Let P = Δ_d = conv{0, e1, ..., e_d} ⊂ R^d be the standard d-simplex. Compute the Ehrhart polynomial L_P(k) and verify Ehrhart–Macdonald reciprocity.
The lattice points in kΔ_d are the integer solutions to x1 + ... + x_d ≤ k with x_i ≥ 0. This is a stars-and-bars count: L_P(k) = C(k+d, d) = (k+d)(k+d-1)...(k+1)/d!. This is a polynomial of degree d with leading coefficient 1/d! = vol(Δ_d). The Ehrhart series is ∑k≥0 C(k+d, d) t^k = 1/(1-t)d+1, so h^*(t) = 1 and h_i^* = 0 for i > 0. The interior lattice points of kΔ_d are solutions to x1 + ... + x_d < k with x_i ≥ 1, i.e., y1 + ... + y_d ≤ k-d with y_i ≥ 0. Thus LP°(k) = C(k-1, d) for k ≥ d, and 0 otherwise. Ehrhart–Macdonald reciprocity: L_P(-k) = C(-k+d, d) = (-1)^d C(k-1, d) = (-1)^d LP°(k), verified.
Exam Strategy: Ehrhart and Toric Geometry
When a question links lattice point enumeration to toric varieties, identify the polytope P and the corresponding divisor D. The Hilbert polynomial of O(kD) is the Ehrhart polynomial L_P(k). The h*-vector of P gives the Betti numbers of X_P if X_P is smooth and projective (by Danilov–Jurkiewicz). Remember: Ehrhart reciprocity L_P(-k) = (-1)^d LP°(k) is the combinatorial avatar of Serre duality. For a reflexive polytope, the h*-vector is palindromic, reflecting the Calabi–Yau condition.

Toric Varieties and Sparse Resultants: The GKZ Discriminant and Resultant

The theory of sparse resultants and discriminants, pioneered by Gelfand, Kapranov, and Zelevinsky (GKZ), generalizes classical elimination theory to systems of Laurent polynomials with prescribed Newton polytopes. Given a configuration A = {a1, ..., a_n} ⊂ Z^d of lattice points, consider the space of Laurent polynomials f_i = ∑a∈A ci,a x^a. The sparse resultant Res_A(c) is an irreducible polynomial in the coefficients ci,a that vanishes iff the system f1 = ... = fd+1 = 0 has a solution in the algebraic torus (C^*)^d. The Newton polytope of Res_A is the secondary polytope Σ(A), whose vertices correspond to regular triangulations of A. The GKZ discriminant ∇_A is the resultant of the system of partial derivatives; its vanishing characterizes when the hypersurface {f=0} ⊂ X_A is singular. The GKZ hypergeometric system H_A(β) governs the periods of the family of hypersurfaces and the A-hypergeometric functions. The principal A-determinant E_A is a product of the sparse resultant and discriminants of faces, encoding the full singularity structure. For a toric variety X_Σ associated to a fan Σ refining the normal fan of conv(A), the Cox ring is the polynomial ring in variables corresponding to A, and the GKZ system describes the quantum differential equation of X_Σ. The secondary fan of A is the normal fan of Σ(A); its chambers correspond to different GIT quotients (phases) of the Cox construction, i.e., different toric varieties birational to X_Σ. The GKZ discriminant locus in the coefficient space is the complement of the union of these chambers. This theory provides the combinatorial foundation for the Batyrev–Borisov mirror construction and for the computation of Gromov–Witten invariants via the mirror theorem.

Toric Varieties and Frobenius Splitting: F-Singularities in Positive Characteristic

In positive characteristic p > 0, the Frobenius morphism F: X → X induces a map on structure sheaves F^#: O_X → F_* O_X. A variety X is Frobenius split if this map splits as a map of O_X-modules. For a toric variety X_Σ over a field of characteristic p, Frobenius splitting is a combinatorial condition on the fan Σ. Mehta and Ramanathan proved that a projective toric variety is Frobenius split iff the anticanonical divisor -K_X is basepoint-free, which for toric varieties is equivalent to the polytope P being reflexive (i.e., the origin is the unique interior lattice point of P). More generally, a toric variety is compatibly Frobenius split with respect to all torus-invariant subvarieties iff it is smooth. The theory of F-singularities (F-regular, F-pure, F-rational) has precise combinatorial characterizations for toric varieties. A toric variety is F-regular iff it is Q-Gorenstein with index not divisible by p and the polytope P is Fano (i.e., the origin is in the strict interior of P). It is F-pure iff the origin is in the interior of P (i.e., -K_X is nef). The F-signature and Hilbert–Kunz multiplicity of toric singularities are computable from the fan. For a cone σ, the Hilbert–Kunz multiplicity e_HK(R_σ) equals the normalized volume of the fundamental parallelepiped of σ. The test ideal τ(X) of a toric variety is the ideal generated by monomials x^m where m lies in the interior of the polytope associated to -K_X. These results connect the minimal model program in positive characteristic (where F-regularity corresponds to klt singularities) to the combinatorics of lattice polytopes. The Frobenius splitting of the diagonal in X × X is equivalent to X being F-regular, and for toric varieties this is controlled by the splitting prime of the fan.

Toric Varieties and Arithmetic Intersection Theory: Arakelov Geometry on Toric Varieties

Arakelov geometry extends intersection theory to arithmetic varieties over Spec Z by adding metrics at the archimedean place. For a toric variety X_Σ over a number field K, the arithmetic Chow groups ĈH^*(X_Σ) incorporate Green currents for torus-invariant divisors. The arithmetic intersection product of two torus-invariant divisors D1, D2 with smooth metrics is computed by the arithmetic intersection formula of Burgos Gil, Philippon, and Sombra: ⟨D1, D2⟩ = ∑v ∫X_van g1 c1(D2) + ∑p (D1 · D2)_p log N(p), where the sum runs over all places v of K, g_i are Green functions, and (·)_p is the intersection multiplicity at the prime p. For toric varieties, the arithmetic volume of an adelic metrized line bundle L̄ is given by the arithmetic Hilbert–Samuel formula ĥ0(X, L̄) = vol(L̄) + o(1), where vol(L̄) is the integral of the Monge–Ampère measure of the metric over the Berkovich analytification. The equidistribution theorem of Yuan and Zhang states that small points (points of height tending to zero) on a toric variety equidistribute with respect to the canonical measure on the Berkovich space. The arithmetic intersection theory of toric varieties is completely combinatorial: the arithmetic Chow ring is generated by arithmetic divisor classes [D_ρ, g_ρ] with relations coming from the fan and the metrics. The arithmetic Hilbert polynomial of a metrized line bundle on a toric variety is expressed in terms of the arithmetic Ehrhart polynomial of the associated polytope with a metric. This theory underpins the proof of the Bogomolov conjecture for toric varieties and the computation of Faltings heights of toric varieties. The arithmetic mirror symmetry program of Burgos Gil, Burgos, and others relates the arithmetic intersection theory of a Calabi–Yau toric hypersurface to the variation of Hodge structure on its mirror.

  • Confusing the abelian/non-abelian correspondence with the abelianization of the quantum cohomology: The correspondence is a precise equality of J-functions after Weyl symmetrization and twisting, not merely an isomorphism of rings.
  • Assuming Ehrhart positivity holds for all lattice polytopes: The h*-vector can have zeros; positivity is equivalent to the nefness of the anticanonical divisor, which fails for many polytopes.
  • Mixing up the sparse resultant and the GKZ discriminant: The resultant vanishes when a system has a solution in the torus; the discriminant vanishes when a single hypersurface is singular. They are different polynomials with different Newton polytopes.
  • Believing Frobenius splitting is automatic for all toric varieties in characteristic p: It requires -K_X to be basepoint-free (reflexive polytope). Many toric Fano varieties are not Frobenius split if p divides the index.
  • Overlooking the role of the secondary fan in the GKZ discriminant: The chambers of the secondary fan correspond to different toric models (phases); the discriminant locus is the union of walls between chambers.
  • Treating arithmetic intersection theory on toric varieties as identical to the complex case: The archimedean and non-archimedean metrics contribute additional terms (Green functions, local heights) that have no analogue in the purely complex setting.
  • Assuming the arithmetic Hilbert–Samuel formula holds without the metrized line bundle being semipositive: The formula requires the metric to be semipositive (plurisubharmonic) at archimedean places and the model to be normal at non-archimedean places.

Toric Varieties and Spherical Embeddings: Colored Fans and Luna-Vust Theory

Toric varieties are the simplest examples of spherical varieties — normal algebraic varieties with an action of a reductive group G such that a Borel subgroup B ⊂ G has a dense orbit. When G = T is a torus, the spherical variety is exactly a toric variety. The Luna–Vust theory classifies all spherical embeddings of a given homogeneous spherical space G/H by combinatorial data called colored fans. A colored fan generalizes the fan of a toric variety: it consists of a fan in the valuation cone V ⊂ Λ ⊗ ℚ (where Λ is the weight lattice of B-eigenfunctions on G/H) together with a set of colors — the B-stable prime divisors of G/H. Each colored cone (C, F) pairs a strictly convex cone C ⊂ V with a subset F of colors not meeting the interior of C. The colored fan axioms require that faces of colored cones are colored cones and that the colored cones cover the valuation cone. For toric varieties, H = {1}, the valuation cone is the whole lattice N ⊗ ℚ, there are no colors, and the colored fan reduces to an ordinary fan in N ⊗ ℚ. The Luna–Vust correspondence extends the orbit–cone correspondence: G-orbits in the spherical embedding correspond bijectively to colored cones, with orbit closures described by face relations. The local structure theorem of Brion and Luna states that any spherical variety is étale-locally isomorphic to a product of a toric variety and a homogeneous spherical space of rank zero. This reduces many questions on spherical varieties to toric geometry. The colored fan also controls the Picard group, the Cox ring, and the Mori cone: the divisor class group is generated by the colors and the G-invariant divisors corresponding to rays of the fan, with relations coming from the exact sequence 0 → Λ → ⊕colors ℤ ⊕ ⊕rays ℤ → Cl(X) → 0. The anticanonical divisor is −KX = ∑colors D + ∑rays aρ Dρ where the coefficients aρ are determined by the valuation of the canonical section. Fano spherical varieties correspond to colored fans whose support is the whole valuation cone and whose anticanonical divisor is ample — a condition checked by linear inequalities on the colored fan data. The classification of Fano spherical varieties of rank ≤ 2 (by Pasquier, Perrin, and others) uses this combinatorial framework. Spherical embeddings also provide a natural setting for the abelian/non-abelian correspondence in quantum cohomology: the quantum cohomology of a spherical variety X is related to that of its toric degeneration (the toric variety associated to the colored fan with all colors removed) by a Weyl-group symmetrization procedure.

Toric Arrangements and Wonderful Compactifications: The De Concini–Procesi Construction

A toric arrangement is a finite collection of hypersurfaces in an algebraic torus T ≅ (ℂ×)n of the form {χm = a} where χm is a character and a ∈ ℂ×. Unlike hyperplane arrangements (which live in vector spaces), toric arrangements live in the torus and their combinatorics is governed by the intersection poset of the layers (connected components of intersections of hypersurfaces). Each layer is a coset of a subtorus, hence isomorphic to a torus of lower dimension. The wonderful compactification of De Concini and Procesi (1995) constructs a smooth projective variety Y containing the complement U = T ∪i Hi as a dense open subset, such that the boundary Y U is a simple normal crossing divisor whose irreducible components correspond to the building set of the arrangement — a collection of layers closed under taking intersections and satisfying a transversality condition. The construction proceeds by iterated blow-ups of T along the closures of the layers in the building set, ordered by increasing dimension. The resulting variety Y is a toric variety if and only if the arrangement is a subtorus arrangement (all a = 1); for general toric arrangements, Y is a toric variety over a torus — a variety with a torus action of complexity zero but not necessarily a toric variety in the strict sense. The cohomology ring H•(Y, ℤ) has a presentation by generators and relations analogous to the Stanley–Reisner ring of a fan: generators correspond to boundary divisors, and relations encode the nested set complex of the building set. The Poincaré polynomial of Y equals the characteristic polynomial of the arrangement evaluated at −1, generalizing the Orlik–Solomon formula for hyperplane arrangements. The moduli space of toric arrangements with fixed intersection poset is a toric variety itself, and its compactification by the wonderful model provides a geometric interpretation of the Feichtner–Yuzvinsky presentation of the cohomology ring. Wonderful compactifications also appear in the tropicalization of toric arrangements: the tropical fan of the complement U is the Bergman fan of the matroid associated to the arrangement, and the wonderful compactification provides a toric variety whose fan is a subdivision of this Bergman fan. This connects to the theory of logarithmic Gromov–Witten invariants of toric arrangements, where the boundary divisors of the wonderful compactification serve as the target for relative stable maps.

Worked Example: Colored Fan of SL₂/ℂ×
Let G = SL₂(ℂ) and H = ℂ× embedded diagonally. The homogeneous space G/H is spherical of rank 1. Determine its valuation cone, colors, and classify all simple spherical embeddings (those with a unique closed G-orbit).
Step 1: Weight lattice and valuation cone. The Borel subgroup B of upper-triangular matrices acts on G/H with an open orbit. The weight lattice Λ of B-eigenfunctions is ℤ (generated by the character diag(t, t−1) ↦ t2). The valuation cone V ⊂ Λ ⊗ ℚ ≅ ℚ is the non-negative ray ℚ≥0 (since G/H is affine spherical, the valuation cone is strictly convex). Step 2: Colors. The B-stable prime divisors in G/H are the two Schubert divisors D+ and D− corresponding to the two simple roots. Their images in Λ ⊗ ℚ are the two rays ℚ≥0 and ℚ≤0 (but only the positive ray lies in V). Thus there is exactly one color D whose valuation lies in V. Step 3: Colored cones for simple embeddings. A simple embedding corresponds to a single colored cone (C, F) where C is a face of V and F ⊂ {D} with D ∉ F if D meets the interior of C. The faces of V are {0} and V itself.
  • If C = {0}, then F can be ∅ or {D}. F = ∅ gives the toric variety ℙ¹ (the unique closed orbit is a point). F = {D} gives the homogeneous space G/H itself (no boundary).
  • If C = V = ℚ≥0, then D meets the interior of C, so F must be ∅. This gives the spherical embedding ℙ² with the standard SL₂-action (the unique closed orbit is ℙ¹).
Thus there are exactly three simple spherical embeddings of SL₂/ℂ×: the homogeneous space, ℙ¹, and ℙ².
Exam Strategy: Spherical vs Toric Data
When a problem asks for the colored fan of a spherical variety, first compute the weight lattice Λ and the valuation cone V (use the Knop's algorithm or the Brion's theorem on the moment polytope). Then list the colors by finding B-stable divisors in G/H. The colored fan is a collection of colored cones covering V. For toric varieties, colors are absent and the colored fan is an ordinary fan.
