Remember: the number of colors equals the number of B-stable divisors in G/H, which is the number of simple roots not in the Levi part of H. In rank 1, there is at most one color in V.

Moduli of Toric Vector Bundles: Stability, GIT, and Quiver Representations

Klyachko's classification describes a toric vector bundle E on a smooth toric variety XΣ by a collection of filtrations Eρ(i) of a fixed vector space E for each ray ρ ∈ Σ(1), indexed by integers i ∈ ℤ, satisfying compatibility conditions at cones. The moduli space of toric vector bundles with fixed Chern classes is constructed via Geometric Invariant Theory (GIT) on the space of filtrations. The key insight (due to Payne, Kool, and others) is that a toric vector bundle is equivalent to a representation of the Klyachko quiver — a quiver whose vertices correspond to the rays of Σ and whose arrows encode the inclusion relations between filtrations at adjacent rays. The dimension vector of the representation is the rank of the bundle at each ray, and the stability condition for the bundle (slope stability with respect to an ample divisor H) translates to King stability for the quiver representation with a weight vector determined by H. The moduli space MΣ(r, c1, c2, H) of H-stable toric vector bundles of rank r and Chern classes c1, c2 is then the GIT quotient of the representation space of the Klyachko quiver by the product of general linear groups at each vertex. This moduli space is itself a toric variety when the stability condition is generic (no strictly semistable objects), and its fan is the secondary fan of a certain polytope (the Klyachko polytope) whose lattice points correspond to filtrations. The wall-crossing as H varies in the ample cone is described by the Thaddeus flips between different GIT quotients, corresponding to birational transformations of the moduli space. For X = ℙ², the moduli space of rank-2 toric bundles with c1 = 0, c2 = k is isomorphic to the Hilbert scheme of k points on ℂ² (the ADHM construction), and the Klyachko quiver is the Jordan quiver with a framing. The Cox ring of the moduli space is generated by the tautological bundles, and its relations are the Plücker relations coming from the quiver. The equivariant cohomology of the moduli space is computed by the localization formula with respect to the torus action, yielding integrals over fixed points that are products of Nekrasov partition functions. This connects to Donaldson–Thomas theory on toric Calabi–Yau threefolds: the moduli space of toric sheaves on a toric Calabi–Yau threefold is a toric variety whose DT invariants are computed by the topological vertex.

Toric Varieties and the Batyrev–Manin Conjecture: Universal Torsors, Peyre's Constant, and the Circle Method

The Batyrev–Manin conjecture predicts the asymptotic distribution of rational points of bounded height on a Fano variety X over a number field K. For toric Fano varieties, the conjecture is a theorem (proved by Batyrev–Tschinkel, Salberger, and others) and the proof reveals a rich interplay between toric geometry and analytic number theory. Let X = XΣ be a smooth projective toric Fano variety over ℚ with fan Σ in N ⊗ ℚ. The anticanonical divisor −KX is ample and corresponds to a lattice polytope P ⊂ M ⊗ ℚ. The height zeta function Z(s) = ∑x∈X(ℚ) H(x)−s (where H is the anticanonical height) admits a meromorphic continuation to Re(s) > dim X − δ with a pole of order r = rank Pic(X) at s = 1. The leading coefficient is Peyre's constant cPeyre(X) = α(X) β(X) τ(X), where α(X) is the volume of the effective cone in the Néron–Severi space, β(X) = |H¹(Gal(K̄/K), Pic(X̄))| (trivial for split toric varieties), and τ(X) is the Tamagawa number of the universal torsor. The universal torsor of a toric variety is the open torus T itself, viewed as a torsor under the Néron–Severi torus TNS = Hom(Pic(X), ℂ×). The height integral on X lifts to an integral over the universal torsor, which factorizes as a product of local integrals: τ(X) = ∏v ∫T(Kv) ωv where ωv is the canonical measure. For toric varieties, these local integrals are volumes of polytopes: at the archimedean place, ∫T(ℝ) ω∞ = vol(P); at a finite place p, ∫T(ℚp) ωp = (1 − p−1)−rank Pic · #X(𝔽p). The circle method (Hardy–Littlewood) applied to the universal torsor gives an alternative proof: the number of rational points of height ≤ B is asymptotic to cPeyre B (log B)r−1 times a product of local densities. The Manin's conjecture for singular toric varieties (e.g., weighted projective spaces) requires the minimal model program to resolve singularities and the peeling of accumulating subvarieties (the Batyrev–Manin stratification). The thin set of exceptions is controlled by the Campana points on the universal torsor. For toric varieties over function fields, the conjecture is related to the geometric Manin conjecture and the motivic height zeta function of Chambert-Loir and Tschinkel.

Toric Degenerations of Schubert Varieties: String Polytopes, Newton–Okounkov Bodies, and the Gelfand–Tsetlin Integrable System

Schubert varieties Xw in the flag variety G/B admit flat degenerations to toric varieties, providing a bridge between representation theory and toric geometry. The string polytope of Berenstein–Zelevinsky (for a fixed reduced word i of the longest element w0) is a lattice polytope whose lattice points parametrize the canonical basis of the irreducible representation V(λ). The toric variety Xi associated to the normal fan of the string polytope is a flat degeneration of the Schubert variety Xw (for w corresponding to i). The Newton–Okounkov body Δ(Xw, v) with respect to a valuation v coming from a flag of subvarieties in Xw coincides with the string polytope when the flag is chosen compatibly with the Bott–Samelson resolution. The Gelfand–Tsetlin integrable system on the flag variety (or on the coadjoint orbit Oλ) provides a completely integrable Hamiltonian system whose moment polytope is the Gelfand–Tsetlin polytope GT(λ). The toric variety associated to the normal fan of GT(λ) is the Gelfand–Tsetlin toric variety, which is a flat degeneration of the flag variety (or the projective embedding of the coadjoint orbit). The tropicalization of the Schubert variety Xw in the flag variety is the tropical Schubert variety, a polyhedral complex whose maximal cones correspond to the vertices of the string polytope. The cluster algebra structure on the coordinate ring of the flag variety (or the double Bruhat cell) provides a family of toric degenerations indexed by clusters; each cluster gives a seed whose exchange matrix defines a quiver, and the associated toric variety is the spectrum of the initial algebra of the cluster algebra. The mirror symmetry for Schubert varieties is encoded in the Landau–Ginzburg potential on the mirror toric variety, whose critical points correspond to the quantum cohomology relations. The quantum Schubert calculus (structure constants of the quantum cohomology of G/B) is computed by the tropical curve counting on the mirror toric variety, generalizing the Rietsch–Williams mirror for the Grassmannian. The K-theoretic version uses the K-theoretic Newton–Okounkov bodies and the quantum K-theory of the flag variety.

  • Confusing spherical embeddings with toric varieties: A spherical variety has a colored fan with colors; a toric variety has no colors. The valuation cone for a spherical variety is a proper subset of the weight lattice, while for toric varieties it is the whole lattice.
  • Assuming all wonderful compactifications are toric: The wonderful compactification of a toric arrangement is toric only if the arrangement is a subtorus arrangement (all equations χm = 1). General toric arrangements yield varieties with torus action of complexity zero but not toric varieties.
  • Mixing up Klyachko's filtrations with quiver representations: Klyachko's data is a collection of filtrations; the quiver representation is a reformulation where the filtration steps become vector spaces at vertices. The stability condition for the bundle (slope stability) translates to King stability with a specific weight vector — not all King weights come from ample divisors.
  • Overlooking the role of the universal torsor in Manin's conjecture: The universal torsor of a toric variety is the torus itself, not a higher-dimensional variety. The height integral factorizes over the universal torsor, and Peyre's constant is a product of local densities. For non-toric Fano varieties, the universal torsor is a non-trivial torsor under the Néron–Severi torus.
  • Believing all toric degenerations of Schubert varieties come from string polytopes: String polytopes depend on a choice of reduced word. Different reduced words give different string polytopes (related by tropical Plücker relations). The Newton–Okounkov body depends on a valuation; different flags give different polytopes. The Gelfand–Tsetlin polytope is a special case for the full flag variety.
  • Ignoring the difference between the Batyrev–Manin conjecture for split vs non-split toric varieties: For split toric varieties, the Galois cohomology term β(X) = 1. For non-split toric varieties (where the fan is defined over ℚ but the torus action is not split), β(X) can be non-trivial and affects the leading constant.
  • Treating the secondary fan of the Klyachko polytope as the same as the secondary fan of the toric variety: The secondary fan of the Klyachko polytope parametrizes stability conditions for toric vector bundles (wall-crossing in the moduli space). The secondary fan of the toric variety XΣ parametrizes triangulations of the polytope of an ample divisor (wall-crossing in the Kähler moduli space). They live in different spaces.

Toric Varieties and the Topological Vertex: Gromov-Witten Theory, the Remodeling Conjecture, and the B-Model

The topological vertex is a combinatorial algorithm that computes the all-genus Gromov-Witten invariants of toric Calabi-Yau threefolds. For a toric Calabi-Yau threefold X_Σ, the fan Σ is a triangulation of a convex lattice polygon in the plane (the toric diagram). The topological vertex assigns to each trivalent vertex of the toric diagram a weight Cλμν(q) depending on three partitions λ, μ, ν and the string coupling q = ei g_s. The partition function Ztop(X_Σ) = ∑λ (-Q)|λ| Cλμν(q) ... is a sum over partitions labeling the internal edges of the toric diagram, with Q = e-t the Kähler parameter. The vertex weight is given by Cλμν(q) = qκ(λ)/2 + κ(μ)/2 + κ(ν)/2 sλ^t(q-ρ) sμ(q-ρ) sν(q-ρ) where s_λ are Schur functions, q-ρ = (q1/2, q3/2, ...), and κ(λ) = ∑_i λ_i(λ_i - 2i + 1). The remodeling conjecture (Bouchard-Klemm-Mariño-Pasquetti) states that the B-model topological string partition function on the mirror curve Σmirror = {H(x,y)=0} ⊂ (C^*)2, computed via the Eynard-Orantin topological recursion, equals the A-model partition function Ztop(X_Σ) after the mirror map. The mirror curve is the zero locus of the Hori-Vafa superpotential W = x + y + ∑ a_i xm_i yn_i. The topological recursion produces multi-differentials Wg,n(z1,...,z_n) on Σmirror^n, and the free energies F_g = ∫ Wg,1 satisfy the holomorphic anomaly equation ∂t̄ F_g = 1/2 Ct̄tt (D_t D_t Fg-1 + ∑r=1g-1 D_t F_r D_t Fg-r). For toric Calabi-Yau threefolds, the holomorphic anomaly equation can be solved recursively using the gap condition at the conifold point and the regularity at the large radius point. The topological vertex also computes the open Gromov-Witten invariants with Lagrangian branes on toric cycles; the open partition function is a generating function of HOMFLY polynomials of knots via the Ooguri-Vafa correspondence. The refined topological vertex (Iqbal-Kozcaz-Vafa) introduces a second parameter t = q^β and computes the refined BPS invariants Ωj_L,j_R which are the dimensions of the cohomology of the moduli space of D-branes. The Nekrasov partition function of the 5d N=1 gauge theory on R4 × S1 with Ω-background (ε1, ε2) is identified with the refined topological string partition function on the toric Calabi-Yau threefold engineering the gauge theory (the geometric engineering correspondence). The AGT correspondence further relates the refined topological vertex to the conformal blocks of the Virasoro algebra on the Riemann sphere.

Toric Varieties and Tropical Homology: The Hodge Theory of Tropical Varieties and the Tropical Hodge Numbers

Tropical homology, introduced by Itenberg-Katzarkov-Mikhalkin-Zharkov, provides a tropical analogue of singular homology and Hodge theory for tropical varieties. For a tropical variety Trop(X) ⊂ R^n of dimension d, the tropical homology groups H_kp(Trop(X)) are defined using the chain complex of tropical cycles with coefficients in the sheaf of tropical (p,p)-forms. A tropical (p,p)-form on a polyhedral complex is a collection of alternating p-forms on each p-dimensional cell, satisfying balancing conditions at the boundaries. The tropical Hodge numbers hp,qtrop(X) = dim H_qp(Trop(X)) satisfy the symmetry hp,qtrop = hq,ptrop and the Hodge decomposition H_k(Trop(X), Z) ⊗ Q = ⊕p+q=k H_qp(Trop(X)) ⊗ Q for smooth tropical varieties. For a smooth projective toric variety X_Σ, the tropical variety Trop(X_Σ) is the fan Σ itself, and the tropical homology groups are isomorphic to the usual homology groups of X_Σ: H_kp(Σ) ≅ H2p-k(X_Σ, Q). The tropical Hodge numbers recover the classical Hodge numbers: hp,qtrop(X_Σ) = hp,q(X_Σ). For a hypersurface X_f ⊂ X_Σ defined by a Laurent polynomial f, the tropical hypersurface Trop(X_f) is a tropical cycle of codimension 1 in Σ. The tropical homology of Trop(X_f) computes the primitive Hodge numbers of X_f via the tropical Lefschetz hyperplane theorem: the map H_kp(Σ) → H_kp(Trop(X_f)) induced by inclusion is an isomorphism for k < d-1 and injective for k = d-1. The tropical Hodge numbers of a tropical Calabi-Yau hypersurface satisfy hp,qtrop = hd-p,d-qtrop and hp,0trop = 0 for 0 < p < d. The tropical Dolbeault complex computes the tropical cohomology groups H^qtrop(Trop(X), Ω^ptrop) which are dual to H_qp(Trop(X)). The tropical Jacobian of a tropical curve Γ is the real torus H1(Γ, R)/H1(Γ, Z) with the tropical theta divisor. The tropical Abel-Jacobi map embeds Γ into its Jacobian. For higher-dimensional tropical varieties, the tropical intermediate Jacobians are defined using the tropical Hodge filtration. The tropical Hodge numbers of a tropical variety can be computed from the face poset of the associated polyhedral complex using the formula hp,qtrop = ∑F (-1)dim F - p dim Gr^p_F Hq(link(F)) where the sum is over faces F of the polyhedral complex and link(F) is the link of F. The tropical Hodge numbers of a tropical complete intersection are given by the tropical Bernstein-Kushnirenko-Khovanskii formula. The tropical Hodge theory provides a combinatorial framework for studying degenerations of algebraic varieties: if X_t → X0 is a toric degeneration, then the tropical homology of Trop(X0) computes the limit mixed Hodge structure on the cohomology of the general fiber X_t.

Worked Example: Tropical Hodge Numbers of a Tropical Elliptic Curve
Let Γ be a tropical elliptic curve in R2, i.e., a trivalent graph of genus 1 with 3 unbounded edges in directions (1,0), (0,1), (-1,-1) satisfying the balancing condition. Compute the tropical Hodge numbers hp,qtrop(Γ) for p,q ∈ {0,1}.
A tropical elliptic curve Γ is a metric graph of genus 1. Its tropical homology groups are: H00(Γ) = Z (connected components), H10(Γ) = 0 (no 1-cycles with 0-forms), H01(Γ) = 0 (no 0-cycles with 1-forms), H11(Γ) = Z (the unique 1-cycle with the canonical 1-form). The tropical Hodge numbers are h0,0trop = 1, h1,0trop = 0, h0,1trop = 0, h1,1trop = 1. These match the classical Hodge numbers of an elliptic curve: h0,0=1, h1,0=1, h0,1=1, h1,1=1. Wait: the tropical Hodge numbers hp,qtrop are defined as dim H_q^p, so h1,0trop = dim H01 = 0, h0,1trop = dim H10 = 0. The classical Hodge numbers are hp,q = dim H^q(X, Ω^p). For an elliptic curve, h1,0 = h0,1 = 1. The tropical Hodge numbers satisfy hp,qtrop = hq,ptrop but do NOT match the classical Hodge numbers directly; rather, the tropical homology of the tropicalization of a degeneration computes the limit mixed Hodge structure. For a smooth tropical curve of genus g, h0,0trop=1, h1,0trop=0, h0,1trop=0, h1,1trop=g. The classical Hodge numbers are recovered from the tropical homology of the SNC model of the degeneration, not from the tropical curve alone.
Exam Strategy: Tropical vs Classical Hodge Numbers
Do not confuse tropical Hodge numbers hp,qtrop = dim H_q^p(Trop(X)) with classical Hodge numbers hp,q(X) = dim H^q(X, Ω^p). For a smooth tropical curve of genus g, h1,1trop = g while h1,0(X) = g. The tropical Hodge diamond is rotated: the tropical (p,q) corresponds to classical (p, d-q) in the limit mixed Hodge structure. In exams, if asked for tropical Hodge numbers of a tropical curve, compute the ranks of H00, H10, H01, H11 from the graph structure. For a trivalent graph of genus g with b unbounded edges, H11 has rank g, H00 has rank 1, others are 0.

Toric Varieties and Bridgeland Stability Conditions: The Space of Stability Conditions on the Derived Category of a Toric Variety

Bridgeland stability conditions on a triangulated category D provide a geometric framework for studying wall-crossing phenomena in Donaldson-Thomas theory and mirror symmetry. For a smooth projective toric variety X_Σ, the bounded derived category of coherent sheaves D^b(Coh(X_Σ)) admits a full exceptional collection of line bundles O(D1), ..., O(D_n) where D_i are torus-invariant divisors. The space of stability conditions Stab(D^b(Coh(X_Σ))) is a complex manifold of dimension equal to the rank of the numerical Grothendieck group Knum(X_Σ) = Zρ+2 where ρ = rank Pic(X_Σ). The central charge Z: Knum(X_Σ) → C is determined by its values on the exceptional collection. For toric varieties, the space of stability conditions can be described using the toric geometry of the fan Σ. The heart of a stability condition is a tilt of the standard heart Coh(X_Σ) with respect to a torsion pair (T, F) defined by a slope function μ_ω(E) = (c1(E)·ωn-1)/rank(E) for an ample class ω. The space of stability conditions contains a distinguished component Stab^†(X_Σ) containing geometric stability conditions (those whose heart is a tilt of Coh(X_Σ)). The autoequivalence group Autoeq(D^b(Coh(X_Σ))) acts on Stab^†(X_Σ). For toric varieties, this group is generated by spherical twists around spherical objects (e.g., structure sheaves of toric subvarieties), tensor products with line bundles, and shifts. The wall-and-chamber structure of Stab^†(X_Σ) corresponds to the wall-crossing of Bridgeland semistable objects. The walls are real codimension-1 submanifolds where the phase of two objects aligns. The chamber structure is related to the Mori chamber decomposition of the ample cone: each chamber corresponds to a different GIT quotient of the quiver variety associated to the exceptional collection. The mirror symmetry conjecture for toric varieties (Kontsevich, Seidel, Auroux-Katzarkov-Orlov) predicts that Stab^†(X_Σ) is isomorphic to the space of stability conditions on the Fukaya category of the mirror Landau-Ginzburg model (Y, W). The central charge on the A-side is given by the period integrals Z(γ) = ∫_γ Ω where Ω is the holomorphic volume form on the mirror Calabi-Yau. The monodromy of the periods around the large complex structure limit corresponds to the autoequivalence given by the spherical twist around the structure sheaf of a point. The space of stability conditions on the derived category of a toric Calabi-Yau threefold is related to the space of scattering diagrams on the tropical base via the work of Bridgeland-Smith and Haiden-Katzarkov-Kontsevich. The stability conditions on the derived category of a toric stack (e.g., a toric orbifold) are described by the GIT stability conditions on the Cox ring with respect to the stacky fan.

Toric Varieties and the Wall-Crossing Formula for Donaldson-Thomas Invariants: The Kontsevich-Soibelman Wall-Crossing and the Motivic DT Invariants

The Donaldson-Thomas invariants DT(γ) ∈ Z count ideal sheaves (or stable pairs) on a Calabi-Yau threefold X with numerical class γ ∈ Knum(X). For toric Calabi-Yau threefolds, the DT invariants can be computed combinatorially via the topological vertex or via the non-commutative Donaldson-Thomas theory of the associated quiver with potential. The wall-crossing formula describes how DT invariants change as the stability condition varies across a wall of marginal stability. The Kontsevich-Soibelman wall-crossing formula states that the product of quantum dilogarithms Ψ(q^γ)DT(γ) over all active classes γ, ordered by the phase of the central charge Z(γ), is invariant under wall-crossing. Here Ψ(x) = ∏k=0^∞ (1 - qk+1/2 x)-1 is the quantum dilogarithm. For a toric Calabi-Yau threefold X_Σ, the quiver with potential (Q, W) is constructed from the toric diagram: vertices correspond to torus-fixed points, arrows to torus-invariant curves, and the potential W encodes the relations from the toric geometry. The DT invariants are the Euler characteristics of the moduli spaces of representations of the Jacobi algebra J(Q, W). The motivic Donaldson-Thomas invariants DTmot(γ) ∈ K0(Var_C)[L-1/2] take values in the Grothendieck ring of varieties and refine the numerical DT invariants. The motivic wall-crossing formula (Kontsevich-Soibelman, Joyce-Song) states that the product of motivic quantum dilogarithms Ψmot(L1/2 x_γ)DTmot(γ) is invariant. The motivic quantum dilogarithm is Ψmot(x) = ∑n=0^∞ [Sym^n(A1)] x^n = ∏k=0^∞ (1 - Lk+1/2 x)-1 in the completed Grothendieck ring. For the conifold (the resolved conifold O(-1)⊕O(-1)→P1), the DT invariants are DT(0) = 1, DT(β) = -1 for the curve class β, and DT(nβ) = 0 for n>1. The wall-crossing formula reproduces the identity Ψ(q) Ψ(-q) = 1. For the local P2 (the total space of O(-3)→P2), the DT invariants are given by the MacMahon function M(q) = ∏n=1^∞ (1 - q^n)-n. The refined DT invariants DTj_L,j_R(γ) ∈ Z are the BPS invariants of the M-theory compactification on X_Σ. The refined wall-crossing formula involves the refined quantum dilogarithm Ψref(x; y) = ∏k=0^∞ (1 - y2k+1 x)-1 (1 - y-2k-1 x)-1. The wall-crossing formula for toric Calabi-Yau threefolds is equivalent to the mutation invariance of the cluster algebra associated to the quiver Q. The cluster DT invariants are the coefficients of the cluster variables in the quantum cluster algebra. The wall-crossing formula also governs the transformation of the topological string partition function under the change of polarization in the B-model.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of the Elliptic Genera: The Hirzebruch χ_y-Genus, the Witten Genus, and Mirror Symmetry for Toric Calabi-Yau Manifolds

The elliptic genus of a compact complex manifold X is a topological invariant that interpolates between the Euler characteristic, the signature, and the Â-genus. For a toric variety X_Σ, the elliptic genus can be computed via localization with respect to the torus action. The Hirzebruch χ_y-genus is χ_y(X) = ∑p,q (-1)^q hp,q y^p = ∫_X ∏i=1^n x_i (1+y e-x_i)/(1-e-x_i) where x_i are the Chern roots of the tangent bundle. For a smooth projective toric variety, the χ_y-genus equals the evaluation of the Todd genus of the virtual bundle T_X ⊗ (1+y) - y. The Witten genus is the specialization φ_W(X) = χy=-1(X) = ∫_X ∏i=1^n x_i / (1-e-x_i) which for a Calabi-Yau manifold equals the Â-genus. For a toric Calabi-Yau manifold X_Σ (non-compact), the elliptic genus is defined via equivariant localization with respect to the torus action preserving the Calabi-Yau form. The equivariant elliptic genus Zell(X_Σ; τ, z, ε) depends on the modular parameter τ, the elliptic parameter z, and the equivariant parameters ε_i. It satisfies the modular transformation properties under SL(2,Z) and the elliptic transformation properties under the lattice Z + τZ. The mirror symmetry for elliptic genera states that the equivariant elliptic genus of a toric Calabi-Yau manifold X_Σ equals the equivariant elliptic genus of its mirror Landau-Ginzburg model (Y, W) computed via the Givental-Lee quantum D-module. The elliptic genus of a toric Calabi-Yau threefold can be expressed as a sum over fixed points of the torus action: Zell = ∑p ∈ X^T ∏i=13 θ(ε_i(p) + z; τ)/θ(ε_i(p); τ) where θ is the Jacobi theta function and ε_i(p) are the weights of the torus action on the tangent space at p. The elliptic genus of a toric Calabi-Yau threefold is a weak Jacobi form of weight 0 and index determined by the Calabi-Yau condition. The elliptic genus of a toric Fano variety is a polynomial in y. The elliptic genus of a toric variety with a torus action can be computed from the fan Σ using the formula χ_y(X_Σ) = ∑σ ∈ Σ (-1)dim σ χ_y(U_σ) where U_σ ≅ Cdim σ × (C^*)n-dim σ is the torus orbit. The elliptic genus of a toric variety satisfies the multiplicative property χ_y(X × Y) = χ_y(X) χ_y(Y). The elliptic genus of a toric hypersurface can be computed via the adjunction formula and the toric mirror symmetry. The elliptic genus of a toric Calabi-Yau threefold is related to the topological string partition function via the Gopakumar-Vafa invariants: Ztop = exp(∑g≥0 ∑β>0 ng,β (2 sin(g_s/2))2g-2 Q^β) where ng,β are the Gopakumar-Vafa invariants, and the elliptic genus is the generating function of the refined BPS invariants nj_L,j_R,β.

  • Confusing the topological vertex with the topological string partition function: The topological vertex is a local building block; the full partition function is a sum over partitions on internal edges with gluing rules. The vertex itself is not the partition function.
  • Assuming tropical Hodge numbers equal classical Hodge numbers: For a tropical curve of genus g, h1,1trop = g but h1,0(X) = g. The tropical Hodge diamond is not the classical Hodge diamond; it computes the limit mixed Hodge structure of a degeneration.
  • Treating all stability conditions on D^b(Coh(X_Σ)) as geometric: The space Stab(D^b(Coh(X_Σ))) has many components; only the distinguished component Stab^† contains geometric stability conditions (tilts of Coh(X_Σ)). Non-geometric stability conditions exist and are not fully classified.
  • Overlooking the role of the quantum dilogarithm in the wall-crossing formula: The invariance is of the PRODUCT of quantum dilogarithms ordered by phase, not of individual DT invariants. The DT invariants themselves change across walls; only the ordered product is invariant.
  • Confusing motivic DT invariants with numerical DT invariants: Motivic DT invariants take values in the Grothendieck ring of varieties K0(Var_C)[L-1/2]; numerical DT invariants are their Euler characteristics. The motivic wall-crossing formula is a refinement, not a replacement.
  • Assuming the elliptic genus of a non-compact toric Calabi-Yau is a modular form: The equivariant elliptic genus is a weak Jacobi form only after including the equivariant parameters; the non-equivariant limit may have poles and is not a modular form.
  • Mixing up the Hirzebruch χ_y-genus with the Witten genus: The Witten genus is the specialization y = -1 of the χ_y-genus, not y = 1. The χ_y-genus at y = 1 is the signature; at y = 0 it is the Euler characteristic.

Toric Varieties and Matroid Theory: Bergman Fans, the Chow Ring of a Matroid, and Hodge Theory of Matroids

The Bergman fan of a matroid provides a tropical analogue of a toric variety, and its geometry has become a central bridge between combinatorics, algebraic geometry, and Hodge theory. Given a loopless matroid M on a finite ground set E of rank r, the Bergman fan Σ_M is a pure (r−1)-dimensional rational polyhedral fan in ℝ^E / ℝ·(1,…,1) whose cones correspond to flags of flats of M. The fan Σ_M is the tropicalization of the matroid's realization space (when M is realizable over a field) and coincides with the tropical linear space associated to M. The toric variety X(Σ_M) is generally singular and non-compact; its compactification via the wonderful compactification of the hyperplane arrangement complement (De Concini–Procesi) yields a smooth projective toric variety whose Chow ring is isomorphic to the Chow ring of the matroid A^*(M) defined by Feichtner–Yuzvinsky and later studied by Adiprasito–Huh–Katz.

The Chow ring A^*(M) is generated by variables x_F for each nonempty proper flat F of M, subject to the relations x_F xF' = 0 for incomparable flats, and ∑F ∋ i x_F = ∑F ∋ j x_F for all i,j ∈ E. The degree map deg: Ar-1(M) → ℤ sends the monomial ∏k=1r-1 xF_k to 1 if the flats form a complete flag, and 0 otherwise. This ring satisfies Poincaré duality, the hard Lefschetz theorem, and the Hodge–Riemann relations — the latter two proved by Adiprasito, Huh, and Katz (2018) using a combinatorial analogue of the Kähler package. The proof constructs a strictly submodular function on the lattice of flats, which plays the role of an ample class, and verifies the Hodge–Riemann bilinear relations by induction on the rank via the "Lefschetz decomposition" of the Chow ring.

For a realizable matroid M = M(A) coming from a hyperplane arrangement A in ℙr-1, the wonderful compactification Y_A is a smooth projective toric variety whose fan is a subdivision of Σ_M. The Chow ring A^*(Y_A) is canonically isomorphic to A^*(M), and the ample class on Y_A corresponds to a strictly submodular function. The Hodge numbers of Y_A are given by the Whitney numbers of the first kind of M, and the Poincaré polynomial of Y_A equals the characteristic polynomial of M evaluated at -t. The tropical Hodge numbers of the Bergman fan Σ_M, defined via the limit mixed Hodge structure of a degeneration, coincide with the classical Hodge numbers of Y_A. This provides a tropical interpretation of the Hodge theory of matroids and explains why the Bergman fan, despite being non-compact, carries a "Hodge structure" satisfying the Kähler package.

The connection extends to the theory of valuations on the function field of the toric variety. The space of valuations on the function field of X(Σ_M) with center on the toric boundary is homeomorphic to the Bergman fan Σ_M equipped with its natural topology. The tropicalization map from the analytification of the torus (in the sense of Berkovich) to Σ_M is a retraction, and the fibers are tori of varying dimension. This identifies the Bergman fan as the non-Archimedean skeleton of the toric variety, linking the theory to the Gross–Siebert program and the study of degenerations of Calabi–Yau varieties.

Toric Varieties and the Positive Grassmannian: Cluster Algebras, Scattering Amplitudes, and the Amplituhedron

The positive Grassmannian Gr≥0(k,n) — the subset of the real Grassmannian Gr(k,n) where all Plücker coordinates are nonnegative — admits a remarkable cluster algebra structure discovered by Scott (2006) and a cell decomposition into positroid cells indexed by decorated permutations. Each positroid cell is isomorphic to (ℝ>0)^d for some d, and the closure relations among cells are governed by the Bruhat order on affine permutations. The cluster variables are certain Plücker coordinates, and the exchange relations are the Plücker relations. The cluster complex is a simplicial sphere whose facets correspond to clusters, and the cluster fan is a complete simplicial fan whose toric variety is a compactification of the positive Grassmannian.

The toric variety X(Σ) associated to the cluster fan of Gr≥0(k,n) is a projective toric variety whose boundary divisors correspond to the frozen variables (the cyclically consecutive Plücker coordinates). The interior of X(Σ) contains the positive Grassmannian as a dense open subset. The cluster algebra structure provides a canonical basis (the cluster monomials) for the homogeneous coordinate ring of X(Σ), which coincides with the coordinate ring of the affine cone over the Grassmannian in its Plücker embedding. The scattering amplitudes of planar N=4 super Yang–Mills theory are computed by integrals over the positive Grassmannian, and the amplituhedron An,k,L — a geometric object living in the Grassmannian Gr(k,k+L) — is defined as the image of Gr≥0(k,n) under a linear map Z: ℝ^n → ℝk+L with positive maximal minors. The amplituhedron is not a toric variety, but its boundary stratification is closely related to the cluster structure of Gr≥0(k,n).

The connection to toric geometry is made precise through the theory of toric degenerations. The Grassmannian Gr(k,n) admits a flat degeneration to a toric variety X(Σ) where Σ is the cluster fan (or the Gelfand–Tsetlin fan, or the string polytope fan). The toric degeneration is induced by a valuation on the function field of Gr(k,n) coming from a highest-term valuation with respect to a cluster seed. The resulting toric variety is normal and Cohen–Macaulay, and its fan is the tropicalization of the Grassmannian with respect to the Plücker embedding. The tropical Grassmannian Trop Gr(k,n) is a subfan of the cluster fan, and its cones correspond to matroid subdivisions of the hypersimplex Δk,n. The positive tropical Grassmannian Trop+ Gr(k,n) is the subfan where all Plücker coordinates are positive, and it coincides with the cluster fan of Gr≥0(k,n).

The amplituhedron can be understood as a projection of the positive Grassmannian, and its volume form (the canonical form) has logarithmic singularities on the boundary of the amplituhedron. The canonical form is computed by summing over BCFW triangulations, which correspond to triangulations of the amplituhedron into simplices. Each simplex is the image of a positroid cell under the map Z, and its volume is given by a rational function of the Plücker coordinates. The cluster algebra structure provides a systematic way to generate all triangulations via mutations, and the cluster fan encodes the combinatorics of the amplituhedron's boundary. The connection to toric geometry is further deepened by the observation that the amplituhedron's canonical form can be expressed as a sum over lattice points in the moment polytope of the toric variety X(Σ), linking scattering amplitudes to the Ehrhart theory of the cluster fan.

Worked Example: Bergman Fan of the Uniform Matroid U2,4
Let M = U2,4 be the uniform matroid of rank 2 on 4 elements {1,2,3,4}. Describe the Bergman fan Σ_M, compute its f-vector, and determine the Chow ring A^*(M). Verify that the hard Lefschetz theorem holds for a strictly submodular function.
Step 1: Flats of U2,4. The flats are: ∅ (rank 0), {1},{2},{3},{4} (rank 1), and {1,2,3,4} (rank 2). The nonempty proper flats are the four singletons. Step 2: Cones of Σ_M. A cone corresponds to a flag of flats ∅ ⊊ F1 ⊊ … ⊊ F_k ⊊ E. For rank 2, flags are either a single rank-1 flat (ray) or a pair of incomparable rank-1 flats (2-dimensional cone). Since any two distinct singletons are incomparable, the rays are generated by e_i (modulo the line (1,1,1,1)), and the 2-cones are spanned by e_i, e_j for i≠j. The fan Σ_M lives in ℝ4 / ℝ·(1,1,1,1) ≅ ℝ3. It has 4 rays and 6 two-dimensional cones. The f-vector is (1, 4, 6). Step 3: Chow ring A^*(M). Generators: x1, x2, x3, x4 corresponding to the four singletons. Relations: x_i x_j = 0 for i≠j (since singletons are incomparable? Wait: in U2,4, any two distinct singletons are incomparable, so x_i x_j = 0 for all i≠j). Also ∑F ∋ i x_F = ∑F ∋ j x_F. For i=1, the only flat containing 1 is {1}, so x1 = x2 = x3 = x4. Let x = x1 = x2 = x3 = x4. Then x_i x_j = x2 = 0 for i≠j, but x_i2 is not constrained by the incomparability relation. However, the relation x_i x_j = 0 for i≠j implies x2 = 0. The degree map: A1(M) is generated by x, and deg(x) = 1 (since the flag {1} ⊂ E gives degree 1). The ring is A^*(M) = ℤ[x]/(x2) with deg(x)=1. Step 4: Hard Lefschetz. A strictly submodular function is a function c on flats such that c(F) + c(G) > c(F∩G) + c(F∪G) for incomparable F,G. Take c({i}) = 1 for all i, c(E)=0. The ample class is ℓ = ∑ x_i = 4x. The Lefschetz map L: A0 → A1 is multiplication by ℓ. A0 = ℤ·1, A1 = ℤ·x. L(1) = 4x, which is an isomorphism (since 4 is invertible in ℚ). The Hodge–Riemann form Q(a,b) = deg(a·ℓ·b) on A0 is Q(1,1) = deg(ℓ) = 4 > 0. The theorem holds.
Exam Strategy: Matroid–Toric Dictionary
Memorize the precise correspondence: flats rays, flags of flats cones, strictly submodular function ample class, Chow ring of matroid Chow ring of wonderful compactification, Whitney numbers Hodge numbers. For the positive Grassmannian, know that the cluster fan is the positive tropical Grassmannian, the frozen variables are the cyclically consecutive Plücker coordinates, and the amplituhedron is a projection of the positive Grassmannian, not a toric variety itself. The toric degeneration of Gr(k,n) to the cluster fan toric variety is induced by a highest-term valuation from a cluster seed. These precise statements are what examiners test.

Toric Varieties and the Duistermaat–Heckman Theorem: Equivariant Localization, the Jeffrey–Kirwan Residue, and the Witten Index

The Duistermaat–Heckman (DH) theorem states that for a Hamiltonian torus action on a compact symplectic manifold (M,ω) with moment map μ: M → 𝔱^*, the pushforward of the Liouville measure under μ is a piecewise polynomial measure on 𝔱^*. For a toric variety X_Σ with a Kähler form ω in the class of an ample line bundle L, the moment polytope Δ = μ(X_Σ) is the lattice polytope associated to L, and the DH measure is the Lebesgue measure on Δ. The DH theorem is a special case of the Atiyah–Bott–Berline–Vergne (ABBV) localization formula in equivariant cohomology, which expresses the integral of an equivariant cohomology class over M as a sum over fixed points of the torus action.

For a smooth projective toric variety X_Σ, the fixed points of the torus action correspond to the maximal cones σ ∈ Σ(n). The tangent weights at the fixed point p_σ are the primitive generators u_ρ of the rays ρ ∈ σ(1), and the equivariant cohomology ring H^*_T(X_Σ) is isomorphic to the Stanley–Reisner ring of the fan modulo the linear relations ∑ ⟨m, u_ρ⟩ x_ρ = 0 for m ∈ M. The ABBV formula for the integral of an equivariant class α ∈ H^*_T(X_Σ) reads ∫X_Σ α = ∑σ ∈ Σ(n) α(p_σ) / e_T(Tp_σ X_Σ), where e_T is the equivariant Euler class. For the equivariant Todd class Td_T(X_Σ), this gives the equivariant Hirzebruch–Riemann–Roch theorem, which computes the equivariant index of a line bundle L_λ as ∑σ e⟨λ, u_σ⟩ / ∏ρ∈σ(1) (1 - e-⟨u_ρ, ·⟩).

The Jeffrey–Kirwan (JK) residue provides a contour integral formulation of the ABBV localization. For a toric variety X_Σ with fan Σ in N_ℝ, the JK residue of a meromorphic form ω on the complexified Lie algebra 𝔱_ℂ with poles along the hyperplanes ⟨u_ρ, z⟩ = 0 is defined by choosing a generic vector η ∈ 𝔱 (the JK reference vector) and integrating over a cycle determined by η. The JK residue of the form ∏ρ (1 - e-⟨u_ρ, z⟩)-1 e⟨λ, z⟩ dz computes the dimension of H0(X_Σ, L_λ) for λ in the ample cone. The JK residue is independent of the choice of η as long as η lies in the same chamber of the hyperplane arrangement, and the wall-crossing formula for the JK residue as η crosses a wall is given by the difference of residues on the two sides, which corresponds to the variation of the cohomology of L_λ as λ crosses a wall of the ample cone.

The Witten index of a supersymmetric quantum mechanics on X_Σ with superpotential given by the moment map is the equivariant index of the Dirac operator twisted by the line bundle L_λ. By the Atiyah–Singer index theorem, this equals the integral of the equivariant Â-genus, which for a toric variety localizes to the same sum over fixed points as the DH formula. The Witten index is a topological invariant that computes the Euler characteristic χ(X_Σ, L_λ) = ∑ (-1)^i dim H^i(X_Σ, L_λ). For λ ample, by Kodaira vanishing, this equals dim H0(X_Σ, L_λ), which is the number of lattice points in the polytope Δ_λ. The DH theorem, the ABBV localization, the JK residue, and the Witten index are thus four equivalent ways to compute the same quantity, each revealing different aspects of the toric geometry: the DH theorem gives the symplectic volume, the ABBV formula gives the fixed-point contribution, the JK residue gives the contour integral representation, and the Witten index gives the physical interpretation.

Toric Varieties and the Grothendieck–Riemann–Roch Theorem: Todd Classes, the Hirzebruch–Riemann–Roch Theorem, and the Computation of Euler Characteristics

The Grothendieck–Riemann–Roch (GRR) theorem for a proper morphism f: X → Y of smooth varieties states that ch(f_! F) · Td(Y) = f_*(ch(F) · Td(X)) in the operational Chow ring (or rational Chow ring) of Y, where f_! is the derived pushforward in K-theory, ch is the Chern character, and Td is the Todd class. For a toric morphism f: X_Σ → X_Σ' induced by a map of fans φ: N → N' compatible with Σ and Σ', the GRR theorem can be made completely explicit in terms of the combinatorial data of the fans. The Todd class of a smooth toric variety X_Σ is Td(X_Σ) = ∏ρ∈Σ(1) (D_ρ / (1 - e-D_ρ)), where D_ρ are the torus-invariant prime divisors. The Chern character of a torus-equivariant line bundle L_λ corresponding to a piecewise linear function λ on Σ is ch(L_λ) = ec1(L_λ) = e∑ λ(u_ρ) D_ρ.

For the structure morphism f: X_Σ → pt, the GRR theorem reduces to the Hirzebruch–Riemann–Roch (HRR) theorem: χ(X_Σ, F) = ∫X_Σ ch(F) · Td(X_Σ). For a torus-equivariant line bundle L_λ on a smooth projective toric variety, this gives χ(X_Σ, L_λ) = ∑σ ∈ Σ(n) eλ(u_σ) / ∏ρ∈σ(1) (1 - e-⟨u_ρ, ·⟩) evaluated at the appropriate differential operator, which by the ABBV localization equals the sum over fixed points of the equivariant index. The non-equivariant limit recovers the Ehrhart polynomial of the polytope Δ_λ: χ(X_Σ, L_λ) = |Δ_λ ∩ M| for λ ample. For a general coherent sheaf F on X_Σ, the HRR theorem expresses χ(F) as a sum over cones of Σ of contributions involving the Chern character of F and the Todd class of the tangent bundle.

The Todd class of a singular toric variety can be defined via the Baum–Fulton–MacPherson Todd class transformation Td_*: A_*(X) → H_*(X) (or to the operational Chow ring). For a toric variety X_Σ with at worst orbifold singularities, the Todd class is given by the same product formula but with the divisors D_ρ replaced by their ℚ-Cartier indices. The GRR theorem for a proper toric morphism f: X_Σ → X_Σ' between possibly singular toric varieties holds in the operational Chow ring with ℚ-coefficients, and the pushforward f_* on Chow groups is given by the map on fans that sends a cone σ to φ(σ) if φ is injective on σ, and to 0 otherwise. The Todd class of the relative tangent complex T_f is the ratio Td(X_Σ) / f^* Td(X_Σ'), and the GRR formula becomes ch(f_! F) · Td(X_Σ') = f_*(ch(F) · Td(X_Σ) / f^* Td(X_Σ')).

The computation of Euler characteristics via HRR is particularly powerful for toric vector bundles. A toric vector bundle E on X_Σ corresponds to a Klyachko filtration {E^ρ(i)} for each ray ρ. The Chern character of E can be expressed in terms of the filtration data, and the HRR integral reduces to a sum over maximal cones of rational functions of the filtration jumps. For the tangent bundle T_X, the HRR theorem gives χ(X_Σ, T_X) = dim H0(X_Σ, T_X) - dim H1(X_Σ, T_X) + …, which computes the Euler characteristic of the deformation complex. For a smooth Fano toric variety, H^i(X_Σ, T_X) = 0 for i > 0 by a theorem of Demazure, so χ(X_Σ, T_X) = dim H0(X_Σ, T_X) = dim Aut(X_Σ)0, the dimension of the connected component of the automorphism group. The GRR theorem also underlies the computation of the quantum cohomology ring via the mirror theorem, where the I-function is a hypergeometric series whose coefficients are given by the HRR integrals of powers of the hyperplane class.

Toric Varieties and Algebraic K-Theory: The Thomason–Trobaugh Theorem, the Gamma Filtration, and the Comparison with Chow Groups

The algebraic K-theory of a toric variety X_Σ is a fundamental invariant that encodes the category of coherent sheaves (or perfect complexes) on X_Σ. For a smooth toric variety, the Thomason–Trobaugh theorem (a generalization of the Quillen localization sequence) implies that the K-theory spectrum K(X_Σ) is equivalent to the homotopy colimit of the K-theory spectra of the torus-invariant affine open subsets U_σ ≅ 𝔸dim σ × (ℂ^*)n-dim σ. Since K(𝔸^d) ≃ K(ℂ) and K(ℂ^*) ≃ K(ℂ) × Σ K(ℂ), the K-theory of each U_σ is a product of copies of K(ℂ). The homotopy colimit over the fan Σ yields a description of K(X_Σ) in terms of the combinatorics of Σ. In particular, the rational K-theory K_*(X_Σ) ⊗ ℚ is isomorphic to the Chow ring A^*(X_Σ) ⊗ ℚ via the Chern character, and the isomorphism respects the gamma filtration on K-theory and the codimension filtration on the Chow ring.

The gamma filtration γ^i K0(X_Σ) is defined by γ^i = ⟨γ^k(x) | k ≥ i, x ∈ K0(X_Σ)⟩ where γ^k are the Adams operations. For a smooth toric variety, the associated graded pieces gr^i_γ K0(X_Σ) ⊗ ℚ are canonically isomorphic to A^i(X_Σ) ⊗ ℚ. The Chern character ch: K0(X_Σ) ⊗ ℚ → ⊕ A^i(X_Σ) ⊗ ℚ is an isomorphism of graded rings, where the grading on the target is by codimension. The Todd class Td(X_Σ) ∈ A^*(X_Σ) ⊗ ℚ is the inverse of the Chern character of the structure sheaf under the GRR isomorphism. The Riemann–Roch theorem without denominators (the "Riemann–Roch theorem for the gamma filtration") states that for any proper morphism f: X → Y of smooth varieties, the pushforward f_*: K0(X) → K0(Y) satisfies f_*(γ^i K0(X)) ⊆ γi-d K0(Y) where d = dim X - dim Y, and the induced map on associated graded pieces is the usual pushforward on Chow groups.

For a singular toric variety X_Σ, the K-theory of coherent sheaves G0(X_Σ) (or K0(X_Σ) for perfect complexes) is more subtle. The Thomason–Trobaugh theorem still applies, but the affine pieces U_σ are singular toric varieties. The K-theory of an affine toric variety U_σ is the K-theory of the semigroup ring ℂ[σ^∨ ∩ M], which can be computed via the resolution of singularities by fan subdivision. The localization sequence for a toric stratification gives a spectral sequence converging to G_*(X_Σ) whose E1 page is a direct sum of G_*(U_σ) over cones σ. The rational G-theory G_*(X_Σ) ⊗ ℚ is isomorphic to the operational Chow ring A^*(X_Σ) ⊗ ℚ via the Fulton–MacPherson Chern class transformation. The gamma filtration on G0(X_Σ) is defined using the Adams operations on the λ-ring structure, and the associated graded pieces are again the Chow groups.

The comparison between K-theory and Chow groups for toric varieties is particularly clean because the torus action provides a cellular decomposition (the Białynicki-Birula decomposition) whose cells are the torus orbits O_σ ≅ (ℂ^*)n-dim σ. The K-theory of a torus (ℂ^*)^k is K(ℂ)[t1±1, …, t_k±1], and the K-theory of X_Σ is the homotopy limit of a diagram of such rings indexed by the fan. This yields a presentation of K0(X_Σ) as the quotient of the free abelian group on symbols [O_σ] by relations coming from the boundary maps in the localization sequence. The Chern character maps [O_σ] to the class of the orbit closure V(σ) in the Chow group, and the Todd class appears as the correction factor in the GRR formula. The Adams operations ψ^k act on K0(X_Σ) by ψ^k([O_σ]) = kdim σ [O_σ] + lower-dimensional terms, and the gamma filtration is the filtration by the order of vanishing of ψ^k - k^i. This combinatorial description makes the K-theory of toric varieties one of the few cases where the full K-theory ring (not just its rationalization) can be computed explicitly for large classes of examples.

  • Confusing the Bergman fan of a matroid with the tropicalization of a linear space: The Bergman fan Σ_M is the tropicalization of the matroid's realization space, but for a non-realizable matroid there is no linear space; Σ_M is still a well-defined fan. The tropical linear space associated to a realizable matroid is the tropicalization of the linear subspace, which coincides with Σ_M.
  • Assuming the positive Grassmannian is a toric variety: Gr≥0(k,n) is not a toric variety; it is a semi-algebraic subset of the Grassmannian. Its cluster fan gives a toric variety that compactifies it, but the positive Grassmannian itself has a boundary stratification that is not toric.
  • Misapplying the Duistermaat–Heckman theorem to non-compact toric varieties: The DH theorem requires a compact symplectic manifold. For non-compact toric varieties (e.g., toric Calabi–Yau), the moment map is not proper, and the pushforward measure is not piecewise polynomial on the whole 𝔱^*; one must use equivariant localization with a regularization (e.g., the Jeffrey–Kirwan residue with a reference vector).
  • Confusing the Jeffrey–Kirwan residue with the ordinary residue: The JK residue depends on a choice of reference vector η and integrates over a cycle determined by η. The ordinary residue is the sum of residues at all poles. The JK residue equals the ordinary residue only when η is in the positive chamber (all ⟨u_ρ, η⟩ > 0).
  • Assuming the Grothendieck–Riemann–Roch theorem holds integrally for singular toric varieties: GRR holds in the operational Chow ring with ℚ-coefficients. The Todd class of a singular toric variety is a rational class, and the pushforward on K-theory does not preserve the gamma filtration integrally. The Baum–Fulton–MacPherson Todd class transformation is required.
  • Overlooking the difference between K0 (perfect complexes) and G0 (coherent sheaves) for singular toric varieties: For smooth X_Σ, K0 ≅ G0. For singular X_Σ, they differ, and the Thomason–Trobaugh theorem relates them via the localization sequence. The rational isomorphism with Chow groups holds for G0, not K0, in the singular case.
  • Treating the amplituhedron as a toric variety: The amplituhedron is the image of the positive Grassmannian under a linear map; it is a semi-algebraic set with a boundary stratification that is not toric. Its canonical form has logarithmic singularities on the boundary, but it is not the moment polytope of a toric variety.

Toric Varieties and Givental's Quantization Formalism

Givental's quantization formalism provides a symplectic framework for the genus‑zero and higher‑genus Gromov–Witten theory of toric varieties. The starting point is the symplectic vector space H = H^*(X_Σ; ℂ)((z-1)) equipped with the symplectic form Ω(f,g) = Resz=0 (f(-z), g(z)) dz, where (·,·) is the Poincaré pairing. The Lagrangian cone L_X ⊂ H encodes the genus‑zero theory: its tangent spaces are of the form z Tt(z) L_X = J_X(t(z), -z) where J_X is the J‑function of X_Σ. For a toric variety, the J‑function admits an explicit hypergeometric expression J_X(t,z) = z et/z ∑d∈Eff(X) q^d ∏ρ ∏m=1⟨D_ρ,d⟩ (D_ρ + m z) / ∏m=1⟨D_ρ,d⟩ (D_ρ + m z) with D_ρ the toric divisor classes. Givental's quantization maps a symplectic transformation R(z) = Id + R1 z + R2 z2 + … acting on H to a differential operator R acting on the total descendant potential D_X = exp(∑g≥0 ℏg-1 F_g). The higher‑genus potentials F_g are then reconstructed from the genus‑zero data via the action of R on the topological field theory associated to the semisimple Frobenius manifold of X_Σ. In the toric case, the R‑matrix is computed from the asymptotic expansion of the fundamental solution of the quantum differential equation ∇_z = z ∂_z + c1(X_Σ) ⋆_τ - (dim X)/2, where ⋆_τ is the quantum product at τ. The formalism yields the Virasoro constraints for toric varieties and provides a proof of the higher‑genus mirror theorem: the total descendant potential of X_Σ equals the oscillatory integral of the Landau–Ginzburg mirror W = ∑ρ ey_ρ over the critical locus, after applying the quantized symplectic transformation that matches the asymptotic bases. This approach underlies the proof of the remodeling conjecture for toric Calabi–Yau threefolds and the computation of higher‑genus invariants via the topological vertex.

Toric Varieties and the Frobenius Manifold Structure on Quantum Cohomology

The quantum cohomology ring QH^*(X_Σ) = H^*(X_Σ; ℂ) ⊗ ℂ[[q]] carries a family of commutative, associative products ⋆_τ parameterized by τ ∈ H^*(X_Σ). For a projective toric variety, the product is determined by the three‑point genus‑zero Gromov–Witten invariants ⟨α, β, γ⟩0,3,d. Dubrovin showed that the data (QH^*, ⋆_τ, (·,·), E, e) defines a Frobenius manifold structure on the formal neighbourhood of the origin in H^*(X_Σ), where E = c1(X_Σ) + ∑ (1 - deg τ_i) τ_i ∂τ_i is the Euler vector field and e = 1 is the unit. The flat coordinates t^α of the Dubrovin connection ∇_z = d - (1/z) (c1(X_Σ) ⋆_τ) dτ are given by the components of the J‑function: t^α(τ) = ⟨τ, φ_α⟩ + ∑d≠0 q^d ⟨τ, φ_α/(z - ψ)⟩0,2,d. The prepotential F0(t) satisfies the WDVV equations ∂_α ∂_β ∂_γ F0 ηγδ ∂_δ ∂_ε ∂_ζ F0 = symmetric in α,β,ε,ζ. For toric varieties, the prepotential can be expressed in terms of the hypergeometric series of the mirror Landau–Ginzburg model. The monodromy of the Dubrovin connection around the large‑radius limit point yields the Stokes matrix of the quantum differential equation, which coincides with the Gram matrix of the exceptional collection in the derived category D^b(Coh X_Σ). The Frobenius manifold structure is semisimple at generic τ, and its canonical coordinates u_i(τ) are the eigenvalues of the quantum multiplication by the Euler vector field. The genus‑zero mirror theorem identifies the Frobenius manifold of X_Σ with the Saito Frobenius manifold of the mirror Laurent polynomial W = ∑ρ xv_ρ on the torus (ℂ^*)^n, establishing an isomorphism of Frobenius manifolds that respects the Euler vector field and the intersection form.

Worked Example: Quantum Product on ℙ2
Compute the quantum product H ⋆ H in QH^*(ℙ2) where H is the hyperplane class, and verify the WDVV equation for the prepotential F0 = (1/6) t03 + (1/2) t0 t12 + ∑d≥1 N_d q^d with N_d the genus‑zero Gromov–Witten invariants.
In ℙ2 the effective cone is generated by the line class d = 1. The only non‑zero three‑point invariants are ⟨H, H, H⟩0,3,1 = 1 and ⟨1, H, H⟩0,3,0 = 1. The quantum product is H ⋆ H = H2 + q ⟨H, H, H⟩0,3,1 1 = pt + q·1, where pt is the point class. The prepotential in flat coordinates t = t0·1 + t1·H + t2·pt is F0 = (1/2) t0 t12 + (1/6) t23 + ∑d≥1 N_d ed t1 with N1 = 1. The WDVV equation ∂13 F0 · ∂23 F0 = (∂12 ∂2 F0)2 reduces to 1·1 = 12, which holds. Higher‑degree terms satisfy WDVV because the quantum product is associative.
Exam Strategy
When asked to identify the Frobenius manifold data of a toric variety, list the five ingredients: (1) the state space H^*(X), (2) the quantum product ⋆_τ, (3) the Poincaré pairing, (4) the Euler vector field E = c1(X) + ∑ (1 - deg τ_i) τ_i ∂τ_i, (5) the unit e = 1. Remember that flat coordinates are given by the J‑function components, and the mirror isomorphism identifies the Dubrovin connection with the Gauss–Manin connection of the mirror Laurent polynomial.

Toric Varieties and the Orbifold Quantum Cohomology of Toric Deligne–Mumford Stacks

For a toric Deligne–Mumford stack 𝔛_Σ presented by a stacky fan (Σ, β) where β: ℤ^m → N extends the fan map, the orbifold quantum cohomology QH^*orb(𝔛_Σ) is a deformation of the Chen–Ruan cohomology ring H^*CR(𝔛_Σ). The state space is the direct sum over twisted sectors indexed by box elements b ∈ Box(Σ) = ∪σ∈Σ (β-1(σ) ∩ [0,1)^m). The orbifold quantum product ⋆orb is defined by genus‑zero orbifold Gromov–Witten invariants ⟨α, β, γ⟩0,3,dorb with insertions in twisted sectors. The mirror theorem for toric stacks (Coates–Corti–Iritani–Tseng) identifies the I‑function I_𝔛(z, q) = z ∑d∈Eff(𝔛) q^d ∏i=1^m ∏m=0⟨D_i,d⟩-1 (D_i + (frac(⟨D_i,d⟩) + m) z) / ∏m=1⟨D_i,d⟩ (D_i + m z) with the J‑function after a change of variables q = Q e^τ. The orbifold quantum product is semisimple at large radius, and its canonical coordinates are given by the critical values of the mirror Landau–Ginzburg superpotential W = ∑i=1^m y_i on the stacky torus. The Crepant Transformation Conjecture predicts that for a crepant resolution π: Y → 𝔛_Σ, the analytic continuation of the quantum cohomology of Y matches the orbifold quantum cohomology of 𝔛_Σ after a symplectic transformation. This has been proved for toric Calabi–Yau threefolds and for weighted projective spaces. The orbifold quantum cohomology also carries a Frobenius manifold structure with Euler vector field E = c1orb(𝔛_Σ) + ∑ (1 - age(τ_i)) τ_i ∂τ_i, where age is the degree shifting number of the twisted sector.

Toric Varieties and the Crepant Transformation Conjecture for Toric Varieties

The Crepant Transformation Conjecture (CTC) extends the Crepant Resolution Conjecture to arbitrary crepant birational transformations between toric varieties or toric stacks. Let X_Σ and X_Σ' be two toric varieties (or stacks) related by a sequence of toric flops or flips that preserve the canonical class. The conjecture asserts that the total descendant potentials DX_Σ and DX_Σ' are identified by an explicit symplectic transformation U acting on the Givental symplectic space, after analytic continuation in the Kähler parameters. For toric varieties, the transformation U is constructed from the wall‑crossing formulas for the I‑functions across the walls of the secondary fan. Each wall corresponds to a flop curve class ℓ, and the wall‑crossing operator is a quantized symplectomorphism W_ℓ = exp(∑k≥0 (ℏ^k/k!) ⟨τ, …⟩0,k+2,ℓ) that implements the change of basis between the asymptotic expansions of the I‑functions on adjacent chambers. The CTC has been verified for all toric Calabi–Yau threefolds (Iritani, Coates–Iritani–Jiang) and for toric Fano varieties related by mutations of the fan. In the Fano case, the analytic continuation is governed by the Stokes phenomenon of the quantum differential equation, and the transformation U coincides with the mutation of the exceptional collection in the derived category. The conjecture also predicts that the genus‑zero Gromov–Witten invariants of X_Σ and X_Σ' are related by the same wall‑crossing formulas, which can be expressed in terms of the tropical counts of rational curves in the corresponding polytopes.

Toric Varieties and the Quantum Lefschetz Hyperplane Theorem

The Quantum Lefschetz Hyperplane Theorem computes the genus‑zero Gromov–Witten invariants of a nef complete intersection Y ⊂ X_Σ in terms of the invariants of the ambient toric variety X_Σ. Let E = ⊕j=1^r L_j be a direct sum of nef line bundles on X_Σ, and let Y be the zero locus of a regular section of E. The theorem states that the J‑function of Y is obtained from the J‑function of X_Σ by the formula J_Y(t,z) = (∏j=1^r ∏m=1⟨c1(L_j), d⟩ (c1(L_j) + m z))-1 J_X(t,z) |q^d → q^d ∏j q_j⟨c1(L_j), d⟩ after a suitable change of variables. Equivalently, the I‑function of Y is I_Y = I_X · ∏j ∏m=1⟨c1(L_j), d⟩ (c1(L_j) + m z)-1. This result follows from the localization formula on the moduli space of stable maps to the total space of E, using the torus action that scales the fibers. The theorem requires the convexity condition H1(C, f^* E) = 0 for all stable maps f: C → X_Σ, which holds when each L_j is nef. For a Calabi–Yau hypersurface defined by a section of the anticanonical bundle -K_X, the formula simplifies to J_Y = J_X / ∏m=1⟨-K_X, d⟩ (-K_X + m z). The Quantum Lefschetz theorem is a key ingredient in the proof of the mirror theorem for complete intersections in toric varieties (Batyrev–Borisov) and in the computation of the quantum cohomology of toric Fano complete intersections via the mirror Landau–Ginzburg model with superpotential W = ∑ρ xv_ρ + ∑j q_j ∏ρ x⟨v_ρ, c1(L_j)⟩.

  • Confusing the Givental R‑matrix with the fundamental solution of the quantum differential equation: the R‑matrix is the asymptotic expansion of the fundamental solution in the semisimple basis, not the solution itself.
  • Assuming the Frobenius manifold structure is defined on the whole cohomology ring: it is only formal near the large‑radius limit point; convergence requires analytic continuation.
  • Treating orbifold quantum cohomology as a simple quotient of the ambient quantum cohomology: the twisted sectors introduce new state space summands and age‑shifted degrees that change the Euler vector field.
  • Neglecting the analytic continuation in the Crepant Transformation Conjecture: the identification of descendant potentials holds only after continuing the Kähler parameters across walls of the secondary fan.
  • Applying the Quantum Lefschetz theorem to non‑nef line bundles: the convexity hypothesis fails, and the formula acquires correction terms from higher‑genus invariants of the fibers.
  • Mixing the I‑function of a toric stack with the J‑function of its coarse moduli space: the stacky I‑function includes fractional powers of z corresponding to the box elements, while the coarse J‑function does not.
  • Overlooking the role of the Euler vector field in the Frobenius manifold: its eigenvalues give the canonical coordinates, and its flow generates the grading operator that controls the genus expansion.

Toric Varieties and the BCOV Holomorphic Anomaly Equations for Higher‑Genus Mirror Symmetry

The Bershadsky–Cecotti–Ooguri–Vafa (BCOV) holomorphic anomaly equations give a recursive method to compute higher‑genus topological string amplitudes on Calabi–Yau threefolds. For a toric Calabi–Yau threefold X_Σ the mirror geometry is described by a Landau–Ginzburg model with superpotential W = ∑ρ∈Σ(1) xv_ρ where the sum runs over the rays of the fan Σ. The genus‑zero data — the prepotential F⁰ — is obtained from the mirror map and the Yukawa couplings computed on the toric side via the GKZ hypergeometric system. The BCOV equations then determine the genus‑g free energies F^g for g≥1 as quasi‑modular forms in the flat coordinates, with the holomorphic anomaly ∂bar i F^g = ½ Cbar ijk ( D_j D_k Fg-1 + ∑h=1g-1 D_j F^h D_k Fg-h ). In the toric setting the propagators Cbar ijk and the covariant derivatives D_j can be expressed entirely in terms of the toric data: the intersection numbers of toric divisors, the Mori cone generators, and the period integrals of the mirror family. This makes the recursion algorithmic and has been implemented for all smooth toric Calabi–Yau threefolds up to Picard rank four (Huang–Katz–Klemm 2010). The resulting higher‑genus invariants agree with the Gromov–Witten invariants computed by localization on the moduli space of stable maps to X_Σ, providing a non‑trivial check of the mirror symmetry conjecture at all genera.

A key technical point is the choice of the holomorphic limit. The BCOV recursion produces F^g up to the addition of a holomorphic ambiguity, a polynomial in the flat coordinates whose coefficients are fixed by boundary conditions at large radius, conifold, and orbifold points of the moduli space. For toric Calabi–Yau threefolds these boundary conditions are read off from the toric fan: the large‑radius limit corresponds to the maximal cone of the secondary fan, the conifold loci correspond to walls where a curve class shrinks, and the orbifold points correspond to stacky fans with non‑trivial generic stabilizers. The ambiguity is therefore a finite‑dimensional problem that can be solved by matching a few low‑degree Gromov–Witten invariants obtained from toric localization. This procedure has been carried out explicitly for the local ℙ² geometry (the canonical bundle of ℙ²) and for the resolved conifold, reproducing the celebrated genus‑one and genus‑two results of Faber–Pandharipande and of Huang–Klemm–Quackenbush.

The BCOV formalism also connects to the theory of the topological vertex. The vertex computes the all‑genus open‑string amplitudes on toric Calabi–Yau threefolds via a combinatorial sum over plane partitions. The closed‑string free energies F^g are obtained by gluing vertices according to the toric diagram and then performing the appropriate summation over Kähler parameters. The equivalence between the BCOV recursion and the topological vertex gluing was proved by Aganagic–Klemm–Mariño–Vafa (2005) using the Eynard–Orantin topological recursion on the mirror curve. For toric varieties the mirror curve is a Riemann surface defined by the Newton polynomial of the fan, and the topological recursion on this curve reproduces exactly the BCOV higher‑genus amplitudes. This gives a completely combinatorial algorithm for higher‑genus mirror symmetry on any toric Calabi–Yau threefold.

Toric Varieties and the Quantum Cohomology of Toric Deligne–Mumford Stacks via the I‑Function

When the fan Σ is replaced by a stacky fan (Σ, β) where β : ℤ^m → N assigns a lattice vector to each ray, the associated toric Deligne–Mumford stack X_Σ has a well‑defined orbifold quantum cohomology. The state space is the Chen–Ruan cohomology H*CR(X_Σ) = ⊕v∈Box(Σ) H*-2 age(v)(XΣ(v)), where Box(Σ) is the set of box elements (vectors in the unit cube of the lattice generated by the rays) and age(v) is the age shift. The genus‑zero orbifold Gromov–Witten invariants are encoded in the I‑function I_X(t,z) = ∑d∈Eff(X) q^d ∏ρ ∏m=1⟨D_ρ,d⟩ (D_ρ + m z)-1 ∏v∈Box ∏m=0⟨v,d⟩-1 (D_v + m z) where D_ρ are the toric divisor classes and D_v are the classes of the twisted sectors. This I‑function was introduced by Coates–Corti–Iritani–Tseng (2015) and satisfies the quantum differential equation z ∂_i I = (c1(L_i) ⋆_τ) I, where ⋆_τ is the orbifold quantum product at the point τ = ∑ t_i c1(L_i). The mirror theorem for toric stacks states that after the change of variables q^d = exp(∑ t_i ⟨c1(L_i), d⟩) the I‑function coincides with the J‑function J_X(τ,z) = eτ/z ( 1 + ∑n,d (q^d/n!) ⟨ τ,…,τ, φ_α/(z-ψ) ⟩0,n+1,d φ^α ). The proof uses the torus localization on the moduli stack of stable maps to X_Σ and the explicit description of the fixed loci in terms of decorated graphs whose vertices correspond to stacky toric strata.

The stacky I‑function contains fractional powers of z coming from the box elements; these encode the age shifts and are essential for the correct orbifold quantum product. For example, for the weighted projective stack ℙ(1,1,2) the box has two non‑trivial elements of ages ½ and 1, and the I‑function acquires factors (z/2) and (z) in the numerator. The resulting quantum cohomology ring is a deformation of the Chen–Ruan cohomology ring, and its semisimplicity at generic τ follows from the fact that the I‑function provides a fundamental solution of the quantum differential equation with a diagonalizable monodromy. The Stokes matrix of this differential equation is identified with the Gram matrix of the exceptional collection in the derived category D^b(Coh(X_Σ)), giving a stacky version of the Dubrovin conjecture. This has been verified for all smooth toric Fano stacks of Picard rank ≤ 3 (Coates–Iritani–Jiang 2014).

An important application is the computation of the orbifold quantum cohomology of the crepant resolution of a toric Gorenstein singularity. If X_Σ → Y is a crepant resolution of an affine toric variety Y with Gorenstein singularities, the orbifold quantum cohomology of the stacky resolution X_Σ is expected to match the quantum cohomology of Y after analytic continuation in the Kähler parameters (the Crepant Resolution Conjecture). The I‑function provides a concrete tool to test this: one computes the I‑function on both sides, performs the analytic continuation across the walls of the secondary fan, and checks that the resulting J‑functions agree up to a symplectic transformation. This program has been carried out for the A_n surface singularities and for the threefold conifold, confirming the conjecture in these cases.

Worked Example: Orbifold I‑Function for ℙ(1,1,2)
Compute the first two terms of the I‑function for the weighted projective stack ℙ(1,1,2) and identify the age‑shift contributions.
The stacky fan has rays v₁=(1,0), v₂=(0,1), v₃=(-1,-2) with β(e_i)=v_i. The box elements are b₁=(½,0) (age ½) and b₂=(0,½) (age 1). The effective cone is generated by the class d of a line. The I‑function is I = ∑k≥0 q^k ∏ρ=13 ∏m=1⟨D_ρ,kd⟩ (D_ρ+mz)-1 ∏b∈Box ∏m=0⟨b,kd⟩-1 (D_b+mz). For k=0 the product is empty, giving 1. For k=1 we have ⟨D₁,d⟩=1, ⟨D₂,d⟩=1, ⟨D₃,d⟩=2, so the divisor factor is (D₁+z)-1(D₂+z)-1(D₃+z)-1(D₃+2z)-1. The box contributions: for b₁, ⟨b₁,d⟩=½ gives no integer m; for b₂, ⟨b₂,d⟩=1 gives m=0 factor (Db₂)-1. Thus the k=1 term is q (D₁+z)-1(D₂+z)-1(D₃+z)-1(D₃+2z)-1 Db₂-1. The age shifts appear as the extra factor Db₂-1 and the half‑integer exponent in the box product, confirming the orbifold structure.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the I‑function of a toric stack, first write down the stacky fan and the box elements. The age of each box element is the sum of its coordinates; this determines the power of z in the numerator. Remember that the divisor product runs over all rays with upper limit ⟨D_ρ,d⟩, while the box product runs from m=0 to ⟨b,d⟩-1. A common mistake is to start the box product at m=1; the correct lower bound is 0, which produces the age‑shift factor D_b-1 when ⟨b,d⟩=1.

Toric Varieties and the Tropical Moduli Space of Curves: Toric Boundary Strata and the Tropical Torelli Map

The moduli space M_g of smooth curves of genus g admits a tropical analogue M_gtrop whose points are metric graphs of genus g. The work of Abramovich–Caporaso–Payne (2012) and of Chan–Galatius–Payne (2018) shows that M_gtrop can be realized as the cone complex of the toroidal compactification of M_g given by the Hassett–Keel program. Each cone corresponds to a stable graph with edge lengths, and the face relations correspond to edge contractions. The toric boundary strata of the compactified moduli space M_g are indexed by the same stable graphs, and the tropicalization map trop : M_gan → M_gtrop sends a degeneration of curves to its dual graph with edge lengths given by the valuations of the smoothing parameters. For toric varieties this picture becomes completely explicit: a family of curves in a toric variety X_Σ over a valuation ring gives a tropical curve in the fan Σ, and the tropicalization of the moduli space of stable maps Mg,n(X_Σ,β) is a polyhedral complex whose cones are indexed by decorated tropical maps. The tropical Torelli map sends a tropical curve to its Jacobian, a principally polarized tropical abelian variety, and this map is compatible with the classical Torelli map on the level of cone complexes. The compatibility was proved by Baker–Rabinoff (2015) using the theory of non‑archimedean analytic spaces and the fact that the tropicalization of the universal Jacobian over M_g is the universal tropical Jacobian over M_gtrop.

In the toric setting the tropical moduli space of maps can be described by the secondary fan of the fiber polytope of the map. For a fixed curve class β ∈ H2(X_Σ,ℤ) the space of tropical stable maps to Σ with degree β is a rational polyhedral complex whose maximal cones correspond to regular subdivisions of the Minkowski sum of the Newton polytopes of the sections defining the map. The dimension of a cone equals the number of edges of the tropical curve plus the dimension of the automorphism group of the source graph. The virtual fundamental class of Mg,n(X_Σ,β) tropicalizes to a cycle on this complex, and the tropical Gromov–Witten invariants defined by Mikhalkin (2005) and refined by Gross–Siebert (2011) coincide with the algebraic invariants after a suitable change of variables. This tropical correspondence theorem has been extended to all genera for toric targets by Cavalieri–Markwig–Ranganathan (2016) using the logarithmic Gromov–Witten theory of Abramovich–Chen.

The toric boundary strata of Mg,n(X_Σ,β) are themselves toric varieties (or toric stacks) whose fans are given by the cones of the tropical moduli complex. This allows one to compute intersection numbers on the boundary by tropical methods. For example, the λ_g class on M_g tropicalizes to the top‑dimensional piece of the tropical Hodge bundle, and its integral over M_g can be evaluated as a sum over trivalent graphs of products of edge lengths. The resulting formula reproduces the Faber–Pandharipande formula for the Hodge integrals in genus 2 and 3. The tropical approach also gives a new proof of the Pixton relations among tautological classes, because the tropical cycle classes satisfy the same relations as their algebraic counterparts.

Toric Varieties and Non‑Archimedean Mirror Symmetry: Berkovich SYZ Fibrations and the Tropical Vertex

Kontsevich and Soibelman (2006) proposed that mirror symmetry for Calabi–Yau varieties can be understood as a duality between non‑archimedean analytic spaces. For a toric Calabi–Yau threefold X_Σ the mirror is a non‑archimedean torus (K^*)3 equipped with a superpotential W = ∑ρ Tv_ρ xv_ρ where K is a non‑archimedean field with valuation v. The Berkovich analytification X_Σan contains a skeleton Sk(X_Σ) homeomorphic to the real torus (S1)3, and the SYZ fibration is the map X_Σan → Sk(X_Σ) given by the valuation of the toric coordinates. The mirror SYZ fibration is the dual torus fibration on the mirror side, and the tropical vertex of Gross–Siebert (2011) describes the wall‑crossing of the affine structure on the base. In the toric case the walls are exactly the codimension‑one cones of the secondary fan, and the wall‑crossing automorphisms are given by the tropical vertex group elements exp(∑k≥1 Nkβ Li2(z^k) ) where Nkβ are the genus‑zero Gromov–Witten invariants of X_Σ. The product of these automorphisms around a loop in the base yields the monodromy of the affine structure, which is identified with the monodromy of the quantum differential equation of X_Σ.

The tropical vertex construction can be carried out entirely in terms of the fan Σ. One starts with the scattering diagram consisting of the rays of Σ and the walls generated by the primitive curve classes. The consistency condition for the scattering diagram is equivalent to the associativity of the quantum product on H^*(X_Σ). The solution of the consistency condition produces a formal power series in the Kähler parameters that coincides with the I‑function of X_Σ. This was proved by Gross–Hacking–Keel (2015) for toric Calabi–Yau threefolds and by Mandel (2015) for general toric varieties. The Berkovich SYZ fibration provides a geometric realization of this scattering diagram: the base of the fibration is the tropicalization of the mirror family, and the walls are the loci where the fiber acquires a singularity. The tropical vertex group elements are the monodromies of the Gauss–Manin connection around these singular fibers.

An important consequence is the computation of the open‑string mirror map for Lagrangian branes in X_Σ. A Lagrangian torus fiber L ⊂ X_Σ corresponds to a point in the base of the SYZ fibration. The disk potential W_L(q) = ∑β n_β q^β counts holomorphic disks with boundary on L. In the tropical picture the disks are represented by tropical curves with one boundary component, and the count n_β is given by the tropical vertex invariant associated to the wall crossed by the path from the large‑radius point to the point representing L. This gives a completely combinatorial formula for the disk potential, which matches the open‑string mirror map computed by Fukaya–Oh–Ohta–Ono (2010) for toric Fano manifolds. The non‑archimedean approach also clarifies the analytic continuation in the Kähler parameters required by the Crepant Transformation Conjecture: the continuation corresponds to moving the base point in the Berkovich skeleton across walls of the scattering diagram.

Toric Varieties and the Logarithmic Gromov–Witten Theory with Expanded Degenerations: The Relative Orbifold Invariants

Logarithmic Gromov–Witten theory, developed by Abramovich–Chen (2014) and Gross–Siebert (2013), provides a modular compactification of the moduli space of stable maps to a log smooth pair (X,D). For a toric variety X_Σ with its toric boundary divisor D = ∪ρ D_ρ, the log structure is the divisorial one, and the moduli space Mg,nlog(X_Σ/D,β) parametrizes stable maps with contact orders along D encoded by a tropical curve in the fan Σ. The expanded degenerations of Li (2002) appear naturally as the Artin fan of the log stack: each stratum of the Artin fan corresponds to a toric blow‑up of X_Σ along a toric subvariety, and the universal expanded degeneration is the stack of log maps to the Artin fan. The virtual fundamental class on the log moduli space is constructed by the perfect obstruction theory of the log cotangent complex, and its degree yields the logarithmic Gromov–Witten invariants Ng,βlog(μ) where μ is a tuple of contact orders.

The relative orbifold invariants arise when the target is a toric Deligne–Mumford stack X_Σ with stacky boundary divisors. The log structure is then the fine saturated log structure associated to the stacky fan, and the contact orders are rational numbers determined by the box elements. The logarithmic invariants Ng,βlog(μ) are related to the ordinary orbifold Gromov–Witten invariants by a degeneration formula that sums over splittings of the tropical curve. This formula was proved by Chen (2016) using the gluing of log structures along the nodes of the source curve. In the toric case the gluing is governed by the tropical vertex, and the degeneration formula becomes a product of vertex invariants associated to the vertices of the tropical curve. The vertex invariants are exactly the open‑string invariants of the toric Calabi–Yau threefold obtained by taking the total space of the canonical bundle of the toric divisor. This gives a recursive algorithm to compute all higher‑genus logarithmic invariants from the genus‑zero vertex, which is known explicitly from the topological vertex.

A striking application is the proof of the Maulik–Toda conjecture for toric surfaces. The conjecture relates the generating series of stable pair invariants on a toric surface S to the generating series of logarithmic Gromov–Witten invariants of (S,D) where D is the toric boundary. Using the tropical vertex and the degeneration formula, Bousseau–Brizi–van Garrel (2020) showed that the two series coincide after the change of variables q = -eiu. The proof uses the fact that the stable pair invariants on S are computed by the topological vertex on the local Calabi–Yau threefold Tot(K_S), while the logarithmic invariants are computed by the same vertex with a different framing. The equality follows from the framing independence of the topological vertex, which is a consequence of the wall‑crossing formula for the tropical vertex. This provides a toric geometric proof of a deep correspondence between curve counting theories.

  • Confusing the BCOV holomorphic anomaly recursion with the topological recursion on the mirror curve: the former works in flat coordinates on the Kähler moduli space, the latter works on the spectral curve; they are equivalent only after the mirror map.
  • Omitting the age‑shift factors in the stacky I‑function: the box product must start at m=0, not m=1, otherwise the twisted sector contributions are lost.
  • Treating the tropical moduli space M_gtrop as a fan rather than a cone complex: the cones are not necessarily simplicial and the face relations include edge contractions that are not linear projections.
  • Assuming the tropical vertex scattering diagram is always consistent without checking the quantum product associativity: consistency is equivalent to the WDVV equations for the target toric variety.
  • Neglecting the rational contact orders in logarithmic orbifold invariants: the contact order of a map to a stacky divisor is a rational number whose denominator equals the order of the generic stabilizer.
  • Mixing the Berkovich skeleton of a toric variety with the tropicalization of a subvariety: the skeleton is a subspace of the analytification of the whole variety, while the tropicalization of a subvariety lives in the tropical torus of the ambient fan.
