  • Applying the degeneration formula for logarithmic invariants without expanding the target: the formula requires the universal expanded degeneration, which introduces additional toric blow‑ups not present in the original pair.

These advanced topics illustrate how toric geometry provides a unifying language for mirror symmetry, tropical geometry, non‑archimedean analysis, and logarithmic enumerative invariants.

Toric Varieties and the Mirror Symmetry for Toric Calabi–Yau Fourfolds

The extension of Batyrev’s mirror construction from threefolds to fourfolds replaces the reflexive polytope in a four‑dimensional lattice by a pair of dual reflexive polytopes Δ and Δ* in ℤ⁴. The anticanonical hypersurface in the toric variety X_Δ is a Calabi–Yau threefold; for fourfolds one considers a complete intersection of two anticanonical divisors, or a single anticanonical hypersurface in a toric Fano fivefold. The mirror family is constructed from the dual polytope Δ* by taking the Newton polytope of a Laurent polynomial whose coefficients are the complex structure parameters. The periods of the holomorphic four‑form on the mirror satisfy a GKZ hypergeometric system whose rank equals the dimension of the middle cohomology of the original fourfold. The mirror map is obtained by identifying the flat coordinates on the Kähler moduli space with the logarithmic solutions of the GKZ system near the large complex structure limit. The genus‑zero Gromov–Witten invariants of the fourfold are then extracted from the prepotential, which is a homogeneous function of degree three in the flat coordinates. Higher‑genus invariants are governed by the BCOV holomorphic anomaly equations, which in the toric setting reduce to a recursive computation on the scattering diagram associated to the fan of X_Δ. A key result of Klemm, Pandharipande, and others is that the topological string partition function on a toric Calabi–Yau fourfold can be expressed as a sum over tropical curves in the fan, each weighted by a product of vertex invariants that are themselves given by the topological vertex of the local Calabi–Yau threefold obtained by restricting to a toric divisor. This tropical vertex formalism provides a completely combinatorial algorithm for all‑genus mirror symmetry on toric Calabi–Yau fourfolds.

The construction relies on the existence of a crepant resolution of the singular toric variety defined by the cone over Δ. The resolution corresponds to a maximal projective crepant subdivision (MPCS) of the fan, which exists for all reflexive polytopes in dimension four by a theorem of Batyrev and Kreuzer. The MPCS is not unique; different triangulations of Δ give rise to different crepant resolutions related by flops. The quantum cohomology rings of these resolutions are isomorphic under the flop transformation, a fact that follows from the crepant transformation conjecture proved by Iritani for toric varieties. The mirror family is independent of the chosen MPCS, reflecting the fact that the complex structure moduli space of the mirror is the same for all crepant resolutions. The Gromov–Witten invariants of the fourfold satisfy the divisor equation, the string equation, and the topological recursion relations, which together with the mirror map determine the full genus‑zero potential. The higher‑genus potentials are constrained by the holomorphic anomaly equation, whose boundary conditions at the conifold locus and at the large volume limit fix the integration constants. In practice, one computes the genus‑zero invariants by solving the Picard–Fuchs equations for the periods, then uses the mirror map to express the Yukawa couplings in flat coordinates, and finally integrates the anomaly equations genus by genus. The resulting invariants have been checked against localization computations on the toric fourfold for numerous examples, including the quintic fourfold in ℙ⁵ and the complete intersection of two quadrics in ℙ⁶.

An important subtlety is the appearance of non‑compact toric divisors in the fourfold, which contribute to the open‑string sector of the topological string. The open‑string invariants are captured by the disk potential, which satisfies an open‑string mirror symmetry statement: the disk potential equals the superpotential of the mirror Landau–Ginzburg model. For toric Calabi–Yau fourfolds, the superpotential is a sum of monomials corresponding to the toric divisors, and its critical points correspond to the vacua of the associated supersymmetric gauge theory. The disk potential can be computed by the topological vertex with a single external leg, and its expansion in Kähler parameters yields the open Gromov–Witten invariants. These invariants are related to the closed invariants by the open‑closed duality, which in the toric setting is a consequence of the gluing rules for the tropical vertex. The full topological string partition function, including both closed and open sectors, is conjectured to be the wave function of a quantum curve obtained by quantizing the mirror curve. This conjecture, known as the remodeling conjecture, has been proved for toric Calabi–Yau threefolds and is expected to hold for fourfolds as well.

Toric Varieties and the Toric Degenerations of Moduli Spaces of Sheaves

Let X be a smooth projective toric variety defined by a fan Σ in N_ℝ. The moduli space M_X(v) of Gieseker‑stable sheaves on X with fixed Chern character v admits a natural toric degeneration when X is a toric surface or a toric threefold with a suitable polarization. The degeneration is constructed by choosing a generic one‑parameter subgroup λ: ℂ* → T of the acting torus and considering the induced ℂ*‑action on M_X(v). The fixed locus of this action consists of sheaves that are equivariant with respect to λ, which are classified by combinatorial data: a collection of Young diagrams attached to the rays of Σ, satisfying certain compatibility conditions along the two‑dimensional cones. The Białynicki‑Birula decomposition of M_X(v) with respect to λ yields a cell decomposition whose cells are affine spaces; the closure of the union of the attracting cells is a toric variety X_Σ' whose fan Σ' is the normal fan of a polytope P_v associated to the Chern character v. This polytope is the Newton–Okounkov body of the line bundle det(Rπ_*E) on the moduli space, where π: X × M_X(v) → M_X(v) is the projection and E is the universal sheaf. The toric variety X_Σ' is a flat degeneration of M_X(v) over 𝔸¹, and its toric boundary divisors correspond to the walls in the space of stability conditions where the moduli space undergoes a flip.

The fan Σ' can be described explicitly in terms of the secondary fan of the point configuration given by the rays of Σ. Each maximal cone of the secondary fan corresponds to a regular triangulation of the polytope P_v, and the associated toric variety is a projective toric variety whose fan is the normal fan of that triangulation. The wall‑crossing of stability conditions on X translates into the wall‑crossing of the secondary fan: crossing a wall changes the triangulation by a bistellar flip, which induces a toric flip on X_Σ'. This provides a geometric realization of the wall‑crossing formula for the Donaldson–Thomas invariants of X, which in the toric case are computed by the topological vertex. The DT invariants of X are the Euler characteristics of the moduli spaces M_X(v), and the degeneration to X_Σ' allows one to compute them by localization on the toric variety X_Σ'. The fixed points of the torus action on X_Σ' correspond to the triangulations of P_v, and the contribution of each fixed point is a product of vertex factors given by the MacMahon function. Summing over all triangulations yields the DT partition function, which coincides with the topological string partition function on the local Calabi–Yau threefold Tot(K_X). This equality is a manifestation of the DT/PT correspondence for toric varieties.

The construction extends to moduli spaces of stable pairs (F, s) where F is a pure one‑dimensional sheaf and s: 𝒪_X → F is a section with zero‑dimensional cokernel. The moduli space of stable pairs P_n(X, β) also admits a toric degeneration whose fan is the normal fan of a polytope Pn,β built from the curve class β and the integer n. The degeneration is compatible with the virtual fundamental class, so the stable pair invariants can be computed by the same localization formula. The resulting invariants satisfy the wall‑crossing formula of Joyce and Song, which in the toric case reduces to the Kontsevich–Soibelman wall‑crossing formula for the tropical vertex. The tropical vertex scattering diagram associated to the fan Σ encodes the wall‑crossing of both DT and stable pair invariants, and its consistency is equivalent to the associativity of the quantum product on the cohomology of the moduli space. This provides a unified framework for curve counting on toric varieties, linking Gromov–Witten theory, Donaldson–Thomas theory, and stable pair theory through the combinatorics of the fan.

Identifying the tropical fan of a moduli space degeneration
Given a toric surface X defined by the fan Σ with rays (1,0), (0,1), (-1,-1) (i.e., ℙ²) and a Chern character v = (r, c₁, ch₂) = (2, (1,1), 0), describe the polytope P_v whose normal fan gives the toric degeneration of the moduli space M_X(v) of stable sheaves. Explain how the rays of the normal fan correspond to the walls in the space of stability conditions.
The polytope P_v is the Newton–Okounkov body of the determinant line bundle on M_X(v). For ℙ² with v = (2, (1,1), 0), the moduli space M_X(v) is a smooth projective variety of dimension 4. The torus action of (ℂ*)² on ℙ² induces an action on M_X(v). The fixed points correspond to equivariant sheaves classified by pairs of Young diagrams (λ₁, λ₂) with total boxes equal to ch₂ = 0, so both diagrams are empty. The Białynicki‑Birula cells are affine spaces indexed by the possible weights of the torus action on the Ext groups. The attracting cells for a generic one‑parameter subgroup λ(t) = (t^a, t^b) with a,b > 0 are those where the weights are positive. The closure of the union of attracting cells is a toric variety whose fan is the normal fan of the polytope P_v = Conv{(0,0), (a,0), (0,b), (a,b)} in ℝ². The rays of this normal fan are the outward normals to the edges of P_v: (-1,0), (0,-1), (1,1). These rays correspond to the three walls in the space of stability conditions where the moduli space undergoes a flip: the walls are given by the equations μ(λ) = μ(v) for the three possible destabilizing subobjects (line bundles 𝒪, 𝒪(1), 𝒪(2)). The wall‑crossing changes the triangulation of P_v by a bistellar flip, which is exactly the toric flip relating the two birational models of M_X(v).
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for the fan of a toric degeneration of a moduli space, first write down the polytope P_v from the Chern character and the chosen one‑parameter subgroup. The normal fan of P_v is the answer; its rays are the primitive inward normals to the facets of P_v. Remember that each ray corresponds to a wall in the stability manifold, and the wall‑crossing is a bistellar flip of the triangulation of P_v. Practice computing P_v for ℙ², ℙ¹×ℙ¹, and Hirzebruch surfaces, as these appear frequently.

Toric Varieties and the Tropical Geometry of Toric Varieties over Valued Fields

Let K be a field equipped with a non‑trivial valuation val: K* → ℝ, and let X_Σ be a toric variety over K defined by a fan Σ in N_ℝ. The analytification X_Σ^an in the sense of Berkovich contains a canonical skeleton Sk(X_Σ) which is a piecewise‑linear space homeomorphic to the tropicalization of the torus T_N = N ⊗ ℤ K*. The skeleton is a union of cones indexed by the cones of Σ, glued along their faces according to the incidence relations of the fan. Each cone σ ∈ Σ corresponds to a stratum T_σ ⊂ X_Σ, and the skeleton restricted to the closure of T_σ is the cone σ_ℝ = σ ⊗ ℤ ℝ. The tropicalization map trop: X_Σ^an → Σ_ℝ sends a point to its valuation vector on the character lattice M. For a subvariety Y ⊂ X_Σ defined by a Laurent polynomial f = Σu∈M c_u χ^u, the tropicalization trop(Y) is the corner locus of the piecewise‑linear function trop(f)(x) = minu (val(c_u) + ⟨u, x⟩). This is a polyhedral complex of pure dimension dim Y, balanced with respect to the fan Σ. The fundamental theorem of tropical geometry holds in this setting: trop(Y) is the support of the tropical cycle associated to Y, and its recession fan is the intersection of the recession fan of Y with Σ.

The tropical geometry over valued fields provides a bridge between non‑archimedean analytic geometry and combinatorial tropical geometry. The Berkovich skeleton Sk(X_Σ) carries a natural integral affine structure, and the tropicalization of a subvariety Y is a tropical cycle in this affine manifold. The intersection theory of tropical cycles on Sk(X_Σ) mirrors the intersection theory of the corresponding subvarieties on X_Σ. In particular, the tropical intersection product of trop(Y₁) and trop(Y₂) computes the tropicalization of the scheme‑theoretic intersection Y₁ ∩ Y₂, provided the intersection is transverse in the tropical sense. This tropical intersection theory is the basis for the tropical proof of the Bernstein–Kushnirenko theorem: the number of solutions in (K*)^n of a system of n Laurent polynomials with given Newton polytopes equals the mixed volume of the polytopes, provided the coefficients are sufficiently generic. The proof uses the fact that the tropicalization of the solution set is a zero‑dimensional tropical cycle whose degree is the mixed volume.

When the valuation is discrete (e.g., K = ℂ((t))), the skeleton Sk(X_Σ) is a rational polyhedral complex, and the tropicalization of a subvariety can be computed by the initial degeneration technique: for each weight vector w ∈ ℝ^n, the initial ideal in_w(I) defines a toric degeneration of Y over the valuation ring. The tropical variety trop(Y) is the set of weight vectors w for which in_w(I) does not contain a monomial. This is the classical definition of the tropical variety via Gröbner fans. The connection with the Berkovich skeleton is that the tropical variety is the image of the analytification Y^an under the tropicalization map, and the fibers of this map are the analytifications of the initial degenerations. The tropical compactification of Y in X_Σ is the closure of Y in a toric variety X_Σ' obtained by subdividing Σ so that trop(Y) becomes a union of cones of the new fan. This compactification is a proper map with toric boundary, and its generic fiber is Y. The tropical compactification is functorial with respect to toric morphisms and provides a geometric realization of the tropical moduli space of curves when Y is a curve in a toric surface.

Toric Varieties and the Quantum Cohomology of Toric Complete Intersections via the Mirror Theorem

Let X_Σ be a smooth projective toric variety defined by a fan Σ, and let E = ⊕i=1^k L_i be a direct sum of nef line bundles on X_Σ such that the total space of E is a Calabi–Yau variety (i.e., c₁(X_Σ) = Σ c₁(L_i)). The zero locus of a generic section of E is a smooth Calabi–Yau complete intersection Y ⊂ X_Σ. The Batyrev–Borisov mirror construction associates to Y a mirror family Y* defined by a Laurent polynomial in the dual torus whose Newton polytope is the Minkowski sum of the polytopes of the L_i. The mirror theorem for toric complete intersections, proved by Coates, Corti, Iritani, and Tseng, states that the genus‑zero Gromov–Witten invariants of Y are encoded in the I‑function I_Y(q, z) = Σd∈Eff(X_Σ) q^d ∏i=1^k ∏m=1⟨c₁(L_i), d⟩ (c₁(L_i) + m z) / ∏ρ∈Σ(1) ∏m=1⟨D_ρ, d⟩ (D_ρ + m z), where the sum runs over effective curve classes d, D_ρ are the toric divisors, and z is the formal variable. The I‑function satisfies the quantum differential equation of Y, and its asymptotic expansion near the large radius limit gives the mirror map and the Yukawa couplings.

The proof uses the toric mirror theorem for X_Σ (Givental’s theorem) together with the quantum Lefschetz hyperplane principle. The quantum Lefschetz theorem for a convex vector bundle E states that the genus‑zero Gromov–Witten invariants of the zero locus Y are obtained from those of X_Σ by multiplying the I‑function of X_Σ by the Euler class of the obstruction bundle R1π_*f*E on the moduli space of stable maps. In the toric case, the obstruction bundle splits as a sum of line bundles whose Chern roots are the linear factors appearing in the numerator of I_Y. The mirror map is the ratio of the coefficient of z⁰ to the coefficient of z¹ in the I‑function after applying the mirror transformation. The Yukawa couplings are the third derivatives of the genus‑zero potential, which can be computed from the I‑function by the formula ⟨τ₀(γ₁), τ₀(γ₂), τ₀(γ₃)⟩₀ = Resz=0 (γ₁, γ₂, γ₃) I_Y / z³. The higher‑genus invariants of Y are conjecturally governed by the BCOV holomorphic anomaly equations on the mirror family Y*, and the genus‑one invariant is given by the Ray–Singer analytic torsion of the mirror. The Gromov–Witten/Donaldson–Thomas correspondence for toric complete intersections relates the GW invariants of Y to the DT invariants of the local Calabi–Yau threefold Tot(K_Y), which can be computed by the topological vertex on the toric diagram of Y.

An important application is the computation of the quantum cohomology ring QH*(Y). The ring structure is determined by the three‑point genus‑zero invariants, which are extracted from the I‑function. The quantum product is associative, and the associativity relations (WDVV equations) are equivalent to the consistency of the scattering diagram of the tropical vertex associated to the fan of X_Σ. The quantum cohomology ring of Y is a Frobenius manifold, and its flat coordinates are given by the mirror map. The Euler vector field acts by grading, and the unit vector field is the identity class. The quantum cohomology of Y is semisimple at the large radius limit, and its Stokes matrix is related to the monodromy of the quantum differential equation. The mirror theorem also implies the Crepant Transformation Conjecture for toric complete intersections: if Y and Y' are related by a flop in X_Σ, their quantum cohomology rings are isomorphic after analytic continuation in the Kähler parameters. This isomorphism is induced by the Fourier–Mukai transform on the derived categories of coherent sheaves, which in the toric case is realized by the mutation of the exceptional collection of line bundles on X_Σ.

  • Confusing the I‑function of a complete intersection with the I‑function of the ambient toric variety: the numerator must include the product over the line bundles defining the intersection.
  • Neglecting the convexity condition on the vector bundle E when applying the quantum Lefschetz theorem; without convexity the obstruction bundle does not split and the formula fails.
  • Treating the tropicalization of a subvariety in a toric variety over a valued field as a fan rather than a cone complex; the recession fan may be non‑simplicial.
  • Assuming the Berkovich skeleton of a toric variety coincides with the tropicalization of every subvariety; the skeleton is a subspace of the analytification of the whole variety.
  • Omitting the age‑shift factors in the orbifold I‑function when the toric variety has stacky structure; the box product must start at m = 0.
  • Mixing the wall‑crossing of stability conditions on the moduli space of sheaves with the wall‑crossing of the secondary fan; they are related but not identical.
  • These traps highlight the subtlety of toric geometry and the need for precise combinatorial bookkeeping.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of p‑adic Mirror Symmetry and the p‑adic Gamma Function

The extension of mirror symmetry to the p‑adic setting replaces the complex torus (C*)^n with the rigid analytic torus (K*)^n over a p‑adic field K. The mirror family is no longer a Laurent polynomial but a p‑adic analytic function whose Newton polytope is the same reflexive polytope Δ. The p‑adic mirror map is constructed using the p‑adic Gamma function Γ_p and the Dwork exponential. For a toric Calabi–Yau hypersurface X_Δ defined by a section of the anticanonical bundle, the p‑adic period integrals are expressed as hypergeometric series with coefficients in the ring of p‑adic integers Z_p. The p‑adic Gamma function satisfies the Gross–Koblitz formula Γ_p(x) = −πx−1 g(ω−x) where g is the Gauss sum and ω the Teichmüller character. This identifies the p‑adic periods with special values of the p‑adic hypergeometric function _nFn−1 evaluated at the mirror parameter ψ. The p‑adic mirror theorem, proved by Dwork, Adolphson–Sperber, and later by Goto–Kato–Saito for toric complete intersections, states that the unit root of the zeta function of X_Δ over F_q coincides with the p‑adic analytic continuation of the hypergeometric series. The p‑adic monodromy of the Picard–Fuchs equation is governed by the p‑adic local system associated to the Dwork family, and its Frobenius structure is computed via the p‑adic Gamma function. The p‑adic B‑model is described by the variation of p‑adic Hodge structure on the rigid analytic mirror family, and the p‑adic Yukawa couplings are the third derivatives of the p‑adic prepotential. The p‑adic Gamma function also appears in the p‑adic Gross–Koblitz formula for the p‑adic periods of Fermat hypersurfaces, which are toric. The p‑adic mirror symmetry for toric varieties provides a bridge between the arithmetic of zeta functions and the enumerative geometry of curves, and it is a key ingredient in the p‑adic Langlands program for GL2(Q_p).

Toric Varieties and the Theory of Mixed Hodge Modules and the Decomposition Theorem for Toric Morphisms

Saito's theory of mixed Hodge modules provides a functorial framework for the Hodge theory of singular spaces. For a toric variety X_Σ, the constant sheaf QX_Σ[dim X_Σ] underlies a pure Hodge module of weight dim X_Σ. The decomposition theorem for a proper toric morphism f: X_Σ → X_Σ' states that the direct image f_* QX_Σ[dim X_Σ] splits in the derived category of mixed Hodge modules as a direct sum of intersection cohomology complexes ICZ_i(L_i) shifted by integers, where Z_i are torus‑invariant subvarieties and L_i are local systems on the smooth locus of Z_i. In the toric case, all local systems are trivial because the torus action forces monodromy to be trivial on invariant strata. The summands correspond to the strata of the fan Σ, and the multiplicities are given by the h‑vectors of the associated polytopes. The hard Lefschetz theorem for toric varieties is a consequence of the relative hard Lefschetz for the morphism f, and the Hodge–Riemann bilinear relations translate into the positivity of the intersection form on the primitive parts of the cohomology of the fibers. The weight filtration on the cohomology of a toric variety is combinatorial: the graded pieces Gr^W_k H^*(X_Σ) are isomorphic to the cohomology of the toric boundary strata. The mixed Hodge structure on the cohomology of a toric variety is always split over Q, and the Hodge numbers hp,q vanish unless p = q. This implies that the Hodge diamond is concentrated on the diagonal, and the Hodge numbers are the Betti numbers. The decomposition theorem for the resolution of singularities π: X_Σ̃ → X_Σ of a toric variety expresses the intersection cohomology of X_Σ as a direct summand of the cohomology of the resolution, and the difference is supported on the exceptional divisors. The Saito vanishing theorem for toric morphisms implies that the higher direct images of the structure sheaf vanish, which is the algebraic counterpart of the Grauert–Riemenschneider vanishing. The theory of mixed Hodge modules on toric varieties also underlies the proof of the combinatorial hard Lefschetz theorem for polytopes by Karu and the proof of the g‑theorem for rational polytopes by Adiprasito–Huh–Katz.

Worked Example: Decomposition Theorem for a Toric Blow‑up
Let X be the toric surface defined by the fan with rays (1,0), (0,1), (−1,−1) (the singular projective plane P(1,1,1) with an A2 singularity). Let π: X̃ → X be the toric resolution obtained by subdividing the cone with the ray (−1,−1) replaced by (−1,0) and (0,−1). Compute the decomposition of π_* QX̃[2] in the derived category of mixed Hodge modules on X.
The resolution X̃ is smooth (it is the blow‑up of P2 at a point). The exceptional divisor E ≅ P1 has self‑intersection −1. The direct image π_* QX̃[2] splits as IC_X(Q) ⊕ IC_E(Q)[−1] ⊕ IC_p(Q)[−2] where p is the singular point. The summand IC_X(Q) is the intersection cohomology complex of X, which coincides with the constant sheaf on the smooth locus and has stalk cohomology H0 = Q, H2 = Q at p. The summand IC_E(Q)[−1] corresponds to the exceptional curve; its stalk at a generic point of E is Q in degree 1. The summand IC_p(Q)[−2] is the skyscraper at p shifted by −2. The Hodge numbers are h0,0=1, h1,1=2 (one from the proper transform of a line, one from E), h2,2=1. The decomposition theorem holds with trivial local systems because all strata are simply connected.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the decomposition theorem on a toric variety, first identify the torus‑invariant strata of the target. The summands are IC complexes of the closures of these strata with shifts determined by the relative dimension of the fibers. The local systems are always trivial in the toric setting. Use the combinatorial description of the fan to read off the strata and their dimensions. The h‑vector of the polytope gives the multiplicities of the summands.

Toric Varieties and the Refined Topological Vertex, the AGT Correspondence, and the Omega Background

The refined topological vertex computes the refined open topological string amplitudes on toric Calabi–Yau threefolds. The refinement introduces a second parameter β (or ε1, ε2 with ε1 + ε2 = 0 in the unrefined limit) that deforms the topological string partition function. For a toric CY3 X, the refined partition function Z_X(q, t) is a sum over plane partitions (3D Young diagrams) weighted by q and t, where q = eiε1, t = e−iε2. The refined vertex Vλμν(q, t) is expressed in terms of the Macdonald polynomials P_λ(x; q, t) and the skew Cauchy identity. The AGT correspondence relates the refined topological string on a toric CY3 that engineers a 4d N=2 gauge theory to the conformal blocks of the Virasoro (or W‑algebra) on a Riemann surface. The toric diagram of X encodes the quiver gauge theory: each compact 4‑cycle corresponds to a gauge node, each non‑compact 4‑cycle to a flavor node, and the intersections give bifundamental matter. The Omega background with parameters ε1, ε2 localizes the path integral of the 4d theory to fixed points classified by Young diagrams, reproducing the refined topological vertex. The Nekrasov partition function ZNek(a, ε1, ε2) of the gauge theory equals the refined topological string partition function Ztop(q, t) after identifying the Coulomb parameters a with the Kähler parameters of X. The refined BPS invariants (or refined Donaldson–Thomas invariants) are the coefficients of the refined partition function expanded in the basis of characters of the quantum torus algebra. The wall‑crossing formula for refined BPS invariants is governed by the quantum dilogarithm and the Kontsevich–Soibelman wall‑crossing in the refined setting. The refined topological vertex also computes the equivariant K‑theoretic Donaldson–Thomas invariants of the Hilbert scheme of points on C3, which are the same as the refined BPS invariants of the local CY3. The AGT correspondence for toric CY3s is a concrete realization of the geometric Langlands correspondence for the quantum group U_q(sl2) at a root of unity.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of Elliptic Stable Envelopes and the Quantum K‑Theory of Nakajima Quiver Varieties

Elliptic stable envelopes, introduced by Okounkov and Aganagic, are a generalization of the stable envelopes in equivariant cohomology to elliptic cohomology. For a toric variety X_Σ with a torus action T, the elliptic stable envelope Stab_Ell: K_T(X_Σ) → K_T(X_Σ) ⊗ Ell_T(pt) is a map between the equivariant K‑theory of X_Σ and its elliptic cohomology, which is a sheaf over the elliptic curve E = C*/q^Z. The construction uses the abelianization of the torus action and the geometry of the fixed points and attracting sets. The elliptic stable envelope satisfies a set of axioms: support, normalization, and a degree condition involving the elliptic weight function. The quantum K‑theory of X_Σ is the deformation of the K‑theory ring by the Kähler parameters, where the product is defined by the genus‑zero, degree‑d, three‑point K‑theoretic Gromov–Witten invariants. The quantum K‑theory ring is isomorphic to the ring of elliptic stable envelopes via the elliptic mirror map. For Nakajima quiver varieties, which include toric varieties as special cases (the Hilbert scheme of points on C2, the resolved conifold), the elliptic stable envelopes are computed by the elliptic vertex, which is a refinement of the topological vertex to elliptic cohomology. The elliptic vertex is expressed in terms of the elliptic Gamma function Γ(z; p, q) and the theta functions. The quantum K‑theory of the Hilbert scheme of points on C2 is generated by the Nakajima operators, and the elliptic stable envelopes provide a basis that diagonalizes the quantum multiplication. The elliptic R‑matrix, which intertwines the elliptic stable envelopes for different chambers, satisfies the elliptic dynamical Yang–Baxter equation. The elliptic stable envelopes also compute the elliptic genus of the 2d N=(2,2) gauged linear sigma model with target X_Σ, and they are the building blocks of the elliptic topological string. The wall‑crossing of elliptic stable envelopes under variation of the stability chamber is governed by the elliptic quantum Weyl group.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of the Coulomb Branch of 3d N=4 Gauge Theories and Symplectic Duality

The Coulomb branch of a 3d N=4 gauge theory with gauge group G and matter representation M is a hyperkähler variety that admits a toric description when G is abelian. For a quiver gauge theory with unitary gauge groups U(1)^r and bifundamental matter, the Coulomb branch M_C is a toric hyperkähler variety constructed as a hyperkähler quotient of a flat hyperkähler space by the gauge group. The moment map equations are the real and complex moment maps for the torus action. The Coulomb branch is isomorphic to the spectrum of the quantized Coulomb branch algebra, which is a deformation of the coordinate ring of M_C by the parameter ℏ. The coordinate ring is generated by monopole operators V_±(λ) labeled by cocharacters λ of the gauge group, subject to relations that are the quantum corrections to the classical relations. The toric fan of the Coulomb branch is the fan of the GIT quotient of the matter representation by the gauge group, which is the Higgs branch M_H. Symplectic duality (or 3d mirror symmetry) exchanges the Coulomb branch of one theory with the Higgs branch of the mirror theory. For toric hyperkähler varieties, the mirror is another toric hyperkähler variety whose fan is the Gale dual of the original fan. The Hilbert series of the Coulomb branch is computed by the Molien–Weyl integral over the gauge group, and it equals the Hilbert series of the Higgs branch of the mirror theory. The quantum Coulomb branch algebra is a non‑commutative deformation whose representation category is equivalent to the category of modules over the quantized Higgs branch algebra of the mirror. The toric geometry of the Coulomb branch is encoded in the fan of the moment polytope, which is the convex hull of the weights of the matter representation. The resolution of singularities of the Coulomb branch corresponds to turning on real mass parameters (Fayet–Iliopoulos terms) in the gauge theory. The intersection cohomology of the Coulomb branch is isomorphic to the cohomology of the Higgs branch of the mirror, and the Betti numbers are given by the h‑vector of the moment polytope. The Coulomb branch of the 3d theory with gauge group U(1)^r and n hypermultiplets is the toric hyperkähler variety T^* (C^n // U(1)^r), and its mirror is the quiver variety of type An−1.

  • Confusing the p‑adic Gamma function Γ_p with the complex Gamma function; the Gross–Koblitz formula relates Γ_p to Gauss sums, not to the complex Gamma function.
  • Assuming the decomposition theorem for toric morphisms holds with non‑trivial local systems; torus actions force all local systems on invariant strata to be trivial.
  • Treating the refined topological vertex as a simple q‑deformation of the unrefined vertex; the refinement requires two parameters (q, t) and Macdonald polynomials, not just Schur functions.
  • Identifying the elliptic stable envelope with the ordinary stable envelope in equivariant cohomology; the elliptic version takes values in elliptic cohomology, a sheaf over an elliptic curve, not in a vector space.
  • Equating the Coulomb branch of a 3d N=4 theory with the Higgs branch of the same theory; symplectic duality exchanges Coulomb and Higgs branches of mirror theories, not of the same theory.
  • Neglecting the quantum corrections to the monopole operator relations in the Coulomb branch algebra; the classical relations are deformed by ℏ and the quantum relations involve the quantum torus algebra.
  • Assuming the p‑adic mirror map is the reduction mod p of the complex mirror map; the p‑adic mirror map is constructed via Dwork's p‑adic analytic continuation and the p‑adic Gamma function, not by reduction.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of Root System Fans

Root system fans provide a canonical class of complete simplicial fans whose associated toric varieties are smooth and projective. For a reduced irreducible root system Φ in a Euclidean space V, the Weyl chambers form the maximal cones of the Coxeter fan Σ(Φ). The rays of Σ(Φ) correspond to the positive roots, and the primitive generators are the corresponding coroots. The toric variety X(Φ) is the wonderful compactification of the adjoint quotient of the associated semisimple group G, and its divisor class group is naturally identified with the weight lattice modulo the root lattice. The anticanonical divisor is the sum of all torus‑invariant prime divisors, and its ampleness is equivalent to Φ being of finite type. The cohomology ring H*(X(Φ), ℤ) is generated by the divisor classes with relations given by the Stanley–Reisner ideal of the fan together with the linear relations ∑⟨λ, αᵛ⟩ D_α = 0 for λ in the weight lattice. The Betti numbers are the h‑vector of the Coxeter complex, which coincides with the coefficients of the Poincaré polynomial of the flag variety G/B. The moment polytope of X(Φ) with respect to the maximal torus action is the convex hull of the Weyl group orbit of a regular dominant weight; its lattice points are in bijection with the weight multiplicities of the corresponding irreducible representation. This construction underlies the geometric Satake correspondence for the Langlands dual group and provides a toric model for the affine Grassmannian in the minuscule case.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of the Toric Hilbert Scheme of Points on Toric Surfaces

The toric Hilbert scheme Hilbⁿ(X) parametrizes torus‑invariant zero‑dimensional subschemes of length n on a smooth toric surface X. When X = ℂ², the torus‑fixed points are indexed by partitions of n, and the attracting cells give a cellular decomposition whose dimensions are the hook lengths of the corresponding Young diagrams. For a general smooth toric surface defined by a fan Σ, the fixed points correspond to collections of partitions assigned to each maximal cone, subject to compatibility along the rays. The tangent space at a fixed point is computed by the Ext¹ of the ideal sheaf with itself, and its torus weights are expressed in terms of the arm and leg lengths of the boxes in the associated multipartitions. The equivariant Euler characteristic of the structure sheaf of Hilbⁿ(X) is given by the Nekrasov partition function, which factorizes as a product over the fixed points of the torus action on X. The generating series of equivariant χ_y‑genera satisfies a blow‑up formula that relates Hilbⁿ of a surface to Hilbⁿ of its blow‑up at a torus‑fixed point. This formula is the geometric incarnation of the AGT correspondence for pure N=2 gauge theory. The toric Hilbert scheme also carries a natural symplectic structure induced from the holomorphic symplectic form on the cotangent bundle T*X, and its fixed point set under the ℂ*‑scaling of the fiber is precisely the set of torus‑invariant subschemes. The resulting equivariant localization computes the Donaldson–Thomas invariants of the local Calabi–Yau threefold T*X.

Worked Example: Fixed Points on Hilb³(ℂ²)
List all torus‑fixed points of Hilb³(ℂ²) and compute the weights of the tangent space at each fixed point for the standard (ℂ*)²‑action with weights (t₁, t₂).
The fixed points correspond to partitions of 3: (3), (2,1), (1,1,1). For a partition λ, the tangent weights are {t₁a(s)+1 t₂-l(s) , t₁-a(s) t₂l(s)+1 | s ∈ λ}, where a(s) and l(s) are arm and leg lengths. For λ=(3): boxes at (0,0),(1,0),(2,0). Weights: (t₁, t₂), (t₁², t₂), (t₁³, t₂) and their duals (t₁⁻¹, t₂⁻¹), (t₁⁻², t₂⁻¹), (t₁⁻³, t₂⁻¹). For λ=(2,1): boxes (0,0),(1,0),(0,1). Weights: (t₁, t₂), (t₁², t₂), (t₁, t₂²) and duals. For λ=(1,1,1): boxes (0,0),(0,1),(0,2). Weights: (t₁, t₂), (t₁, t₂²), (t₁, t₂³) and duals. These six weights at each fixed point give the equivariant tangent character.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the fixed point set of a toric Hilbert scheme, first identify the underlying fan and then translate the combinatorial data (partitions, multipartitions) into torus weights using the arm‑leg formula. Remember that the number of fixed points equals the number of ways to distribute n indistinguishable boxes among the maximal cones respecting ray compatibility; this is often a simple stars‑and‑bars count once the fan is drawn.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of the Toric Mirror Symmetry for Fano Complete Intersections

Batyrev’s mirror construction extends from hypersurfaces to complete intersections in toric Fano varieties via the notion of nef‑partitions. A nef‑partition of the anticanonical divisor −K_X = D₁ + … + D_r corresponds to a decomposition of the reflexive polytope Δ into a Minkowski sum of lattice polytopes Δ = Δ₁ + … + Δ_r such that each Δ_i is the Newton polytope of a section of O(D_i). The mirror family is then constructed from the dual nef‑partition of the polar polytope Δ°. The Picard–Fuchs system governing the periods of the mirror is the GKZ hypergeometric system associated to the combined charge matrix of the nef‑partition. The quantum cohomology ring of the complete intersection Y ⊂ X is generated by the restrictions of the toric divisor classes, and its quantum product is determined by the genus‑zero Gromov–Witten invariants of Y. The mirror theorem of Givental and Lian–Liu–Yau identifies the small J‑function of Y with a hypergeometric series built from the nef‑partition data. In particular, the I‑function I_Y(q, z) = ∑d∈Eff(Y) q^d ∏i=1^r ∏m=1⟨D_i, d⟩ (D_i + m z) / ∏ρ∈Σ(1) ∏m=1⟨D_ρ, d⟩ (D_ρ + m z) satisfies the same differential equations as the J‑function after the mirror map q ↦ Q(q). The convergence of the mirror map is guaranteed by the nef‑partition condition, and the radius of convergence is the complexified Kähler modulus where the quantum cohomology becomes semisimple. This framework also yields a closed formula for the genus‑zero Gromov–Witten invariants of Y in terms of residues of the I‑function, which can be evaluated algorithmically using the toric data of the nef‑partition.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of the Toric Degenerations of Grassmannians

Grassmannians Gr(k, n) admit flat toric degenerations to toric varieties defined by the Gelfand–Tsetlin polytope GT(k, n) or, equivalently, by the string polytopes associated to a reduced word of the longest element in the symmetric group S_n. The toric degeneration is constructed by taking a valuation on the homogeneous coordinate ring of the Plücker embedding given by a highest term order compatible with the Plücker relations. The initial ideal is the Stanley–Reisner ideal of a flag simplicial complex whose facets correspond to standard Young tableaux of shape (n−k)^k. The resulting toric variety X_GT is normal, Cohen–Macaulay, and has at worst Gorenstein singularities. Its fan is the normal fan of the Gelfand–Tsetlin polytope, which is a chain of interlacing inequalities λ(i)_j ≥ λ(i+1)_j ≥ λ(i)j+1. The torus‑fixed points of X_GT are in bijection with the same tableaux, and the attracting cells reproduce the Schubert cell decomposition of Gr(k, n). The cohomology ring of X_GT is isomorphic to the cohomology ring of Gr(k, n) as a graded vector space, but the ring structure is deformed to the Stanley–Reisner presentation. The quantum cohomology of Gr(k, n) can be recovered from the toric quantum cohomology of X_GT by a wall‑crossing in the secondary fan of the polytope, which corresponds to the variation of GIT quotient for the Plücker coordinates. This degeneration underlies the construction of the mirror Landau–Ginzburg model for Grassmannians and provides a combinatorial algorithm for computing quantum Littlewood–Richardson coefficients via the tropicalization of the Plücker relations.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of the Toric Varieties Associated to Cluster Algebras of Finite Type

Cluster algebras of finite type are classified by Dynkin diagrams, and each such cluster algebra admits a natural compactification by a toric variety constructed from the exchange matrix. The fan of this toric variety is the normal fan of the generalized associahedron, whose vertices correspond to clusters and whose edges correspond to mutations. The rays of the fan are generated by the g‑vectors of the cluster variables, and the maximal cones correspond to the clusters themselves. The toric variety X_A is smooth and projective, and its divisor class group is the lattice of tropical points of the cluster variety. The anticanonical divisor is the sum of the boundary divisors associated to the frozen variables, and its ampleness reflects the finite type condition. The cohomology ring H*(X_A) is generated by the boundary divisor classes with relations given by the exchange relations of the cluster algebra. The tropicalization of the cluster variety is the union of the cones of the fan, and the scattering diagram of the cluster algebra coincides with the wall‑crossing structure of the mirror Landau–Ginzburg model on X_A. This construction provides a geometric realization of the Fock–Goncharov duality: the mirror of X_A is the toric variety associated to the Langlands dual cluster algebra, and the tropical points of the mirror correspond to the g‑vectors of the dual cluster variables. The Euler characteristic of X_A equals the number of clusters, which is the Catalan number for type A and the corresponding generalized Catalan numbers for other types.

  • Confusing the Coxeter fan of a root system with the fan of the associated flag variety; the former is a toric fan, the latter is not toric.
  • Assuming every toric Hilbert scheme of points on a toric surface is smooth; singularities appear when the surface has non‑trivial toric singularities.
  • Treating the nef‑partition condition as optional for mirror symmetry of complete intersections; without it the mirror map fails to converge.
  • Identifying the Gelfand–Tsetlin polytope with the moment polytope of the Grassmannian; the former is a string polytope for a specific reduced word, not the moment polytope of the Plücker embedding.
  • Equating the cluster toric variety with the cluster variety itself; the former is a compactification, the latter is an open subvariety with a log Calabi–Yau structure.
  • Neglecting the role of the secondary fan in the wall‑crossing of quantum cohomology under toric degeneration; the quantum product changes when crossing walls of the secondary fan.
  • Assuming the generalized associahedron is always simple; it is simplicial but not necessarily simple for non‑simply‑laced types.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of Toric Degenerations of Moduli Spaces of Stable Maps

The moduli space M̄g,n(X,β) of stable maps from n‑pointed genus‑g curves to a projective toric variety X with curve class β carries a natural torus action induced by the torus of X. By choosing a generic one‑parameter subgroup λ: 𝔾m → TX one obtains a ℂ*‑action on the moduli space. The Białynicki‑Birula decomposition yields attracting cells that are affine spaces, and the fixed locus consists of stable maps whose image lies in the toric boundary strata of X. The limit of this ℂ*‑action as t → 0 produces a flat family whose special fibre is a toric variety whose fan is the tropicalization of the moduli space. This construction, pioneered by Alexeev and developed further by Bertram, Chen, and Coskun, identifies the special fibre with a moduli space of stable maps to a toric degeneration of X, often a union of toric varieties glued along toric divisors. The tropicalization map sends a stable map to its dual graph decorated with edge lengths given by the valuation of the pull‑back of toric coordinates. The resulting tropical moduli space is a polyhedral complex that parametrizes tropical stable maps, and its cones correspond to combinatorial types of degenerate stable maps. The fan of the toric degeneration is the normal fan of the secondary polytope of the tropical moduli space, and the GIT quotient presentation of the moduli space as a quotient of a space of maps by the automorphism group of the source curve translates into a variation of GIT for the toric degeneration. This degeneration preserves virtual fundamental classes, enabling the computation of Gromov–Witten invariants of X via localization on the toric special fibre. The key technical input is the compatibility of the perfect obstruction theory with the ℂ*‑action, which follows from the functoriality of the virtual class under torus‑equivariant morphisms.

An important consequence is the tropical mirror symmetry for Gromov–Witten invariants: the generating function of descendant invariants of X equals the generating function of tropical counts on the degeneration, up to a change of variables given by the mirror map. The mirror map itself arises from the period integrals of the Landau–Ginzburg potential on the toric degeneration, which can be expressed as a sum over tropical curves weighted by their multiplicities. This provides a completely combinatorial algorithm for computing genus‑zero Gromov–Witten invariants of any projective toric variety, bypassing the need for virtual localization on the original moduli space. The algorithm has been implemented in software such as TropicalGW and has reproduced known results for ℙn, Hirzebruch surfaces, and toric Fano threefolds.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of Toric Degenerations of Moduli Spaces of Curves

The Deligne–Mumford compactification M̄g,n of the moduli space of stable n‑pointed genus‑g curves admits a toric degeneration constructed by Hassett, Hyeon, and others using the theory of log structures and tropical geometry. The starting point is the observation that the boundary divisors of M̄g,n correspond to stable graphs, and the intersection pattern of these divisors is encoded by the dual complex of the boundary, which is a simplicial complex known as the tropical moduli space Mg,ntrop. This tropical moduli space is a rational polyhedral cone complex whose cones are indexed by stable graphs with edge lengths in ℝ≥0. By choosing a generic piecewise‑linear function on Mg,ntrop one obtains a toric variety XΣ whose fan Σ is the normal fan of the polytope defined by the function. The toric variety XΣ comes equipped with a flat family over 𝔸1 whose general fibre is M̄g,n and whose special fibre is the toric variety itself. The construction uses the fact that M̄g,n is a log smooth Deligne–Mumford stack over the standard log point, and the tropicalization of the log structure yields the cone complex Mg,ntrop. The degeneration is compatible with the forgetful maps and gluing maps between moduli spaces, making it a degeneration of the whole modular operad of stable curves.

The special fibre XΣ is a union of toric varieties corresponding to the maximal cones of Σ, glued along toric divisors corresponding to codimension‑one faces. Its cohomology ring can be computed via the Mayer–Vietoris spectral sequence, and the result matches the known presentation of H*(M̄g,n, ℚ) in terms of tautological classes and boundary divisors. In particular, the classes ψi and κd degenerate to sums of toric divisor classes on XΣ, and the relations among them (e.g., the WDVV equations) become linear relations among the toric divisor classes. This degeneration has been used to prove the Faber–Zagier relations for tautological classes on M̄g by translating them into combinatorial identities on the tropical moduli space. Moreover, the toric degeneration provides a geometric framework for the tropical Torelli map, which sends a tropical curve to its Jacobian, a principally polarized tropical abelian variety. The image of this map is a subfan of the tropical moduli space of principally polarized abelian varieties, and its closure in the toric degeneration yields a compactification of the universal Jacobian over M̄g.

Worked Example: Computing a Tropical Stable Map Count
Let X = ℙ2 with its standard toric structure. Consider genus‑zero stable maps of degree d = 2 with 3 marked points. Using the toric degeneration of M̄0,3(ℙ2,2) induced by the one‑parameter subgroup λ(t) = (t, t2) in the torus (ℂ*)2, describe the tropical curves that contribute to the Gromov–Witten invariant ⟨τ0(pt) τ0(pt) τ0(pt)⟩0,2 and compute their multiplicities.
The tropicalization of ℙ2 is the fan in ℝ2 with rays (1,0), (0,1), (−1,−1). A degree‑2 tropical stable map to this fan is a metric graph with three unbounded edges of directions (1,0), (0,1), (−1,−1) each of weight 1, and one bounded edge of weight 2 connecting a 3‑valent vertex to a 2‑valent vertex. The balancing condition forces the bounded edge to have direction (−1,−1). There are exactly two combinatorial types: (i) the bounded edge connects the vertex adjacent to the (1,0) and (0,1) legs to the vertex adjacent to the (−1,−1) leg; (ii) the bounded edge connects the vertex adjacent to the (1,0) and (−1,−1) legs to the vertex adjacent to the (0,1) leg. The multiplicity of each type is the absolute value of the determinant of the primitive direction vectors of the three edges meeting at the 3‑valent vertex. For type (i) the vectors are (1,0), (0,1), (−1,−1); determinant = 1. For type (ii) the vectors are (1,0), (−1,−1), (0,1); determinant = 1. Summing multiplicities gives 2, which equals the known Gromov–Witten invariant ⟨pt,pt,pt⟩0,2 = 2.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for a toric degeneration of a moduli space, first identify the torus action on the moduli space (usually induced from the target toric variety). Then write down the associated one‑parameter subgroup and the Białynicki‑Birula cells. The special fibre is the toric variety whose fan is the tropicalization of the moduli space. Remember that the virtual fundamental class is preserved under this degeneration, so Gromov–Witten invariants can be computed by tropical curve counts on the special fibre. In multiple‑choice questions, the correct answer often mentions “tropicalization of the moduli space” or “variation of GIT for the toric degeneration”.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of Toric Degenerations of Moduli Spaces of Vector Bundles

Let X be a smooth projective toric variety and fix a topological type (rank r, Chern classes c1, …, cr) for vector bundles on X. The moduli space MX(r, c) of Gieseker‑semistable vector bundles of that type can be constructed as a GIT quotient of a Quot scheme by the action of a general linear group. When X is toric, the Quot scheme inherits a torus action from the torus of X, and the GIT quotient can be performed in stages using the technique of variation of GIT (VGIT). By choosing a generic character of the torus one obtains a ℂ*‑action on the Quot scheme whose fixed locus consists of torus‑equivariant vector bundles, i.e., bundles that split as direct sums of line bundles on the toric divisors. The attracting cells are affine spaces, and the limit as t → 0 yields a flat degeneration of MX(r, c) to a toric variety whose fan is the tropicalization of the moduli space. This degeneration was first described by Klyachko for toric vector bundles on toric varieties and later generalized by Payne and others to arbitrary moduli of vector bundles on toric varieties.

The tropical moduli space of vector bundles is a polyhedral complex whose cones correspond to filtrations of the bundle by torus‑invariant subsheaves. The fan of the toric degeneration is the normal fan of the secondary polytope of this tropical moduli space. The cohomology ring of the special fibre is generated by the divisor classes corresponding to the walls of the tropical moduli space, and the relations are given by the tropical Plücker relations. In the case of rank‑2 bundles on ℙ2 with c1 = 0, c2 = 2, the moduli space is a smooth projective variety of dimension 8, and its toric degeneration is a union of 14 toric varieties glued along toric divisors. The Betti numbers of the degeneration match those of the original moduli space, confirming that the degeneration is a rational homology equivalence. This construction also provides a geometric interpretation of the Klyachko classification: the torus‑fixed points of the degeneration correspond bijectively to the collections of filtrations (one for each ray of the fan of X) satisfying the Klyachko compatibility conditions.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of Toric Degenerations of Moduli Spaces of Higgs Bundles

The moduli space MHiggs(X, r, d) of semistable Higgs bundles of rank r and degree d on a smooth projective toric variety X carries a natural ℂ*‑action scaling the Higgs field. This action commutes with the torus action on X, giving a (ℂ*)dim X + 1‑action on the moduli space. By choosing a generic one‑parameter subgroup that scales the Higgs field with positive weight and acts on X via a generic cocharacter, one obtains a ℂ*‑action whose fixed locus consists of Higgs bundles whose underlying vector bundle is torus‑equivariant and whose Higgs field is a section of End(E) ⊗ ΩX that is homogeneous with respect to the torus action. The attracting cells are again affine spaces, and the limit yields a toric degeneration of MHiggs. The tropicalization of this moduli space is a cone complex whose cones are indexed by tropical Higgs bundles: a tropical vector bundle (a filtration of the trivial bundle on the tropical variety Xtrop) together with a tropical Higgs field, which is a piecewise‑linear map satisfying a tropical version of the integrability condition. The fan of the toric degeneration is the normal fan of the polytope defined by the tropical Hitchin map, which sends a tropical Higgs bundle to its spectral data. This degeneration has been used by Hausel, Thaddeus, and others to prove the P = W conjecture for toric varieties: the perverse filtration on the cohomology of MHiggs induced by the Hitchin fibration coincides with the weight filtration on the cohomology of the character variety, and both are mirrored by the stratification of the toric degeneration by torus orbits.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of Toric Degenerations of Moduli Spaces of Parabolic Bundles

For a smooth projective toric variety X with a simple normal crossing divisor D = ∑ Di, the moduli space Mpar(X, D, r, α) of parabolic bundles with fixed weights α along D admits a toric degeneration constructed by Bhosle, Nagaraj, and others using the Mehta–Seshadri correspondence and the theory of toric varieties associated to filtered vector spaces. The parabolic structure is a filtration of the fibre of the bundle over each component Di with weights in (0,1). The moduli space is a GIT quotient of a space of parabolic frames by a product of general linear groups. The torus of X acts on the frames, and the parabolic weights provide additional characters for the GIT quotient. By varying the GIT stability condition with respect to these characters one obtains a family of moduli spaces whose walls correspond to changes in the parabolic weights. The tropicalization of this family is a polyhedral complex whose cones are indexed by tropical parabolic bundles: a tropical vector bundle on Xtrop together with a filtration of the restriction to each boundary divisor of Xtrop satisfying tropical weight conditions. The toric degeneration of the whole family is a toric variety over the secondary fan of the weight polytope. The special fibre over a generic weight is a toric variety whose fan is the normal fan of the tropical moduli space for that weight. This degeneration has been used to compute the intersection numbers of tautological classes on Mpar by reducing them to intersection numbers of toric divisor classes on the degeneration, which are given by volumes of polytopes. In particular, the Verlinde formula for parabolic bundles on a toric curve can be derived by counting lattice points in the corresponding weight polytope.

  • Confusing the toric degeneration of a moduli space with the tropicalization of the moduli space; the former is a flat family whose special fibre is a toric variety, the latter is a polyhedral complex that records the combinatorics of the degeneration.
  • Assuming that every toric degeneration of a moduli space preserves the virtual fundamental class; this holds only when the ℂ*‑action is compatible with the perfect obstruction theory, which must be checked case by case.
  • Neglecting the role of the secondary fan in the variation of GIT for moduli spaces of vector bundles; crossing a wall of the secondary fan changes the stability condition and the resulting toric degeneration.
  • Treating the tropical Higgs field as an ordinary section of a bundle; it must satisfy the tropical integrability condition, which is a piecewise‑linear analogue of the vanishing of the commutator of the Higgs field with itself.
  • Equating the tropical moduli space of parabolic bundles with the moduli space of tropical vector bundles; the former includes additional filtration data along boundary divisors that is not present in the latter.
  • Overlooking the fact that the toric degeneration of M̄g,n depends on the choice of a generic piecewise‑linear function on the tropical moduli space; different choices give different toric degenerations that are related by flips in the secondary fan.
  • Assuming that the Betti numbers of the toric degeneration always match those of the original moduli space; this is true for rational homology equivalences but fails when the degeneration introduces non‑rational singularities.

The advanced sections above illustrate how toric degenerations provide a unifying combinatorial framework for a wide range of moduli problems in algebraic geometry.

Toric Degenerations of the Moduli Space of Stable Pairs on Toric Calabi–Yau Threefolds

The Pandharipande–Thomas (PT) stable pair invariants count curves in a Calabi–Yau threefold by considering pairs (F, s) where F is a pure one‑dimensional sheaf with χ(F)=n and s: O_X → F is a section with zero‑dimensional cokernel. On a toric Calabi–Yau threefold X, the torus action lifts to the moduli space P_n(X, β) of stable pairs with curve class β. The fixed locus decomposes into products of moduli of stable pairs on toric surfaces and zero‑dimensional contributions, which can be organised by a three‑dimensional partition (plane partition) combinatorics. The toric degeneration of P_n(X, β) is obtained by taking the limit of the C^*‑action that scales the fibres of the toric fibration; the special fibre is a toric variety whose fan is the normal fan of the Newton polytope of the PT partition function. This degeneration was used by Maulik, Oblomkov, Okounkov, and Pandharipande (2011) to prove the PT/Donaldson–Thomas correspondence for toric Calabi–Yau threefolds: the generating series of PT invariants equals the DT generating series after a change of variables q = -eiu. The degeneration also yields a vertex formalism: the topological vertex computes the contribution of each toric chart, and gluing rules reproduce the full partition function. The toric degeneration respects the perfect obstruction theory, so the virtual fundamental class specialises to the sum of virtual classes of the fixed components, enabling localisation computations that avoid the technicalities of the full moduli space.

Refined Donaldson–Thomas Invariants and Toric Degenerations

Refined DT invariants, introduced by Nekrasov and Okounkov (2003) and later formulated mathematically by Kontsevich and Soibelman (2008), enrich the numerical DT invariants by a second parameter y (or q, t) that records the motivic weight of the virtual motive. For a toric Calabi–Yau threefold X, the refined partition function can be expressed as a product over toric fixed points of refined topological vertices. The toric degeneration of the moduli space of ideal sheaves I_n(X, β) is compatible with the C^* × C^*‑action that refines the usual torus action; the extra circle action comes from the scaling of the holomorphic three‑form. The fixed locus of this refined action is again indexed by plane partitions, but each box now carries a weight (q, t) determined by its coordinates. The refined vertex, computed by Iqbal, Kozçaz, and Vafa (2007), gives the contribution of a single toric chart. The degeneration to the toric special fibre preserves the refined virtual motive because the C^* × C^*‑action lifts to the perfect obstruction theory and the fixed components are smooth toric varieties. This allows the refined DT partition function to be computed entirely by localisation on the toric degeneration, yielding the celebrated Nekrasov–Okounkov formula for the refined MacMahon function. The refined invariants satisfy a wall‑crossing formula of Kontsevich–Soibelman type; the toric degeneration provides a geometric realisation of the wall‑crossing as a variation of GIT stability on the moduli stack of representations of the toric quiver with potential.

Worked Example: Refined Vertex Computation
Compute the refined topological vertex contribution for a single toric chart of the resolved conifold with Kähler parameter Q. Use the refined vertex Cλμν(q,t) and sum over partitions λ, μ, ν with appropriate framing factors.
The refined vertex for the resolved conifold is Zref(Q; q,t) = ∑λ,μ (-Q)|λ|+|μ| f_λ(q,t) f_μ(t,q) C∅λμ(q,t) C∅μ^t λ^t ∅(t,q), where f_λ(q,t) = (-1)|λ| q||λ^t||2/2 t-||λ||2/2 and ||λ||2 = ∑_i λ_i2. The sum can be evaluated using the Cauchy identity for Macdonald polynomials, yielding the refined MacMahon function M(q,t; Q) = ∏i,j≥1 (1 - Q qi-1/2 tj-1/2)-1. This matches the refined DT partition function of the conifold obtained by localisation on the toric degeneration.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the refined DT partition function of a toric Calabi–Yau threefold, first identify the toric diagram and the associated quiver with potential. Write down the refined vertex for each vertex of the diagram, include framing factors f_λ for each internal edge, and sum over all partitions. The result factorises into a product of quantum dilogarithms; recognise the standard infinite product forms (MacMahon, refined MacMahon) to give the final closed expression.

Categorified Donaldson–Thomas Invariants via Cohomological Hall Algebras on Toric Varieties

Categorification of DT invariants replaces the numerical invariant by the cohomology of the moduli stack of objects in the derived category of coherent sheaves, equipped with a natural algebra structure — the cohomological Hall algebra (CoHA). For a toric Calabi–Yau threefold X, the category of coherent sheaves is equivalent to the category of representations of the associated quiver with potential (Q, W). The CoHA of (Q, W) was constructed by Kontsevich and Soibelman (2011) and further developed by Davison and Meinhardt (2015). The toric degeneration of the moduli stack of representations provides a filtration whose associated graded pieces are vector spaces indexed by dimension vectors; the CoHA product corresponds to convolution on the stack of short exact sequences. The torus action on X induces a grading on the CoHA by the character lattice, and the equivariant localisation theorem computes the CoHA as a shuffle algebra on the torus‑fixed points. The resulting algebra is isomorphic to the positive half of the quantum affine algebra U_q(ĝ) where ĝ is the Kac–Moody algebra associated to the quiver. The categorified DT invariants are the graded dimensions of the CoHA components; they satisfy the Kontsevich–Soibelman wall‑crossing formula at the level of algebras. The toric degeneration is essential because it identifies the CoHA with the shuffle algebra, making the wall‑crossing automorphisms explicit as quantum dilogarithm operators acting on the Fock space of the algebra.

Quantum K‑Theory of Toric Deligne–Mumford Stacks and Toric Degenerations

Quantum K‑theory extends quantum cohomology by replacing the Novikov ring with the representation ring of the torus and counting stable maps with a K‑theoretic virtual structure sheaf. For a toric Deligne–Mumford stack 𝔛 presented as a GIT quotient [V/ G] where G is a product of C^* factors, the quantum K‑theory ring QK_T(𝔛) is generated by the K‑theoretic classes of toric line bundles. The toric degeneration of the moduli stack of stable maps 𝔛g,n(𝔛, β) is obtained by the C^*‑action that scales the fibres of the universal curve; the special fibre is a toric stack whose inertia stack is a union of toric substacks indexed by twisted sectors. The localisation formula of Givental and Lee (2003) expresses the quantum K‑theory J‑function as a sum over fixed loci, each contributing a product of equivariant K‑theoretic Euler classes of the virtual normal bundle. The toric degeneration ensures that the fixed loci are themselves toric stacks, so their K‑theory rings are known combinatorially. Iritani (2011) proved that the quantum K‑theory ring of a toric DM stack is isomorphic to the Batyrev ring defined by the fan sequence, and the mirror theorem identifies the J‑function with a hypergeometric series (the I‑function) satisfying a q‑difference equation. The toric degeneration also underlies the proof of the quantum Lefschetz hyperplane theorem in K‑theory: the restriction of the J‑function to a toric hypersurface is obtained by a simple modification of the I‑function, reflecting the degeneration of the moduli space of maps to the hypersurface.

Elliptic Genera of Toric Calabi–Yau Manifolds and the Witten Genus via Toric Degenerations

The elliptic genus of a Calabi–Yau manifold X is the index of the Dirac operator on the loop space LX, valued in modular forms. For a toric Calabi–Yau manifold, the torus action lifts to the loop space, and the equivariant elliptic genus localises to the fixed points of the torus action on X. The toric degeneration of X to a union of toric affine patches provides a combinatorial formula: the elliptic genus equals a sum over toric fixed points of a product of theta‑functions determined by the weights of the torus action on the tangent space. This formula was derived by Borisov and Libgober (2000) using the theory of elliptic genera of singular varieties and the toric resolution of singularities. The Witten genus, which is the elliptic genus of a string manifold, vanishes for toric Calabi–Yau threefolds because they admit a nowhere‑vanishing holomorphic three‑form, implying the existence of a supersymmetric ground state. However, for toric Calabi–Yau fourfolds the Witten genus can be non‑zero and is computed by the same localisation formula, now involving the elliptic gamma function. The toric degeneration also connects the elliptic genus to the Gromov–Witten theory of X via the Givental quantisation formalism: the total descendant potential of X is obtained by applying a quantised symplectic transformation to the product of equivariant elliptic genera of the toric fixed loci. This relationship underlies the proof of the mirror symmetry conjecture for elliptic genera of toric Calabi–Yau manifolds by Coates, Givental, and others (2014).

  • Confusing the toric degeneration of a moduli space with its tropicalisation; the former is a flat family with a toric special fibre, the latter is a polyhedral complex recording the combinatorics of the degeneration.
  • Assuming that every torus action on a moduli space lifts to the perfect obstruction theory; one must verify compatibility case by case, otherwise localisation formulas are invalid.
  • Neglecting the framing factors in refined vertex computations; they depend on the choice of framing of the toric diagram and change the partition function by a known factor.
  • Treating the cohomological Hall algebra as an ordinary algebra without the shuffle product; the CoHA product is defined by convolution on the stack of short exact sequences and is not commutative.
  • Overlooking the role of the inertia stack in quantum K‑theory of toric DM stacks; twisted sectors contribute additional generators to the quantum K‑theory ring.
  • Equating the elliptic genus of a toric Calabi–Yau threefold with the Witten genus; the Witten genus vanishes for threefolds but not for higher‑dimensional toric Calabi–Yau manifolds.
  • Assuming the toric degeneration of the moduli space of stable pairs preserves the virtual fundamental class without checking that the C^*‑action is compatible with the obstruction theory; this compatibility is proved only for toric Calabi–Yau threefolds with a toric crepant resolution.

These advanced topics illustrate how toric degenerations provide a unifying combinatorial framework for a wide range of enumerative and categorical invariants in toric geometry.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of Toric Degenerations of Moduli Spaces of Stable Maps

The moduli space of stable maps Mg,n(X,β) to a toric variety X carries a natural C^*-action induced by scaling the fibres of the universal curve. The associated toric degeneration produces a flat family whose special fibre is a union of toric stacks indexed by decorated graphs. Each vertex corresponds to a toric stratum of X and each edge to a node mapping to a toric divisor. The combinatorial type of the graph records the contact orders with the toric boundary. This degeneration was systematically studied by Abramovich, Chen, Gross, and Siebert (2012) and underlies the logarithmic Gromov–Witten theory of toric varieties. The virtual fundamental class on the special fibre splits as a product of virtual classes on the vertex components, each of which is a toric variety of lower dimension. Consequently, the Gromov–Witten invariants of X can be computed recursively by gluing formulas that involve only the toric geometry of the strata. The degeneration also provides a geometric interpretation of the tropicalization map: the tropical curve associated to a stable map is the dual graph of the special fibre, with edge lengths given by the log parameters. This correspondence is the foundation of the tropical approach to enumerative geometry on toric varieties.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of Toric Vector Bundles via Klyachko’s Classification

Klyachko’s classification (1989) describes equivariant vector bundles on a complete toric variety X_Σ in terms of filtrations of a fixed vector space E indexed by the rays of the fan Σ. For each ray ρ one has a decreasing filtration Eρ(i) of E by subspaces, and the compatibility condition is that for any cone σ the intersection of the filtrations for rays in σ stabilises to a subspace that depends only on σ. The rank of the bundle is dim E, and its Chern classes are expressed as piecewise polynomial functions on the fan determined by the filtration jumps. This classification reduces many questions about toric vector bundles to linear algebra. For example, a toric vector bundle is globally generated iff each filtration is trivial in non‑positive degrees, and it is ample iff the associated piecewise linear function is strictly convex. The moduli space of stable toric vector bundles with fixed Chern character can be constructed as a GIT quotient of a space of filtrations, and its geometry is governed by the secondary fan of the underlying polytope. Recent work by Payne (2008) and by Kool (2011) extends Klyachko’s description to equivariant coherent sheaves and to the derived category, providing a combinatorial model for the Fourier–Mukai transform on toric varieties.

Worked Example: Computing Chern Classes of a Toric Vector Bundle
Let X = P2 with fan Σ having rays ρ1,ρ2,ρ3. Consider the rank‑2 toric vector bundle E defined by filtrations Eρ1(i) = C2 for i≤ 0, = ⟨ e1⟩ for i=1, =0 for i≥2; Eρ2(i) = C2 for i≤0, = ⟨ e2⟩ for i=1, =0 for i≥2; Eρ3(i) = C2 for i≤0, = ⟨ e1+e2⟩ for i=1, =0 for i≥2. Compute the first Chern class c1(E) as a divisor on X.
The first Chern class of a toric vector bundle is the sum over rays of the jump indices times the corresponding toric divisor. For each ray the filtration jumps from C2 to a line at i=1, so the jump index is 1. Hence c1(E) = Dρ1+Dρ2+Dρ3. On P2 the three toric divisors are linearly equivalent to the hyperplane class H, so c1(E)=3H. This matches the fact that the determinant line bundle det E is OP2(3).
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for Chern classes of a toric vector bundle, first write down the filtration jumps for each ray. The first Chern class is the sum of the jump indices times the corresponding toric divisor. For higher Chern classes, use the piecewise polynomial description: the total Chern class is the product over rays of (1 + jump_ρ D_ρ) in the Chow ring, where the product is taken in the Stanley–Reisner ring of the fan. Memorise the compatibility condition for filtrations — it is the most common source of errors in constructing examples.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of Toric Hilbert Schemes and Quiver Varieties

The toric Hilbert scheme Hilb_Σn(X_Σ) parametrises torus‑invariant subschemes of a toric variety X_Σ with a fixed multigraded Hilbert function n. It is a fine moduli space that carries a natural torus action and can be covered by affine toric charts indexed by Σ-families of monomial ideals. The fixed points correspond to monomial ideals whose staircase diagrams are given by the Hilbert function. The tangent space at a fixed point is computed by the Ext group Ext1(I,I)0 in the multigraded category, and its dimension equals the number of corners in the staircase diagram. When X_Σ is a toric Calabi–Yau threefold, the toric Hilbert scheme is closely related to the moduli space of representations of the associated dimer quiver. The quiver variety construction of Nakajima (1994) and the work of Mozgovoy–Reineke (2010) show that the generating function of Euler characteristics of toric Hilbert schemes coincides with the Donaldson–Thomas partition function of the quiver. This equality is a manifestation of the wall‑crossing formula for the motivic Donaldson–Thomas invariants of the quiver with potential. The toric Hilbert scheme also provides a geometric realisation of the crystal melting model for topological strings on toric Calabi–Yau threefolds.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of Toric Degenerations of Flag Varieties and the Gelfand–Tsetlin Polytope

Flag varieties G/B admit toric degenerations to toric varieties whose fans are the normal fans of the Gelfand–Tsetlin polytopes. For G = GL_n the Gelfand–Tsetlin polytope GT(λ) is defined by interlacing inequalities λi+1,j ≤ λi,j ≤ λi+1,j+1 for a dominant weight λ. The toric variety XGT(λ) is a flat degeneration of the flag variety, and its coordinate ring is the associated graded ring of the Plücker algebra with respect to the Gelfand–Tsetlin valuation. This degeneration was constructed by Kogan and Miller (2000) and later generalised by Alexeev and Brion (2004) to arbitrary reductive groups using the theory of spherical varieties. The toric degeneration preserves many geometric properties: the cohomology ring of the flag variety is isomorphic to the Chow ring of the toric variety, and the Schubert classes correspond to the toric divisors. Moreover, the Gelfand–Tsetlin integrable system on the flag variety becomes the moment map of the toric variety. This connection underlies the proof of the saturation conjecture for Littlewood–Richardson coefficients by Knutson and Tao (1999) via the honeycomb model, which is a tropicalisation of the Gelfand–Tsetlin polytope.

Toric Varieties and the Theory of Toric Varieties in Arithmetic Geometry: Heights, Rational Points, and Manin’s Conjecture

For a toric variety X over a number field K equipped with an adelic metrized line bundle L, the height zeta function Z(s) = Σx ∈ X(K) HL(x)-s admits a meromorphic continuation to a half‑plane ℜ(s) > a with a pole of order ρ at s = a, where a is the Fujita invariant of (X,L) and ρ is the rank of the Picard group. This result, due to Batyrev and Tschinkel (1998), proves Manin’s conjecture for toric varieties. The leading coefficient of the pole is expressed as a product of local densities: the archimedean factor is the volume of the moment polytope, and the non‑archimedean factors are given by the p-adic volumes of the toric variety. The proof uses the orbit‑cone correspondence to parametrise rational points by lattice points in the dual cone, and then applies the Hardy–Littlewood circle method or the geometry of numbers. For Fano toric varieties, the exponent a equals 1 and the conjecture predicts asymptotic formula N(B) ∼ c B (log B)ρ-1. The constant c is the Peyre constant, which factorises as a product of the Tamagawa number of the universal torsor and the volume of the polytope. Recent work by Bourqui (2017) extends these results to Campana points and to the case of log Fano pairs (X,D) where D is the toric boundary.

  • Confusing the toric degeneration of a moduli space with its tropicalisation; the former is a flat family with a toric special fibre, the latter is a polyhedral complex recording the combinatorics of the degeneration.
  • Assuming that every torus action on a moduli space lifts to the perfect obstruction theory; one must verify compatibility case by case, otherwise localisation formulas are invalid.
  • Neglecting the framing factors in refined vertex computations; they depend on the choice of framing of the toric diagram and change the partition function by a known factor.
  • Treating the cohomological Hall algebra as an ordinary algebra without the shuffle product; the CoHA product is defined by convolution on the stack of short exact sequences and is not commutative.
  • Overlooking the role of the inertia stack in quantum K‑theory of toric DM stacks; twisted sectors contribute additional generators to the quantum K‑theory ring.
  • Equating the elliptic genus of a toric Calabi–Yau threefold with the Witten genus; the Witten genus vanishes for threefolds but not for higher‑dimensional toric Calabi–Yau manifolds.
  • Assuming the toric degeneration of the moduli space of stable pairs preserves the virtual fundamental class without checking that the C^*-action is compatible with the obstruction theory; this compatibility is proved only for toric Calabi–Yau threefolds with a toric crepant resolution.
